##### padel-rules
Padel looks like tennis in a glass box, and most of the rules feel familiar within ten minutes. The walls are the part that confuses everyone. Here is everything you need to play your first match properly, based on the official rules of the International Padel Federation (FIP). The court at a glance A padel court is a rectangle 20 metres long and 10 metres wide , completely enclosed. At each end there is a 3-metre wall, usually glass, with 1 metre of metal mesh on top. The side walls start with glass near the back and switch to mesh towards the net. The net is 88 cm high in the middle and 92 cm at the posts , slightly lower than a tennis net. On each side, a service line runs across the court 6.95 metres from the net , and a centre line splits that area into two service boxes. Quick numbers Court: 20 m × 10 m Net: 88 cm centre, 92 cm at the posts Service line: 6.95 m from the net End walls: 3 m wall + 1 m mesh = 4 m Padel is played almost always as doubles: two pairs, one on each side of the net. Everything below assumes four players. The serve: underarm, after a bounce The serve is the first big difference from tennis, and it is much easier. You do not toss the ball up and smash it. The FIP rules say the server must: Stand behind the service line , between the centre line and the side wall, without touching either line with their feet. Bounce the ball on the ground behind the service line, on their own side. Hit it at or below waist height , with at least one foot touching the ground. Send it diagonally over the net into the opposite service box. Each game starts from the right-hand side, and the server swaps sides after every point. You get two attempts , exactly like tennis. The receiver must let the serve bounce before hitting it. After it bounces in the correct box, the serve may hit the glass behind and is still good. It is a fault if it bounces outside the box, or if it touches the metal mesh before bouncing a second time. A serve that clips the net and then lands in the right box is replayed (a let ), unless it then touches the mesh before bouncing a second time, which makes it a fault. How the walls work This is the rule that makes padel padel. Get these two lines into your head and you understand 90% of the game. Allowed The ball bounces on your side, hits any wall or the mesh, and you return it before it bounces a second time. Not allowed Your shot hits the opponents' wall or mesh before it has bounced on their side. That point is lost. In practice this means three things: Let it come off the glass. When a deep ball passes you, you can wait for it to bounce, hit the back or side glass, and play it as it comes back out. This is the single most useful skill for beginners. You can use your own walls. If you are in trouble, you may hit the ball into your own back or side wall so that it rebounds over the net, as long as it then bounces in the opponents' court before touching their walls. The ball only gets one bounce. Walls don't count as bounces, the floor does. Once it bounces twice on your side, the point is over. The mesh behaves differently from the glass. The ball comes off it unpredictably, and on the serve it is an automatic fault, so aim your shots at the glass, not the fence. Volleys, the net and your body Apart from the return of serve, you may volley any ball before it bounces. Most points are won at the net. But the net itself is off limits. You lose the point if: you, your racket or anything you're wearing touches the net or the posts during the point; you hit the ball before it has crossed to your side; the ball hits you or your partner , anywhere on the body; you hit the ball into the mesh on your own side ; you drop your racket or break the wrist strap, which is compulsory under the rules. Playing outside the court You may have seen professionals sprint out through the side doors to chase a smash that bounced out of the court. That is legal only where the court has a big enough safety area around it, and the organiser allows it. At most clubs there isn't room, so a ball that leaves the court after bouncing simply wins the point for whoever hit it. Scoring in one paragraph Padel uses tennis scoring: 15, 30, 40, game. A set is first to six games with a two-game lead, with a tie-break at 6–6, and matches are usually best of three sets. At 40–40, clubs play either normal advantage or a single deciding "golden point". Since 2026 the professional tour uses a new format called the Star Point . Our full guide to padel scoring explains every format. Five rules that catch new players out Serving overhead. Waist height or below, always. Playing a ball straight off the opponents' glass. If your shot hits their wall before bouncing, it's your point lost, even if it looks spectacular. Volleying the serve. The return must bounce first. Touching the net after a winning volley. If you touch it before the ball has bounced twice, you lose the point. Thinking the wall is the end. It isn't. Turn, wait and let the glass give you the ball back. Padel vs tennis: the key differences Padel Tennis Court 20 × 10 m, enclosed by walls 23.77 m long, open Players Almost always doubles Singles or doubles Serve Underarm, after a bounce, at or below waist Overhead, usually Walls In play after the ball bounces No walls Racket Solid, no strings, max 45.5 cm Strung, longer Scoring Tennis scoring, plus golden point or Star Point options Tennis scoring The smaller court and the walls mean rallies last longer and the game is less about power. That's why players with no tennis background often pick padel up faster than they expect. Frequently asked questions Can the ball hit the wall before it bounces? Only on your own side. You may play the ball into your own wall so it rebounds over the net, but it must then bounce in the opponents' court before touching their walls or mesh. Can I hit the ball after it bounces twice? No. Two bounces on your side and the point is over. Bounces off the walls don't count; only bounces on the floor do. Can I serve overarm in padel? No. The rules require you to bounce the ball and hit it at or below waist height. Is the mesh in or out? During a rally, the ball may hit the mesh after bouncing on your side and stays in play, though it comes off unpredictably. On the serve, touching the mesh before the second bounce is a fault. Can you play padel singles? Some clubs have narrower singles courts, but the standard game, the professional tour and the FIP rules are built around doubles. 
##### padel-scoring
If you know tennis scoring, you already know 90% of padel scoring. The interesting part is what happens at 40–40, where padel has three different options, including a brand-new one on the 2026 professional tour. Points: 15, 30, 40, game A game is scored exactly as in tennis. Zero is called "love", then 15, 30, 40, game . The server's score is always called first, so "30–15" means the serving pair has won two points and the receivers one. The only question is what happens when both pairs reach 40. The FIP rulebook offers three options, and the organiser of your match or league decides which one you play. Option 1: advantage (deuce) This is the classic version and still the default in most social games. At 40–40 the score is deuce . Win the next point and you have advantage . Win the following point too and the game is yours; lose it and you are back to deuce. In theory a game can go on forever. Option 2: the Star Point (new in 2026) For the 2026 season, the FIP and Premier Padel introduced a middle ground called the Star Point , first used at the FIP Bronze Melbourne in January 2026 and on the Premier Padel tour from the Riyadh P1 in February. It works like this: 40–40: deuce , play an advantage point as normal. Back to deuce? Play a second advantage . Still level at the third deuce? The next point is the Star Point , and whoever wins it wins the game. The receiving pair chooses which player receives the Star Point, and can't switch once chosen. The idea is to give players a fair chance to win a game "properly" with advantage, while still capping how long a game can last. Which one do I play? Ask before the first serve. Social games mostly play advantage, leagues often use golden point, and events following the 2026 pro format use the Star Point. Agreeing up front saves an argument at 40–40. Option 3: the golden point The golden point removes advantage completely. At 40–40 a single point decides the game. As with the Star Point, the receiving pair chooses which of them receives it, from the left or the right box, and they can't switch once chosen. The golden point became famous in the final World Padel Tour seasons, before the professional game went back to advantage scoring and then, in 2026, to the Star Point. Many leagues and tournaments still use it because it keeps matches short and makes every 40–40 dramatic. Sets: first to six, two clear A set goes to the first pair to win six games with a two-game lead . At 5–5 you play on: 7–5 wins the set. At 6–6 you play a tie-break . A standard match is best of three sets . Pairs change ends after the first game and then after every two games, on the odd totals (1, 3, 5 and so on), so neither side keeps the better light or the calmer wind for long. The tie-break, step by step In a tie-break, points are counted simply: 1, 2, 3 and so on. The first pair to seven points with a two-point lead wins the tie-break and the set, 7–6. The player whose turn it is to serve serves the first point from the right . After that, the serve passes to the other pair, who serve two points , starting from the left. From then on, the serve alternates every two points , following the normal player rotation. Pairs change ends every six points . The super tie-break When court time is tight, many clubs and leagues replace the third set with a super tie-break , sometimes called a match tie-break: first to 10 points with a two-point lead , played at one set all. It takes a few minutes instead of the best part of an hour. The FIP rules also allow mini-sets to four games, used in some junior and social formats. Worked example Ana and Leo are serving at 40–30. They lose the point: 40–40. They're playing golden point, so the receivers choose their forehand player on the right box. Leo serves, wins the rally, and the game is theirs. Under advantage scoring that same point would only have given them advantage, and under the Star Point it would have been the first of up to three deuces. Scoring etiquette The server calls the score before each point, loud enough for all four players. Call your own side's lines and doubles honestly. On a close ball, the point usually goes to the opponents. If nobody agrees on the score, go back to the last score everyone agrees on. Serving order through a set In doubles, each player serves a whole game, and the serve rotates around all four players. If Ana and Leo play Sara and Tom, the order might be Ana, Sara, Leo, Tom, then Ana again. Each pair decides which partner serves first in its opening service game, and keeps that order for the set. Within a game, the server starts from the right-hand side and switches sides after every point. Scoring cheat sheet Situation What happens 40–40, advantage scoring Deuce. Win two points in a row to take the game 40–40, golden point One deciding point. Receivers choose who receives 40–40, Star Point Up to two advantages. At the third deuce, one deciding point 5–5 in games Play on. 7–5 wins the set 6–6 in games Tie-break to 7, two clear One set all Third set, or a super tie-break to 10 if agreed Frequently asked questions What is the golden point in padel? A single deciding point played at 40–40, with no advantage. The receiving pair chooses which player receives it. What is the Star Point in padel? A scoring option introduced by the FIP in 2026 and used on the professional tour: up to two advantages are played at deuce, and if the score reaches a third deuce, the next point decides the game. How many games are in a padel set? First to six games with a two-game lead. At 6–6 a tie-break decides the set. How many sets are in a padel match? Usually best of three. Many leagues replace the third set with a super tie-break to 10 points. Who serves first in a tie-break? The player whose turn it is to serve takes the first point from the right. After that, each pair serves two points in turn. 
##### how-to-hit-a-bandeja
The bandeja is the shot that separates beginners from real padel players. It isn't a smash. It's a controlled, sliced overhead whose only job is to keep you and your partner at the net when the opponents lob you. Here is how to hit it, step by step. What the bandeja is for Bandeja is Spanish for "tray", and the name describes the racket position: you hold it up beside your head as if carrying a tray of drinks. You play it when the opponents lob you and the ball is too high to volley but not good enough to smash . In tennis you'd let that lob bounce, or smash it hard. In padel, a hard smash usually just bounces off the back glass and gives the opponents an easy ball. Running back to let it bounce hands them the net. The bandeja solves both problems: you hit it in the air, with control, and you're back at the net before they can attack. The goal A good bandeja rarely wins the point. It wins the position . Count it a success when you're still at the net and the opponents are still at the back. Step 1: turn side-on early As soon as you see the lob, turn your shoulders and feet so your side faces the net , like a tennis serve stance. Move with small side-steps or a crossover step, never backpedalling facing the net. Turning early is 80% of a good bandeja, because everything else depends on the ball being in front of you. Step 2: racket up, other arm pointing Bring the racket up early, head high and beside your head, with the face slightly open. Point your free arm at the ball . It helps you judge the height and keeps your shoulders turned. Use a continental grip, the same grip you use for volleys. Step 3: contact in front, with slice Hit the ball in front of your body and to the side , at roughly head height or a little above. Don't hit it right above your head, and never behind you. Swing forward and slightly down, cutting across the back and underneath of the ball to put slice on it. The slice is what makes the bandeja work. A sliced ball travels with control, bounces low and dies off the back glass instead of popping out for the opponents to attack. Step 4: follow through, then move forward Finish with the racket travelling towards your target and across your body, staying relaxed. The moment the ball leaves your racket, step back towards the net with your partner. The bandeja only works if you take the position it bought you. Where to aim Deep, towards the back glass. The ball should bounce close to the glass and come off it low, so the opponents have to dig it out. Into the corners , especially to the side glass. A ball that bounces and runs into the corner is very hard to lob back well. Through the middle when the opponents are slow to decide who takes it. Avoid aiming short. A bandeja that lands in the middle of their court with no pace sits up perfectly for a counter-attack. Bandeja, víbora or smash? Shot When Spin & pace Aim Bandeja Lob pushes you back from the net Slice, controlled pace Keep the net, push them back Víbora Easier lob, you're closer to the net Side spin and slice, faster Pressure them off the side glass Smash Short, easy lob near the net Flat or topspin, full power Win the point, or force a weak reply The víbora is the bandeja's aggressive sibling. You contact the ball more on its outside, with side spin, so it skids off the side glass. Learn the bandeja first. It's the safer shot, and the víbora grows out of it. Five common mistakes Hitting it too hard. Power without slice means the ball bounces high off the back glass and you get punished. Letting the ball get behind you. Late turns force you to lean back and the shot floats. Staying flat-footed facing the net. Turn sideways every time. Watching your shot instead of moving. Recover to the net immediately. Aiming for winners. The bandeja is about position, not points. Three drills to groove it Feed and recover. Your partner feeds medium lobs from the back. Start at the net, turn, play a bandeja, touch the net area again. Ten in a row. Target zone. Put a towel two metres in front of the back glass. Count how many bandejas bounce between the towel and the glass. Live point. Play points where the defending pair may only lob. The net pair scores double if they still hold the net after three bandejas. You'll find progressions for all of these, from solo to full four-player drills, in The Padel Drill Book. When not to play a bandeja The bandeja is a tool, not a reflex. Two situations call for something else: The lob is short and slow. If you can get under the ball comfortably close to the net, attack it with a víbora or a smash. Playing a gentle bandeja here gives away a chance to finish the point. The lob has gone over you. If you have to lean back or jump backwards to reach it, the bandeja will float. Run back with your partner, let the ball come off the back glass and play a lob of your own to win the net back. Footwork in detail Good bandejas start with the feet, not the arm. For a right-hander: Read the lob the moment it leaves the opponent's racket. Height and depth tell you early whether this is a bandeja ball. Pivot on the right foot and turn your left shoulder to the net. Side-step or cross-step back until the ball will drop just in front of your left shoulder. Stop and set , weight on the back foot, before you swing. Hitting on the move is the main reason bandejas go long. Transfer the weight forward through the shot, and let that forward movement carry you back towards the net. Frequently asked questions What does bandeja mean? It's Spanish for "tray". The name comes from the racket position, held up beside the head like a waiter carrying a tray. Is the bandeja a smash? It's an overhead, but not a smash. A smash tries to win the point with power; the bandeja uses slice and control to keep your net position. What is the difference between a bandeja and a víbora? The bandeja is controlled, with backspin, to keep the net. The víbora is faster, hit on the outside of the ball with side spin, to put pressure on the opponents off the side glass. Which grip should I use? The continental grip, the same one most players use for volleys and the serve. 
##### padel-positioning-doubles
Most club points aren't lost on bad shots. They're lost on bad positions: one player up, one back, and a gap down the middle big enough to drive a bus through. Fix where you stand and your results improve before your technique does. The one rule: move as a pair Imagine you and your partner are tied together with a 3-metre rope. When one of you moves forward, the other does too. When one of you is pushed back by a lob, you both go back. Padel doubles is played side by side, not one up and one back like many social tennis doubles. The reason is geometry. If one player is at the net and the other at the back, the opponents simply hit into the diagonal gap between you, and nobody can reach it. The two home positions At any moment of a point, your pair should be in one of two places: At the net (attack) Roughly two to three metres from the net, close enough to volley at the feet of the opponents and far enough back to cover a lob. At the back (defence) A metre or two in front of the back glass, giving you space to let balls come off the walls. The space between, around the service line, is a transition zone. Pass through it, but don't stay there. A ball at your feet in mid-court is the hardest shot in padel. Win the net, hold the net The pair at the net is usually the pair winning the point. Your aim at the back is to get to the net ; your aim at the net is to stay there . From the back: a good, deep lob over the net players is your ticket forward. As soon as your lob passes them, move up together. At the net: when you get lobbed, answer with a bandeja or víbora and stay forward. Only go all the way back when the lob is too good. Who takes the middle? Balls down the middle cause more arguments than any other shot. Agree a simple rule before the match: For two right-handers, the player on the left side has the forehand in the middle and usually takes it. On lobs down the middle, the player whose forehand or overhead side covers it calls and takes it. Whoever is further forward takes a volley in the middle, because they can hit it earlier. Talk: "mine", "yours", "go". A one-word call loses fewer points than a silent collision. Left side or right side? Padel pairs pick a side and usually keep it for the whole match, receiving from the same box every time. The rules only fix this within a set, so you can swap at the start of a new one. The right side (often called the drive side) suits the steady, consistent player who builds the point, plays the low balls and keeps errors down. The left side (often called the revés side) suits the more aggressive player, usually the one with the stronger overhead, because for right-handers the forehand and smash cover the middle from the left. Neither side is "better". The best pairs combine one patient player and one finisher. Follow the ball sideways When the ball goes wide to one side of the opponents' court, shift across together, about the width of a step or two. The player nearest the ball covers the line; the partner slides towards the middle. Picture your pair as a wall that slides left and right, always facing where the ball is. Positioning checklist After every shot, check: are we level with each other? At the net: 2–3 m off it, racket up, ready for a lob. At the back: give yourself room in front of the glass. Don't stand still in no-man's-land around the service line. Lob, then move forward together. Get lobbed, then decide together. Call every middle ball. Positions when you serve When your pair is serving, you start as close to the attacking position as the rules allow: The server stands behind the service line, serves and then moves forward towards the net, ready for the return. The server's partner starts at the net in the other half of the court, already in the attacking position, watching for a weak return to volley. Within one or two shots, both of you should be level at the net. That's why serving pairs win so many points in padel: the serve is easy, but it lets you take the net straight away. Positions when you receive The receiver stands far enough back to let the serve bounce and, if needed, come off the back glass. The receiver's partner usually stands near the service line on the other side, ready to drop back if the serving pair attacks. The most common return is a lob or a low ball at the feet of the server coming in. A good lob over the net player is the receiving pair's best way to swap roles and take the net. Common positioning mistakes One up, one back. The classic tennis doubles habit. In padel it opens the diagonal. Standing too close to the net. You get lobbed every time. Two to three metres gives you time to turn. Standing on the back glass. You have no room to let the ball come off the wall. Step forward. Watching your own shot. Move as soon as you hit it. Silent middles. Call every ball down the centre. Frequently asked questions Where should I stand at the net in padel? Roughly two to three metres from the net, level with your partner, racket up. Close enough to volley, far enough back to deal with a lob. Should we play one up, one back in padel? No. Padel pairs move side by side: both at the net or both at the back. One up and one back leaves a diagonal gap. Which side should the better player play? The left side usually suits the more aggressive player with the stronger overhead. The right side suits the steady, consistent player. The best pairs combine both. 
##### how-to-choose-a-padel-racket
Walk into any padel shop and you'll see a wall of rackets that all look similar and cost anywhere from a cheap night out to a weekend away. Only four things really matter: shape, weight, balance and materials. Get those right for your level and the brand barely matters. What the rules say Every padel racket is solid, with no strings, and perforated with holes. Under the FIP rules a racket may be at most 45.5 cm long, 26 cm wide and 38 mm thick , and the wrist strap is compulsory. Any racket sold as a padel racket by a mainstream brand will meet these limits, so you can focus on how it plays. 1. Shape: round, teardrop or diamond Shape Sweet spot Feel Best for Round Large, low, centred Control and forgiveness Beginners, defensive and right-side players Teardrop Medium, slightly higher Balance of power and control Improvers and all-round players Diamond Small, near the top Maximum power, less forgiving Advanced, attacking left-side players If you're unsure, choose round . A big sweet spot means more balls go where you meant them to, and that matters far more than power while you're learning. 2. Weight Most adult padel rackets weigh roughly 350 to 380 grams , and junior rackets are lighter. Lighter (under about 370 g): quicker at the net, easier on the arm, less power. The right choice for beginners and anyone with arm or elbow trouble. Heavier (over about 370 g): more power and stability on smashes, but slower to manoeuvre and harder on the elbow if your technique isn't solid yet. 3. Balance Balance tells you where the weight sits, measured in millimetres from the bottom of the handle. Low balance (below about 265 mm): weight near your hand. Fast and easy to control. Medium (around 270 mm): the versatile middle. High (above about 275 mm): weight in the head. More power, but harder to handle and more strain on the elbow. Round rackets tend to have low balance and diamond rackets high balance, which is why shape and balance usually go together. 4. Materials: face and core The face. Fibreglass is softer, absorbs vibration and feels comfortable, good for beginners. Carbon is stiffer and more durable, gives more precise feedback and suits players with solid technique. The core. Soft foam cores feel comfortable and give the ball an easy push even on slow swings. EVA rubber cores are firmer and more durable, and give more control to players who swing fast. As a rule: softer for comfort and easy power, harder for control and players who swing fast. What should you buy? Beginner Round shape, under 370 g, low balance, fibreglass or soft face. A mid-priced racket is plenty. Improver / intermediate Teardrop shape, medium balance, carbon face if your technique is consistent. Advanced players choose by playing style: diamond and high balance for attacking smashers, teardrop or round for control players. Don't buy a professional's racket early. It's tuned for a technique you don't have yet, and it can slow your progress. Protect your elbow "Padel elbow" is real. The biggest risk factors from equipment are a racket that's too heavy, too head-heavy or too stiff for your level. If you feel pain on the outside of the elbow, go lighter, lower the balance, choose a softer face and core, and check your technique, especially on the bandeja and volley. Small things that make a big difference Overgrip: add one and replace it often. It improves grip, comfort and lets you fine-tune handle thickness. Edge protector tape: protects the frame from scrapes on the glass and floor. Try before you buy: many clubs rent demo rackets. Play a full session with one before you commit. Grip and handle Padel rackets come with a fairly standard handle, so you rarely choose a grip size the way you do in tennis. Instead, players adjust the thickness with overgrips: one for a slimmer feel, two for a thicker one. You want to hold the racket firmly without squeezing. If your knuckles go white, the handle is too thin. Rackets for kids and juniors Junior rackets are lighter and often slightly smaller. For players around 10 to 14, a light, round, soft racket makes learning easier and protects young arms. Avoid handing a child an adult's heavy, head-heavy racket. Matching the racket to your side of the court Your position in the pair is a useful tiebreaker between two rackets you like. Right-side players spend most of the point building rallies, playing low balls and returns, so control matters most: round or teardrop, lower balance. Left-side players hit more overheads and finishing shots, so they often prefer a teardrop or diamond with a little more weight in the head. If you switch sides often, a teardrop is the safe all-rounder. Where your money goes A higher price usually buys more carbon in the face and frame, more advanced core rubbers and lower weight for the same stiffness. Those differences are real, but they mostly help players who already hit the ball cleanly and consistently. For your first year of padel, a solid mid-range round racket will improve your game more than a top-of-the-range diamond. Spend the difference on coaching or court time. When to replace your racket Rackets wear out gradually. The core loses some of its liveliness, and cracks can appear in the frame after hits on the glass or floor. Signs it's time for a new one: a visible crack, a rattling sound, or the ball suddenly feeling dead off the face. Regular players often replace theirs around once a season; occasional players can go much longer. Before you buy: a checklist What's my level, honestly: beginner, improver or advanced? Do I prefer control or power? (If unsure: control.) Have I had any elbow or wrist pain? Then go lighter, lower balance and softer. Round, teardrop or diamond, matched to the answers above. Have I played at least one session with it, or a demo of the same model? Frequently asked questions What is the best padel racket shape for beginners? Round. It has the biggest, most forgiving sweet spot and usually a low balance, which makes it easy to control. How heavy should a padel racket be? Most adult rackets weigh around 350 to 380 grams. Beginners and players with arm problems should stay at the lighter end. Carbon or fibreglass? Fibreglass is softer and more comfortable, good for beginners. Carbon is stiffer and more precise, better once your technique is consistent. Are padel rackets regulated? Yes. The FIP rules limit rackets to 45.5 cm long, 26 cm wide and 38 mm thick, with a compulsory wrist strap. 
##### padel-vibora
The víbora (Spanish for "viper") is the overhead that bites. Where the bandeja is about keeping the net, the víbora is about pressure: a fast, side-spun ball that stays low off the glass and forces a weak reply. Here is how to hit it, and when to leave it in the bag. What makes a víbora different Padel has three main overheads, and they do different jobs: Shot Contact Spin Goal Bandeja Under and behind the ball, around head height Slice, controlled Keep the net Víbora On the outside of the ball, head height, in front Side spin plus slice, faster Pressure: a low, awkward ball off the glass Smash Full stretch, higher Flat or topspin, full power Win the point or force a weak reply The key difference from the bandeja is where the racket meets the ball. On a bandeja you cut underneath it. On a víbora you brush around its outside, which gives the side spin that makes the ball skid off the side glass and stay low in the corner. When to play it An easy lob while you're fairly close to the net. You have time and balance, so you can attack. A lob that's too deep to smash cleanly but not good enough to force you all the way back. Medium-height "dead" balls that sit up in mid-court. If you're stretched, late or moving backwards, play a bandeja or let the ball bounce instead. The víbora punishes bad positions, including yours. How to hit it, step by step Continental grip. The same "hammer" grip you use for volleys and the bandeja. Turn fully side-on. Shoulders and feet face the side wall. Get there with short side steps, not by backpedalling. Load the racket. Elbow up around shoulder height, racket behind your head, arm relaxed. Contact in front and away from the body. Arm extended, roughly at head height. Hit it higher and it becomes a flat smash; keep it at head height and the ball stays low. Brush around the outside of the ball. Swing forward and across, with a quick turn of the forearm. That creates the side spin and slice. Finish across your body and recover straight away towards the net with your partner. Where to aim The corner: into the side glass so the ball runs on into the back glass and comes out low. The opponent's backhand corner , where returning a low, spinning ball is hardest. The aim is a ball they can't attack, not a winner. A good víbora often wins the point on the next shot, when the reply floats up. Common mistakes Hitting too hard. Power without control sends the ball high off the glass, straight back to the opponents. Contact above the head. That turns it into a flat smash with none of the víbora's bite. Square stance. Without the side-on turn you can't swing across the ball. Contact behind the body. Usually caused by late footwork. Move first, then swing. Abandoning the stroke halfway. Commit to the swing; a half-hit víbora is the easiest ball to counter. Build it from the bandeja Most coaches teach the víbora after the bandeja, and it makes sense: same grip, same side-on set-up, similar contact height. Once your bandeja is reliable, start moving the contact point from under the ball to its outside edge and add a little more speed. Feed easy lobs, aim for the side glass, and count how many come out below knee height. Frequently asked questions What does víbora mean? It's Spanish for viper. The name comes from the way the ball "bites" off the glass with side spin. Is the víbora harder than the bandeja? Yes. It needs the same set-up plus more racket speed and precise contact. It's usually considered an intermediate-to-advanced shot. Should beginners learn the víbora? Learn the bandeja first. The víbora grows naturally out of a solid bandeja. Where should a víbora land? Deep in the opponents' corner, using the side glass, so the ball comes out low and close to the wall. Footwork: the part everyone skips A víbora is only as good as the position you hit it from. The ball should arrive slightly in front of you and to the side of your hitting shoulder, so you can swing across it with an extended arm. Read the lob early. As soon as the opponent's racket opens up for a lob, start turning. Side-step or cross-step back and slightly away from the ball. You want space between your body and the ball, not to be underneath it. Stop before you swing. A stable base gives you a clean contact; hitting on the run is how víboras end up in the mesh. Transfer the weight forward through the shot, and let that carry you back towards the net. Bandeja or víbora? A quick decision guide Situation Play Lob is good and deep, you're moving backwards Bandeja, or let it bounce Lob is medium, you're balanced and fairly close to the net Víbora Lob is short and easy near the net Smash Opponents are already at the net Bandeja to the feet or a safe deep ball When in doubt, choose the bandeja. Losing the net with a safe shot is rare; losing the point with a rushed víbora is common. How to practise it Shadow swings. Without a ball, practise turning side-on, loading the racket and swinging across. Ten slow reps, ten at speed. Hand-fed lobs. A partner throws easy lobs from the net. Play víboras into the side glass and count how many come out below knee height. Racket-fed lobs from the back. Your partner lobs from the opposite back line. Alternate: one bandeja, one víbora, focusing on changing only the contact point. Live points. Play points where the back pair may only lob. The net pair earns a bonus point every time a víbora forces a weak reply. The Padel Drill Book has a full section on lobs and overheads (drills 41–55), including a dedicated side-spin overhead drill. What a good víbora looks like The ball crosses the net fast and fairly flat. It lands deep, hits the side glass, then the back glass. It comes out low and close to the wall, so the opponent has to dig it out. You and your partner are back at the net before they hit it. 
##### padel-back-glass
Nothing separates tennis players from padel players faster than the back glass. Tennis instinct says the ball is gone; padel says wait, it's coming back. Learning to let the ball come off the glass is the single biggest step most beginners can take. What the rules allow Under the FIP rules, once the ball has bounced on your side it stays in play if it hits the walls, the mesh, the net or the posts. You just have to return it before it bounces a second time on the floor. Allowed Ball bounces on your side, hits the back or side glass, you play it before the second bounce. You may even hit it into your own walls, as long as it then lands in the opponents' court. Point lost Your shot hits the opponents' glass or mesh before bouncing on their side, or hits the mesh or floor on your own side. Read the full rules in our padel rules guide . Step 1: move early, and to the side The most common mistake is running straight back at the glass. Instead, move back early and slightly to the side, towards where the ball will be after it rebounds. Give yourself more space than feels natural: the ball comes out further than you expect. Step 2: turn and prepare as the ball reaches the glass Turn side-on and start your backswing while the ball is still travelling towards the wall. If you wait for the rebound to start preparing, you'll be late every time. Step 3: let it drop, then hit in front of you Patience is the skill. Let the ball come out and drop, and make contact in front of your body, moving forward through the shot rather than away from the glass. Which shot to play Ball height after the glass What to play Ankle to knee Defend softly: a controlled ball back into court or a lob. Waist to chest A controlled groundstroke, cross-court and deep, or a lob to win the net back. Above the shoulder Attack it, often as an overhead. When the opponents are at the net, the lob is often your best option from the back glass: it pushes them back and lets you and your partner move forward. Double walls: back then side, side then back When the ball hits two walls, its path changes: Side wall, then back wall: the ball kicks out towards the middle of the court. Back wall, then side wall: the ball kicks up the court, along the side wall. The trick is the same in both cases: turn, follow the ball's path with your feet, and hit at the end of it rather than chasing it into the corner. Using your own back wall When you're beaten and can't reach the ball before it passes you, you can hit it upwards into your own back glass so it rebounds over the net. It's a last resort: hit up with enough power to clear the net, but not so much that it flies into the opponents' glass without bouncing. Five mistakes to fix first Running straight back instead of back and to the side. Crowding the glass so the ball jams you. Late preparation: backswing only after the rebound. Flat feet: no small adjustment steps as the ball comes out. Over-hitting the rebound instead of playing a controlled ball. A simple practice routine Stand about three metres in front of the back glass, throw the ball firmly at the floor so it bounces into the glass, turn, let it come out and play it cross-court. Twenty forehands, twenty backhands. It's Drill 04 in The Padel Drill Book , and it's also on our free drill sheet . Frequently asked questions Can you hit the ball before it hits the back glass? Yes. Once it has bounced on your side you can play it before or after it hits the glass, as long as you return it before the second bounce. Can the ball hit the glass before bouncing? Not on the opponents' side: if your shot hits their wall before bouncing, you lose the point. On your own side, you may play the ball into your own wall so it rebounds over the net. Why do I keep getting jammed by the back glass? You're too close. Move back and to the side earlier, and leave more room than feels natural. Reading the rebound How the ball comes off the glass depends on how it arrives. With a little practice you can predict it before it hits the wall: Fast, flat ball: comes off hard and travels further out. Give it plenty of room. High, slow lob: drops close to the glass and comes out slowly. Be patient and move in late. Sliced ball: stays low and "dies" off the glass. Bend your knees early. Topspin ball: kicks up higher off the bounce and the glass, often giving you a chance to attack. Forehand side vs backhand side Most beginners are comfortable on the forehand side and panic on the backhand. The movement pattern is the same: turn early, move back and to the side, let the ball come out, contact in front. On the backhand, start the turn even earlier, because the backswing takes longer. Keep the swing short; control is the goal, not power. When to attack off the glass Not every ball off the back glass is a defensive ball. If it comes out above shoulder height, you can take it as an overhead and attack, a shot often called a bajada ("coming down"). Advanced players use it to turn a deep lob from the opponents into an attacking ball. As a beginner, start by playing controlled shots from the back and add the attack once your footwork is reliable. The lob from the back glass When both opponents are at the net, the lob is usually the best ball to play from the back. A good lob from the glass: Goes over the net players' reach, high enough that they can't smash it. Lands deep, between their service line and back glass. Gives you and your partner time to move forward together. A short lob is the opposite: it sits up for a smash. Aim for their back glass, not their service line. Back glass checklist Did I turn as soon as the ball passed me? Did I move back and to the side, not straight at the glass? Did I prepare the racket before the ball hit the glass? Did I wait for the ball to drop and hit in front of me? Did I choose the shot by height: defend low, control the middle, attack high? 
##### padel-elbow
If the outside of your elbow aches after padel, you're in good company: the elbow is one of the most commonly injured areas in the sport. Here is what "padel elbow" is, what the research says, and what you can do about it. Not medical advice This article explains a common padel injury in plain language. If you have pain, especially pain that lasts, see a doctor or physiotherapist. What is padel elbow? "Padel elbow" is the same condition as tennis elbow: lateral epicondylitis , now often called lateral elbow tendinopathy . It's an overuse injury of the tendons that attach the muscles that extend your wrist to the bony bump on the outside of your elbow. Many clinicians prefer the newer name because the problem is mainly tendon degeneration from overload, not inflammation. Typical symptoms, according to the NHS and Mayo Clinic: Pain on the outside of the elbow , sometimes spreading into the forearm and wrist. Worse when gripping, lifting or twisting the wrist , for example holding a cup or shaking hands. It often improves within weeks, but can last a year or more if the cause isn't addressed. It's most common in adults between about 30 and 60. How common is it in padel? A 2023 systematic review of padel injury studies (Dahmen et al., BMJ Open Sport & Exercise Medicine) found: Injury rates of roughly 3 per 1,000 hours of training and 8 per 1,000 matches . The elbow was the most commonly injured site in the studies it reviewed, though the share varied widely between studies. Lateral epicondylitis was the most frequently reported diagnosis. Why padel players get it Mayo Clinic lists racket sports as a classic cause, especially with poor technique, unsuitable equipment or a lot of playing time. Other risk factors include age between 30 and 60, smoking and obesity. In padel, the typical pattern looks like this: A sudden jump in playing time , the "new padel addict" pattern of several sessions a week straight away. Technique: gripping too tightly, hitting late or behind the body, and lots of wristy overheads and volleys. Equipment: coaches and retailers commonly advise that heavy, head-heavy or very stiff rackets put more load on the forearm. There's little research on this, so treat it as sensible advice rather than proven fact. Prevention: what actually helps Build up gradually. Increase your weekly playing time step by step instead of going from zero to five sessions. Strengthen the forearm and the whole arm. Research supports loading exercises for the wrist extensors, including eccentric (slow lowering) wrist exercises. Current thinking goes further: progressive strength work for the whole arm, shoulder and shoulder blade. Relax your grip. Hold the racket firmly enough to control it, no tighter. Squeezing on every shot overloads the forearm. Fix the technique that overloads the arm: late contact, flicky wrist on volleys, arm-only overheads. Use your legs and body rotation. Choose an arm-friendly racket: lighter, lower balance, softer face and core. See our racket guide . If it already hurts The NHS suggests these first steps for tennis elbow: Reduce the activities that cause the pain , including playing time. Painkillers or anti-inflammatory gel, and hot or cold packs, may ease symptoms. A forearm strap or brace can help some people. When to see a doctor See a GP if the pain hasn't improved after about two weeks of rest and self-care. Physiotherapy is usually recommended if symptoms continue beyond around six weeks. Surgery is rare and only considered after many months of symptoms. Frequently asked questions Is padel elbow the same as tennis elbow? Yes. Both are lateral epicondylitis (lateral elbow tendinopathy), an overuse injury of the tendons on the outside of the elbow. Can I keep playing with padel elbow? Reducing the activities that cause the pain is usually the first step. If it doesn't improve within a couple of weeks, see a doctor or physiotherapist before going back to full play. Which padel racket is best for elbow pain? Commonly advised: a lighter racket with low balance and a softer face and core. Technique and playing load matter at least as much as the racket. Load management: the most underrated fix Tendons adapt to load, but slowly. Problems usually start when the amount you play rises faster than your tendons can keep up with. A sensible approach for new or returning players: Start with one or two sessions a week, then add time gradually. Avoid back-to-back long sessions on consecutive days early on. Mix in rest days and other activities, especially when you're building up. If the elbow starts to grumble, reduce the load early instead of playing through it. An example forearm exercise Strength work for the wrist extensors is one of the best-studied approaches for this condition. A commonly used example is the eccentric wrist extension: Sit with your forearm resting on a table, palm facing down, wrist just past the edge. Hold a light weight (a small dumbbell or a water bottle). Use your other hand to help lift the wrist up. Slowly lower the weight on its own over about three to five seconds. Mild discomfort can be normal, but sharp pain isn't. Ask a physiotherapist for a programme suited to you, including shoulder and upper-back strength, which current research recommends alongside forearm work. Technique fixes that take load off the arm Use your legs and body rotation on groundstrokes and overheads, so the arm isn't doing all the work. Hit in front of your body. Late contact forces the wrist and forearm to compensate. Volleys from the shoulder, not the wrist. A short, firm punch beats a flicky wrist. Relax between shots. Loosen the grip while you wait for the ball. Racket and equipment checklist Weight towards the lighter end for your size and strength. Low or medium balance rather than head-heavy. A softer face (fibreglass) and core. A fresh overgrip so you don't need to squeeze to hold the racket. Our beginner racket picks are all on the arm-friendly side. Myths "It's inflammation, so rest fixes it." Rest helps calm symptoms, but the tendon also needs gradual loading to recover. "Only bad players get it." It's common at every level, especially among players who increase their playing time quickly. "A new racket will cure it." Equipment can help, but load and technique matter as much. 
##### padel-vs-pickleball
Padel and pickleball are the two fastest-growing racket sports of the decade, and people often mix them up. Both are social, easy to start and usually played as doubles. But on court they feel completely different. Here's a side-by-side comparison based on the official FIP and USA Pickleball rulebooks. Side by side Padel Pickleball Court 20 × 10 m 20 × 44 ft (about 6.1 × 13.4 m) Walls Fully enclosed by glass and mesh, walls in play Open court, no walls Net 88 cm in the middle, 92 cm at the posts 34 in (86 cm) in the middle, 36 in at the sidelines Racket Solid, perforated racket, max 45.5 cm long, wrist strap compulsory Solid paddle, length plus width max 24 in Ball Pressurised rubber ball, similar to a tennis ball Light perforated plastic ball Serve Underarm after a bounce, at or below the waist, diagonal Underhand, contact below the waist, diagonal, must clear the kitchen Special zone None The "kitchen": 7 ft non-volley zone either side of the net Scoring Tennis scoring (15, 30, 40), sets of six games Games to 11, win by 2; traditionally only the serving side scores Format Almost always doubles Singles and doubles The walls change everything The biggest difference isn't the racket or the ball, it's the enclosure. In padel, once the ball bounces on your side it can come off the back and side glass and you can still play it. Rallies last longer, and positioning and patience matter more than power. Pickleball has no walls; a ball that gets past you is gone, and the game is decided much more by short, fast exchanges at the net. Two rules unique to pickleball The two-bounce rule: the serve and the return must each bounce before being played. Only then can players volley. The kitchen: you can't volley while standing in the 7-foot non-volley zone next to the net, and momentum carrying you into it after a volley is a fault. Padel has no equivalent zone. You can volley right up at the net, as long as you don't touch it. Scoring Padel uses tennis scoring: points 15, 30, 40, game; sets to six games with tie-breaks; plus the golden point and Star Point options. Pickleball games are played to 11, win by 2, with side-out scoring, where only the serving side can score. USA Pickleball also allows rally scoring as a provisional option for some events. How big are they? Pickleball: about 19.8 million players in the US in 2024, according to the SFIA, the fastest-growing US sport for the fourth year running. Padel: about 19.4 million players and more than 58,000 courts worldwide, according to the Global Padel Report 2026 by Playtomic and Strategy&. Which one should you try? Choose padel if you like longer rallies, using angles and walls, and a game where tactics and positioning beat raw power. It's a little more physical. Choose pickleball if you want the gentlest possible start, short quick exchanges at the net, and courts that are easy to find in the US. Honestly? Try both. Many players do. If you're starting padel, our rules guide gets you through your first match. Frequently asked questions Is padel harder than pickleball? Both are easy to start. Padel's walls add a learning curve, but they also make rallies longer and more forgiving once you learn to let the ball come off the glass. Can you use a pickleball paddle for padel? No. Padel rackets are a different shape and size, perforated, and must have a wrist strap under the FIP rules. Is the padel court bigger than a pickleball court? Yes. A padel court is 20 × 10 m; a pickleball court is 20 × 44 ft, roughly 6.1 × 13.4 m. Equipment and cost Both sports are cheap to start. You need a racket or paddle, balls and court shoes. Padel rackets are generally thicker and heavier than pickleball paddles, and padel balls are pressurised, so they lose bounce over time and need replacing more often. Court hire is usually the main cost in both sports, and padel courts are often booked by the hour for four players. What playing feels like Padel Longer rallies, lots of lobs and overheads, and constant movement between the net and the back. Walls give you a second chance on balls that get past you. Pickleball Shorter points, lots of soft "dinks" near the kitchen line, and quick reflex exchanges at the net. The ball is light and slow, so rallies can be very tactical. The learning curve Day one: both are easy. Most people can rally within an hour. First months: in padel, the challenge is learning the walls and the overheads (bandeja, víbora). In pickleball, it's the soft game around the kitchen and the two-bounce rule. Long term: both reward tactics and teamwork more than raw power. For tennis players Tennis players usually adapt quickly to both. In padel, the hardest habit to break is chasing balls into the back glass instead of letting them come off. The second is swinging too hard: padel rewards control and placement. In pickleball, the adjustment is the underhand serve, the kitchen and learning the soft game. Fitness Both are good exercise. Padel usually involves more running between the back and the net and more overhead shots; pickleball has more short, quick movements in a smaller space. Either way, warm up properly and build up your playing time gradually. See our guide to padel elbow for keeping your arm healthy. Quick verdict If you love the idea of walls, lobs and longer rallies, padel is your sport. If you want the gentlest possible entry and a soft, tactical net game, start with pickleball. And if you're curious, there's nothing stopping you from playing both. Common questions Can you play both with the same shoes? Yes, as long as they're court shoes with good lateral support. Padel courts with sand-filled artificial grass grip better with a herringbone or omni sole. Running shoes are a poor choice for either sport, because they aren't built for quick side-to-side movement. Which is better for older players? Both are popular with older players. Pickleball's smaller court and slower ball make it very accessible. Padel's walls keep the ball in play, so you spend less time picking up balls and more time hitting them. The best choice is the one you'll enjoy and keep playing. Which is easier to find a game for? That depends entirely on where you live. Check the clubs and booking apps near you: in some areas padel courts are everywhere, in others pickleball courts are easier to find. Do skills transfer between the two? Partly. Both reward good positioning, teamwork, a compact volley and patience. The walls are unique to padel, and the kitchen and two-bounce rule are unique to pickleball, so expect a short adjustment period whichever way you switch. 
##### padel-serve-and-return
The padel serve is underarm and easy to learn, but it isn't harmless. A well-placed serve is your ticket to the net, and a smart return is how the receiving pair takes it away. Here are the rules first, then the tactics. The serve rules (FIP) Position: stand behind the service line, between the centre line and the side wall, without touching either line. Bounce: bounce the ball on the ground behind the service line, on your side. Contact: hit it at or below waist height, with at least one foot on the ground. Direction: diagonally, into the opponents' opposite service box. Each game starts from the right; you switch sides after every point. Attempts: two, as in tennis. Faults and lets It's a fault if you break any of the position or contact rules, miss the ball completely, the ball lands outside the correct box (lines are in), it hits you or your partner, or it bounces in the box and then hits the mesh before bouncing again. A serve that bounces in the box and then hits the glass is good. A serve that clips the net or posts and lands in the box is replayed, unless it then hits the mesh. If the receiver wasn't ready, the serve is a let. The receiver must let the serve bounce. If the serve hits the receiver or their racket before bouncing, the point goes to the server. Where to aim your serve Target Why Into the side glass The ball comes off the wall towards the receiver and buys you time to get to the net. The T (centre) Cuts the angle of the return and jams the receiver. At the body or feet Hard to step around and attack. Mix the targets so the receiver can't settle. Use slice to keep the ball low after the bounce, go deep, and make your second serve safe and central. The serve doesn't need to win the point, just give you the net. Serve and move The moment you've served, move forward. Your partner already starts at the net in the other half of the court; your job is to join them so you're level within one or two shots. See our positioning guide for where to stand. Returning serve The return's job is simple: get the ball back in play in a way the serving pair can't attack. Strong serve into the glass: let it come off, and block it back towards the server's feet. Strong serve down the T: lob over the net player, or play soft to the incoming server's feet. Easy serve: attack. Drive low at the server's feet, or lob deep and move up to the net yourself. Stand a small step back from the service line area, near the side glass but with room to let the ball rebound. Decide early whether you're taking the ball before or after the glass. Common serve and return mistakes Serving too short , so the receiver steps in and attacks. Staying back after serving instead of moving to the net. Hitting into the mesh : that's a fault, aim for the glass. Going for winners on the return , when a safe, low or deep ball is enough. Standing too close to the side glass and getting jammed by the rebound. Frequently asked questions Can you serve overhand in padel? No. You must bounce the ball and hit it at or below waist height. Can the serve hit the glass? Yes, after it bounces in the correct box. If it hits the mesh after the bounce, it's a fault. What happens if the serve hits the net? If it then lands in the correct box and doesn't hit the mesh, that serve is replayed. Can you volley the return of serve? No. The receiver must let the serve bounce first. Serve technique, step by step Continental grip , the same as for volleys. Stand side-on , front shoulder pointing towards the target box. Drop the ball beside and slightly in front of your front foot so it bounces to around hip height. Swing low to high and hit the ball at or below waist height, with a slightly open racket face for slice. Follow through towards the target , then move forward straight away. First serve vs second serve First serve Second serve Goal Make the receiver uncomfortable Get it in, deep enough that they can't attack Target Glass, T or body Central and deep Pace Medium to fast, with slice Medium, with more margin over the net Serving on the big points On a golden point or Star Point, the receiving pair chooses who receives. Expect their stronger returner. Don't try something new: serve your most reliable serve, deep, and get to the net. See our scoring guide for how these points work. Receiving: where to stand A small step behind where you expect the ball to bounce, with room between you and the side glass. Weight forward on the balls of your feet, racket up. Your partner stands near the service line on the other side, ready to move back if the servers attack. Against slice serves, which stay low, stand a little closer. Against serves with side spin, give yourself more room. Return targets that work at club level The server's feet as they move in: forces an upward volley. A deep lob over the server's partner at the net: pushes them back and lets you move forward. Through the middle when the serving pair isn't communicating. Practice tip: serve 3 sets of 12 from each side to a towel placed near the T. It's Drill 56 in The Padel Drill Book, and it's on our free drill sheet. Five common serve and return mistakes Serving and staying back. The serve is your ticket to the net. If you don't follow it in, you've given away the advantage. Going for too much on the second serve. A double fault gives away the point for free. Keep the second serve simple. Always serving to the same spot. Even a good serve becomes easy to read. Mix the glass, the T and the body. Returning hard and flat at the net player. At club level, this usually gets volleyed straight back. Low to the feet or a deep lob works far better. Standing too close to the side glass when receiving. Leave room so a serve into the glass doesn't jam you. Communicating with your partner Agree on a simple plan before each serve: where you're serving, and what you expect back. When receiving, decide who takes balls through the middle. A short word between points ("glass", "middle", "lob") keeps both of you on the same page and makes the first two shots of every rally much more effective. 
##### best-padel-rackets-for-beginners
Your first padel racket should make the game easier, not harder. That means a round or soft teardrop shape, a manageable weight, a soft face and core, and a price that doesn't hurt if you end up wanting something else in a year. Here are six current rackets that tick those boxes. What makes a racket beginner-friendly Round shape (or a soft teardrop): the biggest, most forgiving sweet spot. Manageable weight , roughly 345–375 g for most adults, with lighter options for smaller players. Low or medium balance : easy to swing and gentle on the elbow. Fibreglass face and soft core : comfortable, with easy power on slow swings. Our full racket guide explains each of these in detail. How we chose We looked for current models (2025–2026 ranges) with beginner-friendly specs confirmed on the manufacturer's or a major retailer's product page: round or soft teardrop shape, moderate weight, fibreglass face, soft core, and an entry-level price. We don't receive anything from these brands, and none of the links are paid. What to avoid as a beginner Diamond-shaped, head-heavy pro rackets. Powerful in the right hands, unforgiving in yours. Very stiff, full-carbon rackets if you have any arm or elbow trouble. Spending top-of-the-range money before you know your style. A mid-range racket and a few lessons will improve your game more. Frequently asked questions How much should I spend on my first padel racket? Most good beginner rackets cost roughly €50–100. You don't need a premium racket to learn the game well. Round or teardrop for a beginner? Round is the safest choice for control and forgiveness. A soft teardrop suits beginners who want a little more power. Do beginners need a carbon racket? No. Fibreglass faces are softer and more comfortable, which suits most beginners better. Budget, mid-range or more? Budget What you get Who it suits Under about €60 Basic fibreglass, round rackets. Fine for learning. Trying padel, occasional players About €60–100 Better materials and feel, still forgiving. Most of the picks above. Beginners who play weekly Over €100 More carbon, more power, often less forgiving. Improvers with consistent technique Lighter players and juniors If you're smaller, have less arm strength or have had elbow trouble, look at the lighter end: around 340–355 g for adults, or a dedicated junior racket for children. A lighter racket is quicker at the net and easier on the arm, and the small loss of power matters far less than control when you're learning. Is last year's model worth it? Often, yes. Brands release new versions every year, and the previous model is frequently discounted. For a beginner, the differences between one year and the next are usually small. What matters is shape, weight, balance and materials, so check those specs rather than the year on the frame. Before you buy: a five-point checklist Round or soft teardrop shape. Weight you can swing comfortably for an hour and a half. Low or medium balance. Fibreglass face and a soft core. Try it first if you can: many clubs rent demo rackets. Look after your racket Add an overgrip and replace it regularly for comfort and grip. Use edge protector tape so scrapes on the glass and floor don't damage the frame. Keep it out of hot cars : heat can affect the core and the face. When to upgrade Upgrade when your technique is consistent and you know what you want more of. If you want more power for overheads, try a teardrop with medium balance. If you want more feel, try a slightly firmer core. If you're happy, don't upgrade just because a new model came out. Common questions Can I use a tennis racket for padel? No. Padel is played with a solid, stringless racket with holes in the face. A tennis racket is too long, and padel balls are slightly less pressurised than tennis balls, so the game is designed around the padel racket. How long does a beginner racket last? That depends on how often you play and how much the racket hits the glass and floor. For a beginner playing once or twice a week, a good racket often lasts a season or more. If the face cracks or the frame is damaged, it's time to replace it. Should I buy a racket or rent one first? If you're not sure you'll keep playing, rent or borrow first. Once you're playing regularly, your own racket gives you consistency: the same weight, balance and feel every session, which helps you improve faster. Do pros' rackets help beginners? Usually not. Professional models tend to be stiffer, more head-heavy and less forgiving. They're built for players who hit the sweet spot consistently. As a beginner, a forgiving round racket will help you more. 
##### padel-court-dimensions
A padel court is small, enclosed and very precisely defined. Knowing its measurements helps you understand the game: why the service box is where it is, why lobs work, and why some smashes leave the court. Here are the official numbers from the International Padel Federation (FIP), explained in plain English. The key numbers at a glance Element Official measurement Playing area 20 m long × 10 m wide (inside measurements) Service lines 6.95 m from the net on each side Net height 0.88 m in the centre, 0.92 m at the ends Net length 10 m Back walls 4 m in total: 3 m of wall plus 1 m of metal mesh Minimum clear height 6 m above the whole court (8 m suggested for new venues) The FIP allows a tolerance of 0.5% on the court's length and width, so every official court around the world plays essentially the same. The lines A padel court has very few lines. There's no baseline or sideline to call: the walls are the boundaries. What you do have: The net , dividing the court into two 10 × 10 m halves. The service lines , parallel to the net and 6.95 m from it on each side. That leaves about 3 m between the service line and the back wall. The central service line , running from the net to the service line and splitting that area into two service boxes. The FIP says it should extend at least 20 cm beyond the service line. The lines only matter on the serve. During the rally, the ball can land anywhere in the opponents' half. See our serve and return guide for how the service boxes are used. The net The padel net is 10 m long and slightly lower in the middle: 88 cm at the centre, rising to 92 cm at the ends. The posts may be up to 1.05 m tall. Because the centre is lowest, a ball hit through the middle has a little more room to clear the net, one reason so many rallies are played there. For comparison, a tennis net is 0.914 m (3 feet) in the centre, so the padel net is slightly lower. The walls The enclosure is what makes padel padel. The FIP rules describe it in detail. Back walls Each end of the court has a back wall 4 m high in total: the lower 3 m is solid wall (usually glass), and the top 1 m is metal mesh. Balls that bounce and then hit the back wall stay in play, which is why learning to play off the back glass is such a big part of the game. Side walls The FIP allows two layouts for the sides: Stepped walls: at each end, a first section 3 m high and 2 m long, then a second section 2 m high and 2 m long. Metal mesh completes the enclosure. Straight "crystal" walls: a 3 m high, 4 m long wall section at each end, with no step. Metal mesh fills the rest of the sides. With stepped walls, it reaches 3 m in the central 6 m of the court and 4 m at both ends; with straight walls, it reaches 4 m over the outermost 2 m at each end. The mesh openings must be between 5 and 7.08 cm diagonally. The difference matters during play. The ball bounces predictably off glass, but mesh absorbs speed and gives an irregular rebound. Many players aim at the mesh deliberately for exactly that reason. The doors Courts have openings in the side walls so players can get in and out, and in some formats, play the ball outside the court. The FIP sets sizes for courts with one opening per side (between 1.05 × 2.0 m and 2.20 × 2.20 m) and with two openings per side (between 0.72 × 2.0 m and 1.10 × 2.20 m). Outside the court, a safety area must be free of obstacles if players are allowed to leave the court during a point. That's what makes the dramatic "out-of-court" recoveries possible. Read more in our smash guide , which covers the por tres and por cuatro. Height and lighting Lobs are a core part of padel, so the space above the court matters. The FIP requires at least 6 m of clear height across the whole court, with no lights or beams in the way, and suggests 8 m for new facilities. Light poles must stand outside the court, lights must be at least 6 m above the ground, and the light must be even and must not impair players' vision. For outdoor courts, the FIP recommends a north–south orientation so the low sun doesn't shine straight into players' eyes. The playing surface The rules allow cement, synthetic materials or artificial grass, as long as the bounce is regular. The surface must be one colour: green, blue or terracotta. Most clubs today use sand-filled artificial grass, which affects the kind of shoes you need. See our guide to padel shoes . Padel vs tennis vs pickleball courts Padel Tennis (doubles) Pickleball Size 20 × 10 m 23.77 × 10.97 m 44 × 20 ft (about 13.4 × 6.1 m) Walls Yes, in play No No Net (centre) 0.88 m 0.914 m 34 in (about 0.86 m) Depending on the layout, two or three padel courts can fit into the space of one tennis court with its run-off areas, which is one reason padel courts are popping up so quickly. More on the differences in padel vs pickleball . The ball and racket, too The FIP also defines the equipment. A padel ball measures 6.35 to 6.77 cm in diameter and weighs 56.0 to 59.4 g. Dropped from 2.54 m onto a hard surface, a new ball must bounce between 135 and 145 cm. Rackets may be at most 45.5 cm long and 38 mm thick, with a compulsory wrist strap. Our racket guide explains what those specs mean in practice. Building your own court? If you're planning a court at home or for a club, use the official FIP rulebook and the specifications from a reputable court builder, not just the summary above. Things like glass certification, drainage and the safety area outside the court have their own requirements. 
##### padel-lob
In padel, the pair at the net usually wins the point. The lob is how you take the net back. It's the shot that turns defence into attack, and good players hit it more than almost any other. Here is how to hit it well and where to put it. Why the lob matters so much When your opponents are at the net and you're at the back, you're under pressure. Hitting harder rarely helps: net players are close, quick and expect it. A good lob changes everything. It sends the ball over their heads, forces them to turn and run back, and gives you and your partner time to move forward and take the net. That's why the lob isn't a "defensive" shot in padel. It's the main way to win back the attacking position. How to hit it, step by step Prepare early. Turn side-on with the racket back, as you would for a normal groundstroke. The earlier you're ready, the harder the lob is to read. Get low. Bend your knees so you can get under the ball. Open the racket face so it points more towards the sky. This, not a big swing, creates the height. Swing low to high , pushing up through the ball with your legs. Finish high , with the racket above your head. Move forward with your partner as soon as the lob gets past the net players. How high and how deep? The perfect lob goes high enough that the net players can't reach it with an overhead, and lands deep, just beyond their service line but before the back glass. Too short and it sits up for a smash. Too long and it bounces high off the back glass, giving them an easy ball. A useful image: you want the ball to drop behind the net players, so they have to turn around to play it. Where to aim Direction Pros Cons Cross-court Longest distance, most room for error. Harder for opponents to attack off the glass. Gives the net players a little more time to turn and chase it. Down the line Close to the side wall, so it's hard to smash. Shortest distance, least margin. Down the middle Can cause confusion over who takes it. The easiest lob for opponents to attack. If you play it, keep it fast rather than high. For most club players, the cross-court lob is the safest default. When to lob When both opponents are at the net and you're at the back. This is the classic situation. From the back glass , after letting a ball come off the wall. When the net players are close to the net , leaving space behind them. On the return of serve , especially against a strong net player. See our serve and return guide . When you need time , for example after being pulled out of position. The lob is less effective when the opponents are already standing back, or when the ball comes to you too low or too fast to control. In that case, a low ball to the feet, like a chiquita , may be better. The offensive lob Advanced players also use a lower, faster lob, sometimes with topspin. It's harder to hit, but it passes the net players quickly and gives them less time to react. Try it once your normal lob is reliable. A misjudged attacking lob is an easy smash for the opponents. Common mistakes Lobbing short. The most common mistake, and the most expensive. Aim deeper than feels natural. Swinging too hard. Height comes from the open racket face and the lift from your legs. Staying back after a good lob. If the lob forces the opponents back, move forward and take the net. Telegraphing it. If you open the racket face early, or prepare differently from your other shots, opponents can read it. Lobbing into the ceiling or lights indoors. Know the venue's height before you play. How to practise the lob Touch drills. Balance the ball on the racket, drop it and lift it up. Then rally by tapping the ball softly up into the air. Target lobs. Place a towel or cones just behind the service line on the other side. Lob from the back and count how many land in the zone. Two up, two back. Two players at the net, two at the back. The back pair must start every point with a lob. Swap every few points. Lob and move. After every lob that gets past the net pair, both back players move up to the net. The goal is to win the net, not just hit the lob. The Padel Drill Book includes a full section of lob and overhead drills you can use as a pair or a four. What the net players do If you're on the other side, a good lob forces a choice: play an overhead such as a bandeja or víbora , or turn, let the ball bounce and play it off the back glass. Understanding this helps you lob better: the deeper and higher your lob, the harder it is for them to attack it. Lobbing off the back glass Many of the best lobs are played after the ball has come off the back glass. Let the ball come out, wait for it to drop, then get under it with an open face. Because the ball is moving towards the net after the rebound, you don't need much swing: the lift from your legs and the open racket face do most of the work. Patience is key. Players who rush this shot usually hit it short. Common questions Forehand or backhand lob? Both. Club players tend to lob more comfortably on the forehand, so opponents often attack the backhand side. Practise the backhand lob with the same open face and low-to-high swing, and keep it short and controlled. How do I know if my lob was good? Watch the net players. If they turn and run back, or let the ball bounce and play it off the glass, the lob did its job. If they step back and smash, it was too short. 
##### padel-volley
Most points in padel are won at the net, and the volley is the shot you'll hit most from there. A good padel volley isn't a big swing. It's a short, firm, controlled punch that keeps the ball low and keeps you in the attacking position. The grip: continental Use the continental grip, sometimes called the hammer grip, for both forehand and backhand volleys. Hold the racket as if you were about to knock a nail into the wall with its edge. The big advantage is that you never have to change grip at the net, where there's no time. Hold the handle loosely while you wait and firm up only at contact. A tight grip kills your feel and tires the forearm. See our guide to padel elbow for why that matters. The ready position Racket up and in front of you, roughly at chest height. Elbows slightly away from the body. Knees softly bent, weight on the balls of your feet. Feet a little wider than shoulder-width. Stand about two metres from the net, so you can move forward into the ball and still cover the lob. How to hit it, step by step Turn your shoulders slightly towards the ball. On fast balls, the shoulder turn is enough of a backswing. Keep the backswing short. The racket shouldn't go back further than your shoulder. Step forward into the ball with the opposite foot if you have time. Contact in front of your body , with the racket head up and the face slightly open. Punch through, high to low, with a short follow-through. The power comes from the incoming ball and your body, not your wrist. Recover straight away to the ready position. Three volleys every player needs The block volley When the ball comes fast or you're stretched, don't swing. Just hold the racket firm and block the ball back, deep and low. It buys you time. The deep volley The bread-and-butter padel volley. With a slightly open face and a high-to-low motion, you put slice on the ball and send it deep into the opponents' court. It keeps them at the back and comes off the glass low. The angled or attacking volley When the ball is slower and higher, you can step in and play an angle towards the side glass or a firm volley at an opponent's feet. Choose this when you're balanced and close to the net. Where to aim Deep, at the opponents' feet , so they have to hit up from a low position. Into the side glass near the corner, so the ball stays low. Down the middle , where opponents may hesitate over who takes it. Don't aim for winners off every ball. In padel, the walls keep the ball in play, so patience at the net wins more points than power. Forehand vs backhand volley With the continental grip, both use the same basic motion. On the backhand side, turn the shoulders a little more and keep the elbow in front of the body. Many players find the backhand volley easier to control once the grip is right, because the arm naturally stays in front. Common mistakes Swinging like a groundstroke.
