A big swing at the net costs time and control. Late preparation. If you're reaching back to hit the ball, you were late. Dropping the wrist , which opens the face too much and floats the ball up. Gripping too tightly , which kills feel. Standing too close to the net , which leaves you open to the lob and removes the room to step in. Volleys and positioning Volleying well is only half the job. With your partner, move as a unit: if the ball goes to the left, you both shift left; if you're lobbed, you both move back. Our article on padel positioning explains how pairs cover the court together. How to practise volleys Volley to volley. Two players at the net, keeping a soft, controlled rally going. Count consecutive volleys. Feed and volley. A partner feeds from the back, you volley deep to a target near the back glass. Two at the net vs two at the back. The net pair may only volley deep. Score a point each time the back pair has to hit up. Wall volleys. On your own, volley against a solid wall to work on a firm wrist and short swing. The Padel Drill Book includes a full section of net and volley drills for solo, pairs and group practice. Volleys on low balls Not every ball at the net arrives at a comfortable height. When the ball is below the net, bend your knees rather than dropping the racket head, open the face a little and lift the ball over the net, deep. Don't try to attack a low ball: the aim is to get it back deep and low so you keep the net. Attack on the next, higher ball. The half-volley Sometimes the ball lands right at your feet. Then you play it just after the bounce with a very short, firm motion, almost like a block. Keep the racket face stable and push the ball deep. Good positioning, about two metres from the net, means you'll need it less often. Who takes the middle? Balls through the middle cause many lost points at club level. A simple rule many pairs use: the player whose forehand is in the middle takes it, unless the ball is clearly closer to the other player. Agree on it before the match and call "mine" or "yours" early. Quick volley checklist Continental grip, relaxed hand. Racket up and in front. Short backswing, contact in front. Punch high to low, short follow-through. Aim deep and low, not for the lines. Recover and move with your partner. Volleying against the lob The better you volley, the more your opponents will lob. That's why the ready position keeps you about two metres from the net. When you see the opponent's racket face open up, start moving back early, turn side-on and decide: overhead if you can reach it comfortably, or turn and let it bounce if you can't. Volleying well and covering the lob go together. 
##### padel-smash
The smash is padel's most spectacular shot, and its most overused. Because of the walls, a hard smash that isn't perfect often comes straight back. The trick is knowing which ball to smash, how, and when to play a bandeja instead. Smash, bandeja or víbora? Padel has three overheads, and choosing the right one is half the battle: Ball Best overhead High, short lob, you're close to the net and balanced Smash Medium lob, you're near the service line Víbora or a controlled smash Deep lob, you're moving backwards Bandeja , or let it bounce A common coaching rule: if you have to force the smash, it's the wrong shot. Smash the easy balls; play the bandeja on the rest. Why smashing is harder in padel In tennis, a hard smash that lands in the court usually wins. In padel, the ball bounces, hits the back glass and comes out again, often at a comfortable height. The harder and flatter you hit towards the back, the more the glass gives back. So padel smashes either have to leave the court, or be placed so the rebound is awkward. Flat smash technique Turn side-on early and move under the ball with short steps. Point at the ball with your free hand to track it and keep your balance. Load the racket behind your head, elbow up. Contact at full extension, slightly in front of your hitting shoulder. Turn the forearm through the ball (pronation) rather than chopping down. Recover forward . If the ball comes back, you want to be at the net. Most players use a continental grip, the same as for the volley and bandeja. The por tres (x3) "Por tres" means "by three" in Spanish. It's a smash that bounces in the opponents' court, rebounds off the back wall and then goes out of the court over the side, named after the 3 m height of the side enclosure. Players usually hit it from close to the net with plenty of height and spin, so the ball bounces before the service line and kicks off the back wall near the corner and out over the side. The por cuatro (x4) "Por cuatro" means "by four." It's a flat, powerful smash hit from very close to the net, aimed down into the court so the ball bounces high and goes out over the back of the court, which is 4 m high (3 m of wall plus 1 m of mesh). It needs a high, short ball and a lot of power. What the rules say about balls leaving the court According to the FIP rules: If a ball bounces correctly in the opponents' court and then leaves the court, the opponents lose the point unless they can legally play it. Where the court and its safety area allow out-of-court play, players may run out through the door and return a ball that went over the side wall or through a door, as long as it hasn't bounced a second time or touched anything outside the court. That's how professionals sometimes rescue a por tres. A ball that goes over the back wall after bouncing correctly can't be played. The point is lost, even where out-of-court play is allowed. That's why a good por cuatro can't be returned. At many clubs, leaving the court during a point isn't authorised. Then any ball that bounces and leaves the court, through a door or over a wall, wins you the point. Check the rules where you play. More in our padel rules guide . Where else to aim your smash The side glass , so the ball comes off at an awkward angle. Between the opponents , especially if they're slow to communicate. Not at the back glass with a flat smash from the service line. It usually comes back at a comfortable height. Common mistakes Smashing from too deep. From the back third, the ball can't leave the court and comes back off the glass. Smashing balls you can't reach at full stretch. If the ball has dropped to around head height, it's a bandeja or víbora ball. Watching your own shot. Recover immediately; a padel smash often comes back. Going for x3 and x4 too early. Start with smashes you can place reliably, then add height and power. Losing the net. A failed smash that leaves you stuck in mid-court is worse than a safe bandeja. How to practise the smash Shadow swings. Practise the side-on turn, pointing hand and full extension without a ball. Fed short lobs. A partner feeds easy, short lobs. Smash to the side glass, then recover to the net. Choose the shot. The feeder mixes short and deep lobs. Smash the short ones, bandeja the deep ones. Por tres practice. Once your smash is consistent, try sending a few short, high balls out over the side wall. The Padel Drill Book has a full section of overhead drills, from bandeja to smash. The smash and your partner When you go for a smash, your partner's job is to stay alert. If the smash comes back, they need to cover the volley or the next lob. Many pairs agree that the player who smashes moves straight back towards the net while the partner holds position. If your smash fails and you're stuck in mid-court, your partner should be ready to cover the lob over your head. Defending against the smash If you're on the receiving end, step back and give the ball room. Most smashes that stay in the court come off the back glass. Turn, let the ball come out, and reply with a lob or a low ball. Our guide to the back glass shows how. Smashing in the wind and sun Outdoors, conditions change the smash. In the wind, the ball can drift late, so take smaller adjustment steps and keep your pointing hand on the ball for longer. Against a low sun, a bandeja is often the safer choice: you can play it with a shorter look at the ball. Many players simply avoid big smashes when they can't see the ball clearly, and that's sensible padel. 
##### padel-chiquita
Not every way back to the net goes over your opponents' heads. The chiquita (Spanish for "little one") is a soft, low ball that just clears the net and drops at the net players' feet. It forces them to hit up, and that's your chance to move forward and take the net. What a chiquita is A chiquita is a slow, low shot played from the middle or back of the court towards the feet of opponents at the net. It isn't a power shot or a winner. Its job is to make the opponents volley upwards from a low position, so their reply is weak and you can attack. Think of it as the low alternative to the lob . Both aim to win back the net: the lob goes over the net players, the chiquita goes under their comfort zone. When to play it When the net players are a little too far back , around the service line, leaving space at their feet. When they've been volleying comfortably and you want to change the rhythm. When the ball comes to you low and slow , too low for a good lob. To mix up your game after several lobs, so the net players can't sit back and wait. Avoid it when the net players are very close to the net and ready, as they can simply step in and volley it away. And don't play it when you're pinned against the back glass: you won't get forward in time. How to hit it, step by step Read the ball early and move into position with short steps. Set up like a lob. Get under the ball with the racket face slightly open, so the opponents can't tell what's coming. Bend your knees to get low and stay balanced. Swing slowly and gently. This is a touch shot: placement over power. Aim just over the net , so the ball dips at the net players' feet. Move forward straight away with your partner. Most chiquitas are hit fairly flat. Advanced players add topspin, so the ball dips faster, or slice, so it stays low after the bounce. Where to aim At the feet of the net player, ideally on their backhand side. Down the middle , between the two net players, where neither is sure who takes it. Into any gap the opponents have left. The follow-up is everything The chiquita only works if you use the time it creates. A good one forces a soft upward volley, which is your cue to move up to the net together. If you stay at the back admiring the shot, the opponents recover and you've given them an easy ball. A classic pattern: chiquita to the feet, weak volley back, then you and your partner take the net and volley the next ball deep. Common mistakes Hitting too hard. A fast chiquita becomes a comfortable volley for the net player. Hitting too high. If the ball comes up above net height, it's an easy put-away. Not moving forward after the shot. Playing it from too deep , close to the back glass. Being predictable. Mix chiquitas with lobs so the net players have to guess. Chiquita vs lob: a quick guide Situation Better choice Net players very close to the net Lob Net players around the service line Chiquita Ball comes to you high and comfortable Lob Ball comes to you low and slow Chiquita You need time to recover position Lob How to practise it Target practice. Place a towel a few metres beyond the net on the other side, where the net players' feet would be. From the service line and then from the back, play soft balls that land on or near it. Two up, two back. The back pair must alternate lob and chiquita. The net pair scores a bonus point for every chiquita they can volley down. Chiquita and move. After every chiquita, the back pair moves to the net. Play out the point. The Padel Drill Book has drills for touch shots and transitions from the back to the net. Defending against a chiquita If you're the net player facing a chiquita, the key is to stay low and keep the volley deep. Bend your knees rather than dropping the racket head, open the face a little and push the ball back deep into the corner. Don't try to attack a low ball: just get it back deep and keep your position. Stepping in towards the net early, before the ball dips, makes the chiquita much less effective. A chiquita in a rally: example The opponents hit a deep volley; you let it come off the back glass. You see both net players standing around the service line. Instead of lobbing, you play a soft chiquita at the feet of the player on your diagonal. They have to volley up from below the net. You and your partner move forward together and volley the floating reply deep. Now you have the net. Common questions Is the chiquita a beginner shot? It's a touch shot, so it takes some practice, but beginners can start using it once they can control the ball at slow pace. It's a great way to learn that padel is about placement, not power. Does the chiquita work in the women's and men's pro game? Yes. It's a standard shot at all levels, and you'll often see professionals use it to break up long sequences of lobs and overheads. Building it into your game Start by using the chiquita once or twice a set, in a situation you've practised: the opponents around the service line and a slow ball coming to you. As it becomes reliable, use it more often and from different positions. The goal is that the net players can never settle, because they don't know whether a lob or a low ball is coming next. Quick chiquita checklist Opponents around the service line? Consider the chiquita. Same set-up as a lob, to disguise it. Slow, gentle swing; just clear the net. Aim at the feet or the middle. Move forward with your partner straight away. 
##### padel-shoes
Shoes are the most underrated piece of padel gear. The game is all short sprints, side steps and quick stops on artificial grass. The right pair helps you move faster and more safely; the wrong pair can make you slip, or hurt your ankles and knees. Do you really need padel shoes? For your first session, any court shoe with good lateral support is fine. If you're going to play regularly, a proper pair of padel shoes is worth it. Padel is played mostly on sand-filled artificial grass, and the movement is mainly sideways: side steps, quick changes of direction, sudden stops at the net. Shoes built for padel give you grip on that surface and support for that movement. Why not running shoes? Running shoes are built for forward motion. They usually have soft, high cushioning and little side support, which can make you feel unstable when you change direction. Their outsoles aren't designed for sand-filled grass either. Many coaches and retailers advise against them on a padel court. Padel shoes vs tennis shoes Tennis shoes are much closer, and many people start in them. The main differences: Surface: tennis shoes are designed for hard courts, clay or grass. Padel shoes are designed for padel surfaces, especially sand-filled artificial grass. Sole: padel shoes use sole patterns that grip in sand without clogging. Reinforcement: many padel shoes have extra protection at the toe and sides, where they wear from dragging and quick stops. A clay-court tennis shoe with a herringbone sole can be a reasonable stand-in while you're starting out, though padel-specific soles often have a deeper tread for sand-filled grass. Sole types: herringbone vs omni Sole What it looks like Best for Herringbone A zigzag pattern across the sole Grips well on most padel surfaces while still allowing controlled sliding. Omni / studded Small, dense studs or pimples Designed for sand-filled artificial grass, where the studs resist clogging. Hybrid A mix of herringbone and studded zones Players who play on different courts. Both main types work on padel courts, and advice on which suits sandier courts varies between brands and retailers. The practical test: try a pair on the courts you actually play on. If you slip or feel stuck, try the other sole type or a hybrid. What to look for Lateral support: the shoe should hold your foot in place when you push off sideways. A stable, low platform: enough cushioning for comfort, but not so much that you feel wobbly. Grip without stickiness: you want to stop quickly but still be able to turn. Durability at the toe: padel shoes wear fastest at the front and on the inside. Breathability , especially if you play outdoors in hot weather. Getting the fit right Try them on in the afternoon , when your feet are slightly bigger, and with the socks you play in. Leave about a thumb's width of room at the front. Check the heel: it shouldn't slip when you walk or move sideways. Do a few side steps and quick stops in the shop. Your foot shouldn't slide inside the shoe. When to replace them Look at the sole. Once the pattern at the front of the foot is worn smooth, the grip is gone and you're more likely to slip. Also check the upper: if the shoe no longer holds your foot firmly, or the cushioning feels flat, it's time for a new pair. How long that takes depends on how often you play and on the surface. Shoes for kids Children grow fast, so a mid-range junior court shoe with a herringbone or omni sole is plenty. What matters most is a good fit and sideways support, not the brand. Our Kids Edition has more advice for young players and parents. The rest of your kit Shoes, a racket and balls are all you need to start. If you're still choosing a racket, see our best padel rackets for beginners and our guide on how to choose a padel racket . If you've never played, read the rules of padel first. They take about five minutes. Quick checklist Court shoes, not running shoes. Herringbone, omni or hybrid sole to suit your club's courts. Firm sideways support and a heel that doesn't slip. A thumb's width of room at the toe. Replace them when the front of the sole is worn smooth. Indoor vs outdoor courts Indoor courts are usually cleaner, with less loose sand and no rain, so grip is more consistent. Outdoor courts can be dusty, sandy or damp, depending on the weather. If you mostly play outdoors, test your shoes on those courts, since grip can change with sand and moisture, and look for a breathable upper for hot days. Looking after your shoes Use them only on court. Walking on pavement wears down the sole pattern quickly. Knock the sand out after each session. Let them dry at room temperature , not on a radiator or in a hot car. Loosen the laces to take them off, rather than pushing them off with the other foot. Common questions Can I wear my padel shoes for tennis? On clay or artificial grass, often yes. On hard courts, a padel sole may wear down quickly, so a hard-court tennis shoe is the better choice there. Are expensive padel shoes worth it? Not necessarily. For most club players, a mid-range shoe with the right sole and a good fit does the job. Higher-end models may add lighter materials, more durability or extra stability, which matter more if you play several times a week. Socks and insoles Good socks matter more than people think. Sport socks with some padding at the heel and forefoot reduce blisters and help the shoe fit snugly. If you use custom insoles, take them with you when you try on shoes, as they change the fit. Most padel shoes have a removable insole, so swapping one in is usually easy. One last tip: if your feet or knees hurt after playing, don't just push through it. Check your shoes first, and if the pain continues, have it looked at. 
##### can-you-play-the-ball-off-your-own-wall
Yes: in padel you can hit the ball against your own glass wall, and it is a legal return as long as the ball then crosses the net and bounces in your opponents' court (FIP Rule 14b). What you cannot do is send it into your own metal mesh or your own ground, and the ball must not hit your opponents' walls before it bounces on their side. Those three conditions decide almost every own-wall argument at club level. The short answer Hitting your own glass is allowed. The ball then has to clear the net and bounce first in the opponents' court. If it touches your own mesh or your own ground after you hit it, or flies straight into the opponents' glass or fence without bouncing, you lose the point. What the rules say Several rules work together here, so it helps to take them one at a time. FIP Rule 14b defines a correct return. The ball is good if it bounces directly in the opponents' court, or if it first strikes a wall of your own court and then bounces in the opponents' court. That second half is the whole reason own-wall play exists. You can play the ball into your back glass or your side glass, let it rebound over the net, and the shot is just as valid as a normal drive. FIP Rule 13l sets the limit. You lose the point if, after you hit it, the ball touches the metal fence, the ground on your own side, or any object unrelated to the court lying on the ground on your side. So the permission is for glass and walls only. The mesh is treated completely differently, and many courts have mesh panels on the side walls next to the glass, so you need to know which part of your wall you are aiming at. FIP Rule 13g covers the other end of the shot. If you return the ball, either directly or off your own walls, and without bouncing it strikes the opponents' walls, their metal fence or anything not related to their court, you lose the point. An own-wall shot therefore has two jobs: get over the net, and land on the opponents' ground before it touches anything else on their side. FIP Rule 12 explains why you are often hitting off your own glass in the first place. Once the opponents' shot has bounced in your court, it may touch any part of the court (inside of the walls, the mesh including its frame, the ground, the net and the posts) and is still in play. You just have to return it before it bounces a second time. So letting the ball come off your back glass and then playing it is standard padel; hitting it back into your own glass on purpose is the extra step that Rule 14b allows. Allowed You hit the ball into your own back or side glass, it rebounds over the net and bounces in the opponents' court (Rule 14b). The ball off your own glass clips the net and still lands in their court (Rule 14e). Not allowed After your hit, the ball touches your own mesh or bounces on your own ground (Rule 13l). The ball comes off your glass, crosses the net and hits the opponents' glass or fence before bouncing (Rule 13g). Real match situations The emergency shot off the back glass A lob goes over your head, bounces near the back of your court and you are too late to play it normally. You turn and hit the ball hard into your own back glass so it flies back over the net. Players often call this a contrapared . Verdict: legal, provided it lands in the opponents' court first (Rule 14b). If it drops short and bounces on your side, the point is lost (Rule 13l). Your own side glass, before the ball crosses You are near the side wall and hit a ball into your own side glass, and it angles back across the net. This is the question many people ask: can a ball go off your own side wall before it crosses? Yes. Rule 14b talks about a wall of your own court without limiting it to the back wall, so side glass counts. Just check it is glass and not mesh: the same shot that touches a mesh panel on your side is a lost point under Rule 13l. Off your glass and straight into theirs You play a hard shot into your back glass, it rockets over the net and smacks into the opponents' back glass on the full. Verdict: point lost. Rule 13g explicitly includes returns played off your own walls. The ball has to bounce in the opponents' court before it reaches their walls or fence. The ball touches your mesh on the way Your partner plays the ball into your side wall, but it catches the edge of the mesh section above the glass before coming back over. Verdict: point lost under Rule 13l. It does not matter that the ball eventually reached the opponents' court; once your shot touches your own fence, the point is over. Your partner plays it off the glass Nothing in the rules limits own-wall shots to one player. Either partner can use the glass. What matters is that only one of you hits the ball: if both partners touch it, Rule 13o applies and the point is lost. The ball hits you after it comes off your own wall You hit the ball into your back glass and it rebounds into your own body or your partner's leg. Verdict: point lost. Rule 13j says that after a player hits the ball, it must not touch the player, the partner or anything they wear. Common misconceptions "Own-wall shots are only for beginners." They are a legal shot for everyone. Advanced players use them mostly as a last resort, but the rule is the same at every level. "You can only use the back wall, not the side wall." Rule 14b refers to a wall of your own court. Side glass is a wall. "The fence is part of the wall, so it is fine too." Not for your own shots. Rule 13l singles out the metal fence of your own side. Glass yes, mesh no. "If it goes over the net, it is good." It still needs to bounce in the opponents' court before touching their glass or mesh (Rule 13g). "Playing the ball after it comes off your back glass is an own-wall shot." Not quite. That is simply playing an opponent's ball after it has bounced and touched part of your court, which Rule 12 allows. An own-wall shot is when your hit goes into your wall. Is the serve different? Yes. Rule 14b is about returns during the rally. For the serve, Rule 6.5 requires the ball to go diagonally into the receiver's box and land within its lines, and the rulebook does not describe serving off your own wall. Serve directly into the box and save the glass for the rally. For everything that can go wrong with a serve, see padel service faults . Social games vs official matches With an umpire, the umpire decides whether the ball touched glass or mesh. In social games you call it yourselves, and the honest convention is that the player closest to the ball, or the pair who played it, calls their own shot. The tricky calls are almost always about whether an own-wall shot brushed the mesh panel or the top of the glass, so make the call quickly and in good faith. If nobody saw it clearly, many clubs replay the point; that is a convention, not something the FIP rulebook sets out. Some recreational players have their own house rules, for example banning own-wall shots for juniors. Agree anything like that before the match, because under the FIP rules the shot is legal. When to use your own wall Treat the own-wall shot as an escape. It buys you a return when you are beaten by a lob or caught too close to the glass, but it is hard to control and usually gives the opponents an easy ball at the net. A few practical points: Hit up and hard enough. The ball needs height and pace to clear the net from deep in your court. The most common failure is a ball that drops on your own side. Aim at the glass, not the corner seam. A ball that catches the joint between back and side glass can fly anywhere. Know your court. Before the match, look at where the glass ends and the mesh begins on each side wall. Prefer the normal play when you can. If you have time to let the ball come off the back glass and hit a proper return, that is almost always better. Our guide to playing off the back glass covers this, and the lob is often the best reply once you are back in position. You will also hear the Spanish word bajada on court. It describes attacking an overhead after the ball has come off your back glass. It is a tactic name, not a separate rule; the relevant rules are the same ones above. Frequently asked questions Can you hit the ball into your own side wall in padel? Yes, if the part you hit is glass (a wall) and the ball then bounces in the opponents' court (FIP Rule 14b). If it touches your own mesh, the point is lost (Rule 13l). Can the ball hit your own wall twice before crossing the net? Rule 14b speaks of the ball striking a wall of your own court and then bouncing in the opponents' court; the rulebook does not specifically address one wall versus two. The firm limits are that it must not touch your own mesh or ground (Rule 13l) and must bounce on their side before hitting their walls (Rule 13g). What happens if my own-wall shot hits the net and goes over? If it then lands in the opponents' court, it is a correct return. Rule 14e says a ball that touches the net or posts and lands in the opponents' court is good. Is hitting your own wall the same as a double hit? No. A double hit (Rule 13i) is touching the ball twice with the racket. Hitting the ball once into your own glass is a single hit and is allowed under Rule 14b. Can I play the ball after it hits my own fence from the opponents' shot? Yes. If the opponents' ball bounces in your court and then hits your mesh or glass, it is still in play (Rule 12). The mesh restriction in Rule 13l only applies to the shot you hit. For the complete picture of how walls, fence and scoring fit together, see the rules of padel or browse the rules hub . 
##### padel-ball-hits-the-fence
When the ball hits the metal fence (the mesh) in padel, it stays in play if it bounced in that court first, and the point is lost if it hit the mesh on the full or touched the hitter's own mesh (FIP Rules 12, 14c, 13g and 13l). On the serve, any contact with the mesh before the second bounce is a fault (Rule 7e). The mesh is the part of the court where glass and fence are treated differently, so it is worth knowing exactly how. The short answer After the ball bounces in the opponents' court, it can hit their mesh and the rally goes on. If your shot hits the opponents' mesh before bouncing, or touches your own mesh at any point, you lose the point. A serve that bounces in the box and then touches the mesh is a fault, even though the same serve off the glass would be good. What the rules say FIP Rule 12 lists the parts of the court: the inside of the walls, the metal mesh fence including its frame, the ground, the net and the posts. Once a ball has bounced on the opponents' ground it may touch any of these and is still in play. The opponents then have to return it before it bounces a second time. So the mesh is part of the court, just like the glass, and the frame holding it counts as fence. FIP Rule 14c confirms it from the hitter's side: if your ball bounces in the opponents' court and then hits their fence or wall, it is a correct return. FIP Rule 13g covers the ball on the full. If you return the ball (directly or off your own walls) and, without bouncing, it strikes the opponents' walls, metal fence or anything unrelated to their court, you lose the point. FIP Rule 13l deals with your own mesh. If you hit the ball and it touches the metal fence or ground of your own side, you lose the point. Your own glass is different: hitting it is allowed under Rule 14b. FIP Rule 7e is the serve rule. If a serve lands in the correct box and then touches the metal fence before its second bounce, it is a fault. If it hits the glass instead, it is a good serve. FIP Rule 15a covers the strange cases. If, after bouncing in the opponents' court, the ball goes through a hole in the mesh or gets stuck in it, the hitter wins the point. Point continues Your shot bounces in the opponents' court and then hits their mesh, the fence frame or the glass (Rules 12 and 14c). The opponents' shot bounces on your side, hits your mesh and you return it before the second bounce (Rule 12). Point lost or fault Your shot hits the opponents' mesh without bouncing (Rule 13g). Your shot touches your own mesh (Rule 13l). A serve bounces in the box and touches the mesh before the second bounce (Rule 7e). Real match situations The drive that runs into the side fence You hit a cross-court drive, it bounces in the opponents' court near the side wall and runs into the mesh panel. Verdict: still in play (Rule 14c). The opponents have to deal with it before it bounces again. Because mesh absorbs pace and deflects unpredictably, this is often a winning shot. The smash that hits the fence on the full You smash hard, the ball clears the net and strikes the opponents' back mesh before it bounces. Verdict: point lost (Rule 13g). It feels like a great shot, but the ball needed to land on their ground first. Your partner's volley clips your own fence Your partner volleys close to the side wall and the racket sends the ball into the mesh on your own side, from where it drops over the net. Verdict: point lost (Rule 13l), no matter where the ball ends up. The serve that kicks off the glass into the fence Your serve lands in the box, hits the side glass and then rolls into the mesh before its second bounce. Verdict: fault (Rule 7e). The glass touch was fine; the mesh contact is what makes it a fault. A net serve that lands in the box and then touches the mesh is also a fault rather than a replay, because Rule 9.1a only allows a repeat if the ball does not touch the metal fence before the second bounce. The ball gets stuck in the mesh Your lob bounces deep in the opponents' court and wedges itself in the wire. Verdict: point won by you (Rule 15a). The same applies if it squeezes through a hole in the mesh after the bounce. The ball lands on the fence frame After bouncing in the opponents' court, the ball hits one of the metal uprights or the frame around a mesh panel. Verdict: in play. Rule 12 treats the frame as part of the fence, so it is the same as hitting the mesh. Glass vs mesh at a glance Situation Glass Mesh Your shot hits your own side Allowed if it then bounces in their court (14b) Point lost (13l) Your shot hits their side on the full Point lost (13g) Point lost (13g) Your shot bounces in their court, then hits it In play (14c) In play (14c) Serve bounces in the box, then hits it Good serve Fault (7e) Ball stuck in it or through a hole after the bounce Not applicable Point to the hitter (15a) Common misconceptions "The fence is out." It is not. The mesh is part of the court under Rule 12. A ball that bounces first and then hits it is still live. "You can play the ball off your own fence like your own glass." No. Rule 13l makes your own mesh a lost point for your shot. "A serve off the fence is the same as off the glass." No. Rule 7e makes mesh contact on the serve a fault before the second bounce. "A ball stuck in the fence is a let." No. Rule 15a gives the point to the player who hit it, provided it bounced in the opponents' court first. "The metal posts of the fence are something different." The frame is part of the fence (Rule 12), so it is treated the same as the mesh. What about the gate? Many courts have openings in the side walls. If out-of-court play is not authorised and the ball passes through a gate after bouncing, the pair who had to return it loses the point (Rule 13d). When out-of-court play is authorised, a ball through the door is only lost if it bounces a second time or touches something unrelated to the court (Rule 13e). See out-of-court play for the full picture. Social games vs official matches Mesh calls are some of the easiest in padel, because the ball sounds and moves differently off wire than off glass. In social games the pair whose shot it was should call their own mesh touch straight away. The harder call is whether a shot bounced before it reached the opponents' fence, especially on deep balls into the corners. With an umpire, the umpire decides. Without one, the receiving pair, who had the best view of their own ground, normally makes the call. That is a club convention, not an FIP rule. Playing balls off the mesh The mesh kills pace and sends the ball off at odd angles, so the practical advice is simple. Do not wait for it. Unlike a ball off the back glass, a ball off the mesh rarely comes out where you expect. Where possible, play it before it reaches the fence. Give yourself space. Stand a little further from the fence than you would from the glass, so you can react to a dead drop. Use the fence when attacking. A smash or a víbora that bounces and then hits the side mesh is one of the hardest balls to return. Learn the glass first, then adapt to the mesh. Our guide to playing off the back glass covers the rebounds you can predict. Frequently asked questions Is the ball out if it hits the fence in padel? Only if it hits the fence on the full or touches the hitter's own fence. After a bounce in the opponents' court, the fence is part of the court and the ball stays in play (FIP Rules 12 and 14c). Can the serve hit the fence? No. If the serve bounces in the box and touches the metal fence before its second bounce, it is a fault (Rule 7e). The glass is fine. What if the ball gets stuck in the fence? If it bounced in the opponents' court first, the hitter wins the point (Rule 15a). Can I return a ball that bounced on my side and then hit my fence? Yes. The opponents' ball is still in play after touching your mesh, as long as you return it before its second bounce (Rule 12). Does the fence frame count as fence? Yes. Rule 12 lists the metal mesh fence including its frame as part of the court. For how points are scored once a rally ends, see padel scoring , or go back to the rules of padel . 
##### can-you-volley-the-serve-in-padel
No: in padel you cannot volley the serve. The receiver must let the ball bounce in the receiving box and then return it before it bounces a second time (FIP Rule 8.1). If the serve touches either receiving player or their racket before it bounces, the point goes to the server (Rule 8.5). Here is how the return of serve works, including who receives and what happens when you are not ready. The short answer The serve must bounce before you hit it. Volley it, or let it touch you, your partner or either of your rackets before the bounce, and the server wins the point. Let it bounce twice, and the server also wins the point. You may play the serve off the glass after it has bounced. What the rules say FIP Rule 8.1 is the core rule. The receiver must let the serve bounce in the receiving box and must hit it before it bounces a second time. That rules out volleying the serve completely, and it also means a serve that bounces twice before you get to it is a point to the server, effectively an ace (Rules 8.1 and 13c). FIP Rule 8.5 covers contact before the bounce. If the serve touches either receiving player or their racket before bouncing, the point goes to the server. That includes the receiver's partner, not just the receiver. The rule as written does not make an exception for a serve that looked as if it was heading long, so the safe habit is to get out of the way and let a bad serve land. FIP Rules 8.2 and 8.3 set the receiving order. In the first game of each set, the receiving pair decides which of them receives first, and that player receives the first serve of every game in that set. The partners then receive alternately, and the order stays fixed for the set or tie-break. Rule 8.4 adds that if the order is changed by mistake, you carry on until the end of that game or tie-break and then go back to the correct order. FIP Rules 9.2a and 6.10 deal with readiness. The server must wait until the receiver is ready, though the receiver has to adapt to the server's reasonable rhythm (Rule 6.10). If the serve is struck while the receiver is not ready, it is a let (Rule 9.2a), and no fault can be called on that serve. Once the receiver attempts a return, though, they can no longer claim they were not ready (Rule 6.11). A let on a first serve means the point is replayed with two serves (Rule 9.2b); a let on a second serve means only that second serve is repeated (Rule 9.2c). Allowed Letting the serve bounce in your box and hitting it straight away (Rule 8.1). Letting it bounce, come off the back or side glass, and then returning it before its second bounce (Rules 8.1 and 12). Not allowed Volleying the serve, or the serve touching either receiving player or their racket before it bounces: point to the server (Rules 8.1 and 8.5). Letting the serve bounce twice: point to the server (Rules 8.1 and 13c). Real match situations The fast serve at your body The server hits a quick, low serve straight at you. You instinctively stick out your racket and block it before it bounces. Verdict: point to the server (Rule 8.5). Step aside or back and let the ball land first. The serve that is clearly going long The serve is flying towards the back of your box and looks long. You reach up and catch it, or it clips your shoulder. Verdict: under Rule 8.5 as written, a serve that touches either receiving player before bouncing is a point to the server. Do not touch a serve before it bounces, even if you think it is out. Let it land and call the fault. Playing the serve off the back glass The serve bounces deep in your box and you let it run onto the back glass, then hit it as it comes out. Verdict: perfectly legal. After the bounce, Rule 12 allows the ball to touch any part of the court; you just need to hit it before the second bounce. For many receivers this is the safest return against a deep serve. Our guide to the serve and return covers the technique. The serve that dies in the corner The serve skids off the side glass and bounces a second time before you can get your racket to it. Verdict: point to the server (Rules 8.1 and 13c). The wrong partner receives In the first game of the set, your partner received on the right. In game three, you both drift to the wrong sides and you receive from the right by mistake. Verdict: Rule 8.4 says you finish that game as you are and then go back to the correct order. Points already played stand. You are not ready You are still adjusting your grip and have not looked up when the server strikes the ball. If you do not try to play it, the serve is a let (Rule 9.2a). If you try to return it, you can no longer claim you were not ready (Rule 6.11). Signal clearly instead, for example by raising your hand, and the server must wait (Rule 6.10). Common misconceptions "You can volley the serve if it is a good serve." Never. Rule 8.1 requires the bounce every time. "If it was going out, it is a fault even if it hit me." Rule 8.5 gives the point to the server when the serve touches either receiving player before bouncing; it does not make an exception for serves that looked long. "The receiver's partner can take the serve if it comes near them." No. If the serve touches either receiving player or their racket before it bounces, it is the server's point (Rule 8.5). After the bounce, Rule 8.1 makes the receiver the player who returns it, and the receiving order in Rules 8.2 and 8.3 fixes who that is. The rulebook does not set out an explicit penalty for the partner playing it instead; at clubs it is treated as a point to the server, but that is convention, not an FIP rule. "You must return the serve before it reaches the glass." No. Once it has bounced, glass contact is fine (Rule 12). "The receiver has to stand inside the receiving box." No. Rule 3 lets the receiver, and both partners, stand anywhere on their own side of the court. "Diagonally opposite" only decides which opponent receives the serve; it is not a position rule. Where to stand to receive Rule 3 lets the receiver stand anywhere on their side of the court; it does not fix a spot. In practice most receivers stand a metre or two behind the service line, towards the side glass of their box, so they can step in to a short serve or let a deep one come off the glass. Standing too close makes the Rule 8.5 mistake more likely, because a fast serve reaches you before it has bounced. For more on where both players stand, see positioning in doubles . Social games vs official matches In official matches the umpire judges whether the serve bounced before touching the receiver. In social games, the receiver should own the call: if a serve hit you on the full, it is the server's point. Readiness is where social games get messy. Agree a simple habit before you start: the server waits until the receiver is in position and looking, and the receiver does not delay beyond a reasonable rhythm. Remember that play should be continuous, with no more than 20 seconds between points (Rule 2). Returning well, legally Back up for the fast serve. More distance gives the ball time to bounce and you time to read it. Use the glass. A deep serve off the back glass is easier to control than one you rush to hit early. Aim low or lob. A low return at the server's feet or a lob over the net player are the two standard replies. Frequently asked questions Does the serve have to bounce in padel? Yes, twice over: the server bounces the ball before striking it (Rule 6.2), and the receiver must let the serve bounce in the box before hitting it (Rule 8.1). What happens if the serve hits the receiver? If it touches either receiving player or their racket before bouncing, the point goes to the server (Rule 8.5). Can you return the serve off the glass? Yes. After the serve has bounced in your box, it may touch the glass and you return it before the second bounce (Rules 8.1 and 12). If it touches the metal fence, the serve is a fault (Rule 7e), so you would not need to play it. Who receives first in padel? The receiving pair decides in the first game of each set, and that player receives the first serve of every game in the set (Rule 8.2). Partners then alternate (Rule 8.3). Is a serve that bounces twice an ace? Effectively, yes. The receiver must hit it before the second bounce, so the server wins the point (Rules 8.1 and 13c). For the full list of serve rules, see padel service faults and the waist-height rule . 
##### padel-serve-rules-waist-height
No: you cannot serve overarm in padel. At the moment you strike the ball it must be at or below your waist, and at least one of your feet must be touching the ground (FIP Rule 6.4). The ball must also be bounced in your own service area first (Rule 6.2), which is why every padel serve is an underarm or sidearm stroke. The short answer Bounce the ball inside your service box, with at least one foot behind the service line, then hit it with the ball at or below your waist and at least one foot on the ground. Overarm serves, contact above the waist and jump serves are all faults under Rule 7a. What the rules say The serve is covered by FIP Rule 6. Here are the parts that decide how you serve: Rule 6.1, where you stand. The server stands with at least one foot behind the service line, inside their service box (between the extension of the central line and the side wall), and stays there until the ball has been served. Rule 6.2, the bounce. Before striking it, you bounce the ball on the ground inside your own box, behind the service line. The ball may not cross the service line or the imaginary central line before it is struck; if it does, that is a fault under Rule 7a. Rule 6.3, the lines. You may not touch the service line or the imaginary central line with either foot. Rule 6.4, height and feet. At contact, the ball must be at or below waist height, and at least one foot must be in contact with the ground. Rule 6.5, the target. The serve goes diagonally into the receiver's box and must land within its lines. You start each game from the right side and alternate boxes. Rule 6.6, what counts as a serve. Any contact with the ball, or an intentional attempt to hit it, counts as a serve. Breaking any of Rules 6.1 to 6.6 is a service fault under Rule 7a . Swinging and missing is a fault under Rule 7b . Two faults in a row lose the point (Rule 13q). Good serve Ball bounced behind the service line in your box, struck at or below waist height with one foot on the ground, landing in the receiver's box (Rules 6.2, 6.4, 6.5). Underarm or sidearm, flat or with spin. Fault Contact above the waist, overarm serving or jumping so both feet leave the ground (Rules 6.4 and 7a). Stepping on the service line or central line (Rules 6.3 and 7a). Swinging and missing (Rule 7b). How to judge waist height The FIP rulebook sets the limit at waist height but does not define the waist in centimetres or by a body landmark. In practice, players and umpires judge it against the server's own body, so a tall player can make contact a little higher than a short player. The common club reading is that the waist sits roughly between the belly button and the top of the hip bone. That is convention, not an FIP definition. A few practical checks help: Your elbow is a rough guide. If your hitting arm is bent upwards at contact, you are probably above the waist. Bending the knees lowers your waist, too. Crouching does not let you hit higher; the waist moves down with you. Do not let the ball bounce too high. Many accidental faults happen because the ball is bounced hard and struck on the way up, above the waist. Drop it softly and let it fall back towards hip height. Real match situations The tennis player's serve A new player throws the ball up and serves overarm, tennis style. Verdict: fault (Rules 6.2, 6.4 and 7a). There was no bounce and the contact was far above the waist. The high-contact sidearm serve You bounce the ball well, then hit it at chest height with a flat, sidearm swing. Verdict: fault (Rules 6.4 and 7a). The swing shape is fine; the contact height is not. The jump serve You hop forward as you swing and both feet are off the ground at contact. Verdict: fault (Rules 6.4 and 7a). One foot has to be on the ground when the racket meets the ball. Lifting your back heel or swinging your back leg through is fine as long as the other foot stays down. Stepping on the line As you serve, your front toe lands on the service line. Verdict: fault (Rules 6.3 and 7a). The same applies to touching the imaginary extension of the central line. The fluffed bounce You drop the ball, it bounces badly off your foot, and you catch it without swinging. Rule 6.6 says a serve happens when you make contact with the ball or intentionally attempt to hit it. Catching it without a swing is neither, so the rulebook does not treat it as a serve. Just reset and bounce again, keeping within the 20-second limit between points (Rule 2). The air shot You bounce the ball, swing and miss it completely. Verdict: fault (Rules 6.6 and 7b). An intentional attempt counts as a serve even without contact. Common misconceptions "The serve must be underarm." The rule is about height, not swing shape. A sidearm serve is fine as long as contact is at or below the waist (Rule 6.4). "You can serve without bouncing the ball if you hit it below the waist." No. Rule 6.2 requires the bounce in your own box first. "You have to bounce it exactly once." The rulebook requires a bounce before contact but does not specify a number of bounces. Keep it brisk and within the 20-second limit (Rule 2). "Spin serves are illegal." The serve rules govern height, feet, the bounce and where the ball lands. Nothing in them restricts spin or pace. "You can walk in as you serve." Rule 6.1 says you stay in position until the serve is struck. The rulebook does not cover movement in more detail than Rules 6.1 to 6.4, so keep your feet within the box and off the lines until contact. Social games vs official matches With an umpire, the umpire judges the waist and the footwork. In social padel, the receiving pair usually calls an obviously high serve, but it is one of the most argued calls in the game because the waist is not a fixed line. Two habits keep it friendly: agree before you start that clear overhead serves are faults and borderline ones are given to the server, and if a regular partner keeps serving high, tell them between games rather than mid-point. Those are conventions; the FIP rule is simply at or below the waist. A legal serve that works Because you cannot hit down on the ball, the padel serve is about placement and depth, not power. Most good servers aim deep into the receiver's box so the ball runs onto the glass, or wide at the side glass so the receiver is pulled out of position. Stand side-on inside your service box, with at least one foot behind the service line and clear of the central line. Drop the ball softly slightly in front of you and to your racket side. Let it fall back to around hip height after the bounce. Swing low to high with a continental grip, keeping your front foot on the ground. Move forward to the net after the serve to take your volley position. The rest of the serve and return is covered in our serve and return guide , and you will find serving games in the drills section. Frequently asked questions Can you serve overhead in padel? No. The ball must be at or below waist height at contact (FIP Rule 6.4). An overhead serve is a fault under Rule 7a. Do you have to bounce the ball before serving in padel? Yes. Rule 6.2 says the ball is bounced on the ground inside your own box, behind the service line, before you strike it. Can you jump when serving in padel? No. At least one foot must be in contact with the ground at contact (Rule 6.4). Is a sidearm serve legal in padel? Yes, as long as contact is at or below the waist and the other serve rules are met. What is the penalty for a high serve? It is a fault (Rule 7a). If it was your second serve, it is a double fault and you lose the point (Rule 13q). For every other way a serve can go wrong, read padel service faults , or start from the rules of padel . 
##### padel-service-faults
A padel serve is a fault if it breaks the serving rules (position, bounce, foot or height), if you miss the ball, if it lands outside the receiver's box, if it hits you or your partner, if it touches the metal fence before the second bounce, or if it leaves through a gate on a court without a safety zone (FIP Rules 7a to 7f). Two faults in a row lose the point (Rule 13q). Here is each one, with the situations that cause most arguments. The short answer There are six types of service fault in the FIP rules, listed as Rule 7a to 7f. Lines are good, glass after the bounce is good, and a net serve that lands in the box is replayed. Mesh after the bounce, a serve that hits your partner, and a serve through a gate on a court without a safety zone are all faults. What the rules say FIP Rule 7 lists the service faults. Here they are in plain English. Rule Fault What it means 7a Breaking Rules 6.1 to 6.6 Wrong position, no bounce, foot on the service or central line, contact above the waist or both feet off the ground, wrong box 7b Missing the ball A swing intended to hit the ball counts as a serve, so an air shot is a fault 7c Landing outside the box The ball must land in the receiver's box; balls on the lines are good 7d Hitting the server's pair The serve touches the server, the partner or their equipment 7e Touching the mesh The ball lands in the box and touches the metal fence before its second bounce; glass is fine 7f Through the gate On a court without a safety zone, the ball lands in the box and leaves through a gate Two consecutive service faults cost the point ( Rule 13q ). That is the padel double fault. 7a in detail: the serving technique Rule 7a covers any breach of Rules 6.1 to 6.6. You must stand with at least one foot behind the service line, inside your service box, and stay there until the ball has been served (6.1), bounce the ball in your own box behind the service line without it crossing the service line or the imaginary central line before you strike it (6.2), keep both feet off the service line and the imaginary central line (6.3), and strike the ball at or below waist height with at least one foot on the ground (6.4). The serve goes diagonally into the receiver's box (6.5). The height rule causes the most debate; see the waist-height rule for how to judge it. 7c and the lines The receiving box is bounded by the net, the service line, the central line and the side wall; its lines are good, so a ball touching any of them is in. A ball landing beyond the service line, in the wrong box, or in the server's own court is a fault. 7e: glass vs mesh After a good bounce in the box, the serve may hit the glass and stays in play. If it touches the metal fence before its second bounce, it is a fault. This is the key difference between serves and rallies: in a rally, a ball that bounces and then hits the mesh is in play (Rule 12), but on the serve it is a fault. More on that in when the ball hits the fence . 7f: the gate rule Some courts have openings in the side walls. On a court without a safety zone around it, a serve that lands in the box and then leaves through a gate is a fault. Good serve Lands on the line of the receiver's box (Rule 7c). Bounces in the box and then hits the side or back glass. Touches the net and lands in the box without touching the mesh: replayed, not a fault (Rule 9.1a). Fault Bounces in the box and touches the mesh before the second bounce (Rule 7e). Hits your partner at the net (Rule 7d). Swung at and missed (Rule 7b). Lands beyond the service line (Rule 7c). Real match situations The serve that clips the line Your serve lands with part of the ball on the service line. Verdict: good serve (Rule 7c). The lines belong to the box. The serve that hits your partner Your partner is standing at the net and your serve hits them in the back. Verdict: fault (Rule 7d), not a point to the receivers. Partners at the net should stand a little wide of the line of the serve. Net, then the fence The serve clips the net, lands in the box and then runs into the side mesh. Verdict: fault. Rule 9.1a only allows a replay if the serve does not touch the metal fence before its second bounce, so the mesh contact turns it into a fault under Rule 7e. Net, then your partner The serve clips the net tape and drops onto your partner standing close to it. Rule 9.1b says that is a net: the serve is repeated, not faulted. Compare this with the previous scenario: a serve that hits your partner without touching the net first is a fault under 7d. Wrong side You serve your first serve from the left when you should have been on the right, and it is a fault. Nobody notices until the next point. Rule 6.7 says the mistake is corrected when it is noticed, points already played stand, and a first-serve fault already made still counts. The second-serve fault Your first serve hits the mesh after the bounce and your second lands beyond the service line. Verdict: double fault, point to the receivers (Rule 13q). Common misconceptions "A net serve is a fault." Only if it then lands outside the box or touches the mesh. If it lands in the box, it is replayed (Rule 9.1a). "A serve onto the line is out." Lines are good (Rule 7c). "A serve that hits the receiver's partner is a fault." Rule 7d is about the server and the server's partner. A serve that hits either receiving player before it bounces is a point to the server (Rule 8.5). "The glass is out on the serve." No. After the bounce, the glass is fine. Only the mesh makes it a fault (Rule 7e). "You get a third serve after a let." A let on the first serve replays the whole point with two serves (Rule 9.2b). A let on the second serve repeats only the second serve (Rule 9.2c). For more, see let and net serve rules . Social games vs official matches With an umpire, every fault is called by the officials. In social games, the receiver calls whether the serve landed in or out and whether it touched the mesh, because the ball is on their side. The server calls their own foot faults and partner hits, which are usually obvious. Waist-height faults are the grey area; many groups agree before the match to call only clear overhead serves. That is a convention, not an FIP rule, so check it with your opponents before you start. Reducing your own faults Aim for the middle of the box on second serves. Depth is a bonus; a double fault is a free point. Avoid the mesh. On courts with mesh panels near the service area, aim for the glass side of the box. Same routine every time. A consistent bounce and stance removes most foot and height faults. Tell your partner where you are serving. It helps them avoid the line of the ball and the Rule 7d fault. Practise serving to targets in the drills section, and read the serve and return guide for technique. Frequently asked questions How many serves do you get in padel? Two. Two consecutive faults lose the point (Rule 13q). Net serves that land in the box and lets are replayed and do not count as faults. Is the line in on a padel serve? Yes. A ball touching the lines of the receiver's box is good (Rule 7c). Can the serve hit the glass? Yes, after bouncing in the correct box. Only the metal fence makes it a fault (Rule 7e). What happens if my serve hits my partner? It is a fault (Rule 7d). If it touched the net first and then hit a player, it is a net and is repeated (Rule 9.1b). Is missing the ball on the serve a fault? Yes. Any intentional attempt to hit counts as a serve (Rule 6.6), so a miss is a fault (Rule 7b). For the scoring side of a double fault, see padel scoring , or browse the rules hub . 
##### padel-let-and-net-serve-rules
There is no limit: a serve that clips the net or posts and then lands correctly in the receiver's box is simply replayed, as often as it happens, as long as it doesn't touch the metal fence before its second bounce. Lets on serve are replayed too, for reasons the rulebook lists. What matters is knowing exactly when a net serve is a replay, when it turns into a fault, and when a whole point is replayed instead of a single serve. The short answer A serve that touches the net or posts and then lands in the correct box is a “net” and is repeated, with no limit on how many times (FIP Rule 9.1a). If that same serve then hits the metal fence before its second bounce, it is a fault, not a net. A net on the first serve repeats the first serve; a net on the second serve repeats only the second serve (FIP Rule 9.2c). What the rules say The FIP rulebook separates two ideas that club players often mix up: the net on a serve (Rule 9) and the let , where a serve or a whole point is replayed because something interrupted play (Rules 9.2 and 10). The net serve (Rule 9.1) FIP Rule 9.1a: if the serve touches the net or the posts and then lands in the correct service box, the serve is repeated. The condition is that the ball must not touch the metal fence (the mesh) before its second bounce. FIP Rule 9.1b: it is also a net if, after touching the net or posts, the ball hits a player or their equipment. The serve is repeated. The rulebook mentions no limit on the number of net serves. You can clip the net five times in a row and you still have the serve you were on. The let on serve (Rule 9.2) FIP Rule 9.2a: a let is called if the receiver was not ready. This ties in with Rule 6.10, which says the server must wait until the receiver is ready, while the receiver has to adapt to the server's reasonable rhythm. FIP Rule 9.2b: a let on the first serve means the whole point is replayed, so the server gets two serves again. FIP Rule 9.2c: a net or a let on the second serve means only the second serve is repeated. A fault already made on the first serve still counts. Let points during a rally (Rule 10) A let point means the point is replayed from the start. FIP Rule 10 lists when that happens: 10a: the ball splits. 10b: a foreign object enters the court, such as a ball from the next court. 10c: play is interrupted by unexpected circumstances that have nothing to do with the players. 10d: the ball hits an object on the opponents' side (for example another ball) and that object moves in a way that could interfere with play or put someone in danger. 10e: the player must tell the umpire immediately. If play carries on, the right to a let is lost. 10f: if the let request is not valid, the player who asked for it loses the point. Rule 11 adds one more source of lets: an involuntary hindrance of an opponent is a let, while a deliberate one costs the point. A second involuntary hindrance by the same pair costs them the point. Our guide to hindrance and interference covers that in detail. Replay the serve The serve clips the net tape or a post, lands in the correct box and then bounces again, or hits the back or side glass, or strikes a player. That is a net under Rule 9.1, and the serve is taken again with no limit. Fault The serve clips the net and lands outside the box (Rule 7c), or clips the net, lands in the box and touches the metal fence before its second bounce (Rules 9.1a and 7e). That serve is a fault, not a net. Real match situations 1. Net cord, then the side glass Your first serve trickles over the net tape, lands just inside the receiver's box and runs into the side glass. Glass is fine on a serve, so this is a net. Replay the first serve (FIP Rule 9.1a). 2. Net cord, then the mesh Same serve, but you are serving from a position where the ball bounces in the box and then kicks into the metal fence near the corner before bouncing a second time. That is a fault. The net exception only applies if the ball avoids the fence before its second bounce (FIP Rules 9.1a and 7e). If it was your second serve, it is a double fault and you lose the point (FIP Rule 13q). 3. Net cord, then the receiver's body The serve clips the net and hits the receiver on the full before it bounces. Without the net touch, Rule 8.5 would give the point to the server. Because the ball touched the net first, Rule 9.1b treats it as a net and the serve is repeated. 4. Three nets in a row on a second serve You fault your first serve, then clip the net three times on your second serve, each time landing in the box. Each one is a net. You keep repeating the second serve only; your first-serve fault still stands (FIP Rule 9.2c). The fourth serve decides it. 5. The receiver wasn't ready You serve while the receiver is still turning round after collecting a ball. The receiver doesn't play at it and signals they weren't ready. That is a let (FIP Rule 9.2a). If it happened on your first serve, you replay the point with two serves (9.2b). If it was your second serve, you replay just the second serve (9.2c). If the receiver attempts a return, they can no longer claim they were not ready (Rule 6.11); and Rule 6.10 expects the receiver to adapt to the server's reasonable rhythm. Rule 6.11 also means a fault can't be called on a serve struck while the receiver wasn't ready. 6. A ball rolls in from the next court mid-rally Halfway through a rally, a ball from the neighbouring court rolls across your service box. Stop and call it straight away: that is a let point under FIP Rule 10b and the whole point is replayed, including two serves. If you keep playing and only complain after you lose the rally, you have lost the right to the let (FIP Rule 10e). Common misconceptions “You only get one or two net serves.” No such limit exists in the FIP rulebook. Every net serve that lands in the correct box without touching the fence is repeated. “A net serve that hits the fence is still a let.” It isn't. Touching the metal fence before the second bounce makes it a fault (Rules 9.1a and 7e). Hitting the glass is fine. “A net on the second serve gives you two new serves.” Only the second serve is repeated (Rule 9.2c). Two new serves come only with a let on the first serve (9.2b). “You can claim a let after the point.” You must call it immediately. If play continues, the right is lost (Rule 10e), and an invalid let request costs you the point (Rule 10f). “A net serve that lands in the wrong box is replayed.” It must land in the correct box to be a net. Outside the box it is a fault (Rule 7c), and remember the lines are good. Social games vs official matches In an official match the umpire calls nets and lets. In most club games there is no umpire, so the players call them. A few conventions keep things friendly: The receiving pair usually calls the net , because they are watching the ball come over. The server can't always see a light touch on the tape. Call it at once. The FIP rulebook doesn't spell out what happens if the receiver plays a net serve and the point continues. The sensible club convention is to stop and replay the serve as soon as anyone notices, rather than finishing the rally and arguing afterwards. Agree beforehand how you handle balls from other courts. At busy clubs, stray balls are common. Under Rule 10b they are a let, and calling it immediately (10e) is both the rule and the safest habit. Be generous with doubt. If nobody is sure whether the serve touched the net, most club players simply replay it. Practical tips A net serve that keeps landing in the box is not bad luck forever; it usually means your trajectory is too flat. The serve in padel is hit at or below waist height (Rule 6.4), so a slightly higher arc over the net gives you more margin without losing depth. For more on that, see our guide to the serve and return . Watch the corner on net cords. A ball that dies off the tape often lands short and wide, close to where the side glass gives way to the mesh. If you aim your serve into the centre of the box rather than tight to the side, a net cord is far less likely to drift into the fence and become a fault. Our page on service faults lists every other way a serve can go wrong. Frequently asked questions Is there a limit on net serves in padel? No. The FIP rulebook mentions no limit. Each net serve that lands in the correct box without touching the metal fence before its second bounce is replayed (FIP Rule 9.1a). What is the difference between a net and a let in padel? A net is a serve that touches the net or posts and then lands correctly (Rule 9.1). A let is a replay for another reason, such as the receiver not being ready (9.2a) or a ball entering from another court (10b). Does a let on the second serve give me two serves? No. A net or let on the second serve repeats only the second serve (FIP Rule 9.2c). A let on the first serve replays the whole point (9.2b). Is a net serve that hits the glass a fault? No. After landing in the box the serve may hit the glass. Only the metal fence before the second bounce turns it into a fault (Rules 9.1a and 7e). What if a net serve hits my partner? Rule 9.1b says that if the ball touches the net or posts and then hits a player or their equipment, it is a net and the serve is repeated. For the full picture, our rules of padel guide covers scoring, serving and every way a point is won or lost. 
##### can-you-touch-the-net-in-padel
No: if you, your racket or anything you wear or carry touches the net, the posts or the tension cable while the ball is in play, your pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13a). The same applies to touching any part of the opponents' court. The key word is “while the ball is in play”: once the point has been decided, the net is just a net again. The short answer Touching the net, posts or tension cable with your body, racket, clothing or anything you carry loses the point, as long as the ball is still in play (FIP Rule 13a). Jumping over the net during a point also loses it (13n). After the point is over, touching the net doesn't matter. What the rules say Three rules cover the net directly. FIP Rule 13a: a pair loses the point if a player, their racket, or anything they wear or carry touches the net, the posts, the tension cable or any part of the opponents' court while the ball is in play. FIP Rule 13b: an exception for courts where out-of-court play is authorised. There, the vertical post dividing the door openings is a neutral zone above 0.92 m, and any player may touch or hold it. FIP Rule 13n: a pair loses the point if any player jumps over the net during the point. To apply 13a you need to know when the ball is “in play”. FIP Rule 12 says the ball is in play from a valid serve until a let is called or the point is decided. That means the ball is still in play after your winning shot bounces, until it has bounced twice, gone out, or the point has been settled some other way. Notice what is not on the list. The ball touching the net is not a problem in itself: a return that touches the net or posts and lands in the opponents' court is a correct return (FIP Rule 14e). The rule is about players and their equipment, not the ball. Rule 13a also mentions “any part of the opponents' court”. Rule 12 defines the parts of the court as the ground, the inside of the walls, the metal mesh fence including its frame, the net and the posts. So reaching across and touching the opponents' ground or glass with your racket while the ball is live costs you the point in the same way. Allowed Touching the net after the point has been decided; the ball itself touching the net; standing very close to the net without contact; on authorised out-of-court courts, holding the dividing door post above 0.92 m (Rule 13b). Not allowed Touching the net, posts or tension cable with your body, racket, cap, sleeve or anything you carry while the ball is in play (Rule 13a); touching the opponents' court; jumping over the net during the point (Rule 13n). Real match situations 1. Winning volley, then you brush the net You punch a volley at the net. It bounces in the opponents' court and is clearly unreachable, but before it bounces a second time your momentum carries your racket into the net tape. The ball was still in play, so you lose the point (FIP Rule 13a). This is the most common net-touch in club padel. 2. You touch the net after the ball has bounced twice Same volley, but you only stumble into the net after the ball has bounced twice on the other side. The point was already yours (Rule 13c), so the ball is no longer in play and the touch doesn't count. 3. Your cap falls into the net You lunge for a drop shot and your cap flies off and lands against the net while the rally continues. Rule 13a includes anything a player wears or carries, so your pair loses the point. The same applies to a towel, a spare ball falling out of a pocket, or a loose sweatband. 4. Your follow-through hits the post You hit a wide ball near the side of the court and your racket clips the net post on the follow-through. Posts are named in Rule 13a, so if the ball is still in play, you lose the point. 5. The opponent touches the net first Your opponent at the net touches the tape with their racket while reaching for your lob, then you smash the next ball into the net. The point ended the moment they touched the net, because the ball was in play (Rule 13a). It's your point, and the later smash doesn't matter. 6. Jumping the net to chase a ball A ball bounces on your side and spins back over the net. Your instinct is to hurdle the net to chase it. Don't: jumping over the net during the point loses it (FIP Rule 13n). Rule 14g does allow you to reach over and play a ball that has spun back, but only without touching the net, posts or the opponents' court. Our guide on hitting the ball on the other side of the net explains that rule. Common misconceptions “It only counts if your racket touches the net.” Your body, clothing and anything you carry count too (Rule 13a). “Once I've hit a winner, I can touch the net.” Not until the point is decided. The ball stays in play until it has bounced twice or the point ends another way (Rule 12). “Touching the net post is fine, it's only the net that counts.” The posts and the tension cable are both named in Rule 13a. “If the touch didn't affect the shot, play on.” The rule doesn't depend on whether the touch made a difference. Contact while the ball is in play loses the point. “The ball can't touch the net during a rally.” It can. A return that touches the net and lands in the opponents' court is good (Rule 14e). Only on the serve does a net touch mean a replay (Rule 9.1). Social games vs official matches With an umpire, net touches are called by the umpire. In social games, the convention is that the player who touched the net calls it on themselves. They are usually the only one who felt a light brush on the tape, and calling your own touch is one of the unwritten courtesies of club padel. Two things are worth agreeing before you play. First, whether the door-post exception in Rule 13b applies at all: it only exists where out-of-court play is authorised, and most clubs don't authorise it. Second, how you treat genuine doubt. If nobody saw clearly whether a net touch happened before or after the second bounce, most club players replay the point. That is a convention, not an FIP rule, but it keeps the game moving. How to stop touching the net Volley from a step back. Standing pinned against the net leaves no room for your follow-through. A position roughly two metres from the net lets you cover more balls and finish your swing safely. Our volley guide covers the set-up. Shorten the follow-through on put-aways. A compact punch volley needs very little finish. Long, flowing swings at the net are how rackets end up in the tape. Brake before the net. On drop shots and short balls, plan your last step so your weight stops before the net, not into it. Secure loose items. Keep spare balls out of your pockets or in a pocket that closes, and avoid loose caps if you play a lot at the net. Frequently asked questions Is touching the net a fault in padel? It is not called a fault, but the effect is the same: your pair loses the point if any player touches the net, posts or tension cable while the ball is in play (FIP Rule 13a). Can you touch the net after the point is over? Yes. Rule 13a only applies while the ball is in play. Once the point has been decided, touching the net has no consequence. What if my clothing touches the net? That counts. Rule 13a covers anything a player wears or carries, so a sleeve, cap or towel touching the net while the ball is in play loses the point. Can you jump over the net in padel? Not during a point. Jumping over the net while the point is being played loses it (FIP Rule 13n). Can I hold the post between the doors? Only on courts where out-of-court play is authorised. There, the vertical post dividing the door openings is a neutral zone above 0.92 m (FIP Rule 13b). Otherwise the normal rules apply. For every other way to lose a point, see our full rules of padel . 
##### can-you-reach-over-the-net-in-padel
Usually not: if you hit the ball before it has crossed the net to your side, you lose the point (FIP Rule 13f). There is one important exception. If your shot bounces correctly in the opponents' court and comes back over the net (usually through backspin or wind), they may reach over and play it on your side, as long as they don't touch the net, posts or your court (FIP Rule 14g). The short answer You must wait for the ball to cross the net before you hit it; striking it on the opponents' side first loses the point (FIP Rule 13f). The exception is a ball that has bounced on your side and spun back over the net: you may reach over and play it, provided you don't touch the net, the posts or the opponents' court (Rules 14g and 13a). What the rules say Four rules work together here. FIP Rule 13f: a pair loses the point if a player hits the ball before it has crossed the net. The ball must come to you; you can't go and meet it on the other side. FIP Rule 14g: if a ball bounces correctly in a pair's court and then goes back over the net to the hitter's side (typically through backspin or wind), the receiving pair may play it on the other side. That is only valid if they don't touch the net, the posts or the other court with their body, clothing or racket, and the return otherwise meets the normal conditions of a correct return (14a–f). FIP Rule 13a: touching the net, posts, tension cable or any part of the opponents' court while the ball is in play loses the point, whatever the reason. FIP Rule 13n: jumping over the net during the point loses the point. It is worth being clear about what the FIP rulebook does not address. There is no separate rule about reaching over the net in general, and nothing specific about a follow-through that carries your racket across the net plane.
