What the rulebook does cover is where the ball is when you hit it (13f) and whether you touch the net or the other court (13a). Those two tests answer almost every real situation. Point continues You hit the ball after it has crossed to your side, even if your racket finishes over the net, as long as nothing touches the net, posts or the other court. You reach over to play a ball that bounced on your side and spun back, without touching anything (Rule 14g). Point lost You meet the ball before it crosses the net (Rule 13f). Your racket or body touches the net, posts or the opponents' court while reaching (Rule 13a). You jump over the net to chase the ball (Rule 13n). Real match situations 1. Poaching a weak volley above the net Your opponent pops up a soft volley right on top of the net. You lean in and hit it while it is still on their side. That is a lost point (FIP Rule 13f), even if your shot is unreturnable. Wait half a second for it to cross, then put it away. 2. Your follow-through crosses the net You hit a high ball on your own side of the net and, on the follow-through, your racket passes over the net without touching it. The rulebook doesn't specifically cover follow-throughs. The ball was struck on your side, so 13f isn't broken, and nothing touched the net or the other court, so 13a isn't broken either. Play continues. If your racket clips the tape, though, you lose the point under 13a. 3. A drop shot with heavy backspin comes back Your opponents play a drop shot that bounces on your side and, with backspin, drifts back over the net to their side. You may reach over and play it there (FIP Rule 14g), as long as your body, clothing and racket stay off the net, the posts and their court. Your shot then has to be a correct return in the usual way. 4. Reaching over and touching their ground Same situation, but to reach the ball you plant your racket on the ground on their side for balance. That is touching the opponents' court while the ball is in play, so you lose the point (FIP Rule 13a). Rule 14g is explicit that the exception only applies without that contact. 5. A net cord that drops on your side Your opponent's return clips the net and dribbles over, landing a few centimetres from the net on your side. That is a correct return (Rule 14e). You can play it, but carefully: the ball is right by the net, so a lunge that brushes the tape loses the point (13a). 6. Hurdling the net for a spinning ball A ball spins back hard and lands well inside the opponents' court. You can't reach it from your side, so you jump over. That is a lost point under Rule 13n, however athletic it looks. Common misconceptions “Your racket can never cross the net.” The rulebook has no such rule. The tests are where you hit the ball (13f) and whether you touch the net or the other court (13a). “You can hit the ball as soon as it's above the net.” The ball has to have crossed the net (13f). A ball sitting on top of the tape on their side is not yet yours. “A ball that spins back over the net is automatically a winner.” Not if the receiving pair reaches over and plays it legally under Rule 14g. “You can run round the net post to chase a ball.” The FIP rulebook doesn't address a ball crossing outside the posts, and going round the net usually means leaving the court. At clubs where out-of-court play isn't authorised, being outside the court when you hit loses the point (Rule 13p). Our guide to out-of-court play explains when that changes. Social games vs official matches In official matches the umpire decides whether the ball had crossed the net when you hit it. That is a judgement call, often made in a fraction of a second. In social games, the convention is that the player who hit the ball is honest about it, and that genuine doubt goes in favour of the hitter or leads to a replay, as you prefer. Agree on that before you start. The rule on balls that come back over the net (14g) also catches many club players by surprise. Before a match against new opponents, it is worth a quick check that everyone knows a spinning ball can be played over the net. It prevents arguments later. Tactical tips Be patient at the net. When a ball floats up on the tape, let it come to you. The extra moment rarely costs you the winner, and it never costs you the point under 13f. Use the spin-back ball, but stay clean. If a drop shot is coming back over, a gentle reach and tap is enough. Big swings near the net are how players touch it. Know your distance. Positioning a step or two back from the net, rather than right on it, gives you room to let balls cross and still attack them. Our pages on the volley and positioning in doubles go into detail. Frequently asked questions Can your racket go over the net in padel? The FIP rulebook doesn't ban it as such. What loses the point is hitting the ball before it crosses (Rule 13f) or touching the net, posts or opponents' court (Rule 13a). What happens if the ball spins back over the net? The receiving pair may play it on the other side, as long as they don't touch the net, posts or the other court and their return is otherwise correct (FIP Rule 14g). If they can't reach it, they lose the point. Can I hit the ball on top of the net? Only once it has crossed to your side. Hitting it before it has crossed the net loses the point (FIP Rule 13f). Can you jump over the net to reach a ball? No. Jumping over the net during the point loses it (FIP Rule 13n). Is a ball that clips the net and falls on my side good? Yes. A return that touches the net or posts and lands in the opponents' court is a correct return (FIP Rule 14e). You have to play it before its second bounce. See our guide can you touch the net in padel for more on net contact, or the full rules of padel . 
##### padel-ball-hits-player
If a ball hit by your opponents touches any part of your body or clothing other than your racket, your pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13k). The rule makes no exception for a ball that was going out or for a player standing outside the court. The same applies if your own shot hits you or your partner (13j). The serve has its own rules, which can give the point to the server, make it a fault or lead to a replay. The short answer Getting hit by the ball loses you the point. A ball from your opponents that touches your body or equipment other than the racket is their point (FIP Rules 13k and 14a). Your own shot hitting you or your partner is also a lost point (13j). On the serve: a serve hitting either receiving player or their racket on the full is the server's point (8.5), a serve hitting the server's partner is a fault (7d), and a net serve that hits anyone is replayed (9.1b). What the rules say During a rally FIP Rule 13k: a pair loses the point if the ball hit by the opponents touches any part of a player's body or equipment other than the racket. The rule makes no exception for a player standing outside the court or for a ball that was going out: the ball is still in play until it lands (Rule 12). FIP Rule 14a: from the hitter's side, it is a correct return if the opponents volley it, or if the ball touches the opponents' body, clothing or racket. Touching the racket is simply a volley; touching anything else is a point to the hitter under 13k. FIP Rule 13j: a pair loses the point if, after a player hits the ball, it touches that player, their partner, or anything they wear. On the serve FIP Rule 8.5: if the serve hits either receiving player or their racket before it bounces, the point goes to the server. The receiver must let the serve bounce (8.1), so blocking it on the full, even by accident, costs the point. FIP Rule 7d: if the serve hits the server, their partner, or their equipment, it is a fault. FIP Rule 9.1b: if the serve touches the net or posts and then hits a player or their equipment, it is a net, and the serve is repeated. One detail is worth knowing: the only exception the rulebook makes in 13k is the racket. It doesn't carve out a separate exception for the hand holding the handle, so don't assume a ball off your fingers is safe. Point continues The ball hits your racket, even on the frame or as a reflex block; that is a volley and the rally goes on. A net serve hits you; that is a replay (Rule 9.1b). Point lost The ball touches your body, clothing, cap or anything you wear before you can play it (13k); the rule makes no exception for a ball that was going out. Your own shot hits you or your partner (13j). The serve hits you or your partner before bouncing when your pair is receiving (8.5). Real match situations 1. A smash that was flying out hits you Your opponent smashes hard. The ball is clearly going to clear the back glass without bouncing, which would normally win you the point. But it clips your shoulder on the way. Your pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13k). The direction of the ball doesn't matter once it touches you. 2. You are standing outside the court On a court with open sides, you have stepped out through the gate to collect a ball, and the opponents' shot hits you there. Rule 13k makes no exception for a player outside the court, so your pair loses the point. 3. Your lob hits your partner at the net You play a defensive lob from the back of the court and it hits your partner in the back. After you hit the ball, it touched your partner, so your pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13j). The same is true if your shot comes off the glass and hits you. 4. The serve hits the receiver on the full Your serve is long and flat and hits the receiver's leg before bouncing. The receiver can't claim it was going out of the box: a serve that hits either receiving player or their racket before bouncing is the server's point (FIP Rule 8.5). The same would apply if it had hit the receiver's partner. 5. The serve hits your own partner You serve and the ball clips your partner, who is standing at the net. That is a fault (FIP Rule 7d). If it was your second serve, you lose the point to a double fault (13q). 6. A net serve hits the receiver The serve touches the net tape and then strikes the receiver before bouncing. Because it touched the net first, it is a net under Rule 9.1b, not a point to the server. Replay the serve. Common misconceptions “If it was going out, it's my point.” Not once it touches you. Rule 13k makes no exception for a ball that was going out; it is still in play until it lands (Rule 12). “It only counts if it hits your body, not your clothes.” Rule 13k covers your body and equipment, and Rule 14a names clothing. A ball brushing your shirt is enough. “A ball off your hand counts as hitting the racket.” The exception in 13k is the racket. The rulebook doesn't extend it to your hand. “If your partner gets hit by your shot, you replay.” There is no replay. It is a lost point under 13j. “A serve that hits the receiver is a let.” Only if it touched the net first (9.1b). Otherwise it is the server's point (8.5). Social games vs official matches In official matches the umpire decides whether the ball touched a player. In social games, the honest convention is that the player who was touched says so, because the hit is often too light for anyone else to see. The FIP rulebook doesn't regulate aiming at opponents during a rally, though its etiquette section bans hitting the ball at opponents when it is not in play and treats aggressive, unsportsmanlike behaviour severely. At club level, the common convention is to avoid hard shots at the head or face of a player standing close at the net, and to apologise if a ball hits someone. In official matches, Rule 2 lets the umpire allow up to five minutes' recovery for a player hit by the ball. A hard, low ball at the body of a well-positioned opponent is a normal tactical shot; a full-power smash at someone's face from two metres is not, and most clubs expect players to know the difference. Practical tips Keep your racket up at the net. A racket held in front of your chest turns most body shots into volleys, which keep the point alive under 14a. Our volley guide covers the ready position. Don't duck into the ball. If you can't play a ball that's going out, move out of its path rather than crouching where it might clip you. Talk to your partner on lobs. Many 13j points come from a lob that hits the partner at the net. A quick call before you lob lets them step aside. See positioning in doubles . Servers, check your partner's position. A partner standing in line with your serve risks a 7d fault. Have them stand a little wider or closer to the net. Frequently asked questions If the ball hits me but was going out, who wins the point? The opponents. A ball hit by them that touches your body or equipment other than the racket loses you the point (FIP Rule 13k), and the rule makes no exception for a ball that was going out. What if the ball hits my partner? If your partner is hit by the opponents' shot, your pair loses the point (13k). If your own shot hits your partner, your pair also loses the point (13j). What happens if the serve hits the receiver? If it hits either receiving player or their racket before bouncing, the point goes to the server (FIP Rule 8.5), unless the serve touched the net first, in which case it is replayed (9.1b). Is it a fault if my serve hits my partner? Yes. A serve that hits the server, their partner or their equipment is a fault (FIP Rule 7d). Does the ball hitting my racket count as being hit? No. The racket is the exception in Rule 13k. A ball that hits your racket has been volleyed, and the rally continues if your return is good. More on the serve in our guide to serve and return , and on replays in net serve and let rules . 
##### padel-ball-hits-ceiling
It depends on whether the ball has already bounced in the opponents' court. If it bounces there first and then hits the ceiling, lights or another object above the court, it is a correct return (FIP Rule 14d), and since the ball has left the playing area via an element unrelated to the court, the hitter wins the point. If it hits the ceiling or lights before bouncing in the opponents' court, the hitter loses the point (FIP Rules 13g and 14). The short answer Before the bounce, the ceiling is out: a ball that hits the ceiling, lights or beams before bouncing in the opponents' court loses the hitter the point (FIP Rules 13g and 14). After a correct bounce in the opponents' court, a ball that is carried into the ceiling or lights by the force of the shot is a correct return (FIP Rule 14d), so in practice the point goes to the hitter. What the rules say The ceiling is not part of the court. FIP Rule 12 lists the parts of the court as the ground, the inside of the walls, the metal mesh fence (including its frame), the net and the posts. Everything else, including the ceiling, lights, beams and roof structure, is an element unrelated to the court. That is why these points matter: FIP Rule 13g: a pair loses the point if a player returns the ball and, without bouncing in the opponents' court, it strikes the opponents' walls, metal fence or any element not related to the court. A lob that hits the roof over the opponents' half before landing falls under this rule. Your own side: Rule 13l covers the metal fence, the ground and unrelated elements located on the ground on your own side, not overhead fixtures. A lob that clips a light or beam before bouncing in the opponents' court is not a correct return (Rule 14) and loses the point; the rulebook has no separate clause for overhead fixtures on your own side. FIP Rule 14d: if the ball bounces in the opponents' court and then, by the force or direction of the shot, leaves the court by hitting the ceiling, lights or another object unrelated to the court, it is a correct return (Rule 14d), and since the ball has left the playing area via an element unrelated to the court, the hitter wins the point. Rule 14d defines a correct return; the “point won” rules in Rule 15 only cover holes in the fence and the top of the wall. How high should the ceiling be? The FIP rulebook's court specifications require a minimum free height of 6 metres across the whole court, with nothing such as spotlights projecting into that space. For new facilities it recommends more, 8 metres. Many indoor clubs meet the minimum but not much more, which is why ceiling and light hits are common indoors and almost unknown outdoors. Good shot Your ball bounces in the opponents' court and the force of the shot carries it up into the ceiling, a beam or a light. That is a correct return (Rule 14d), and since the ball has left the playing area, the point is yours. Point lost Your ball hits the ceiling, a light or a beam before it bounces in the opponents' court. It is not a correct return (Rule 14), whether it touched over your half or theirs (Rule 13g covers their side). Real match situations 1. A defensive lob clips a light Under pressure at the back, you throw up a high lob. It clips a hanging light above the opponents' service box before landing. The ball struck an element unrelated to the court before bouncing in the opponents' court, so you lose the point (FIP Rule 13g). It doesn't matter that it would have landed in. 2. A smash that bounces and kicks into the roof You hit a big topspin smash. It bounces in the opponents' court, rebounds high off the back glass and strikes the roof structure. The ball bounced correctly first, and the force of your shot took it into the ceiling, so it is a correct return (FIP Rule 14d), and since the ball has left the playing area via an element unrelated to the court, you win the point. 3. A lob that touches the ceiling on your own side You lob from close to your back glass and the ball brushes a beam while it is still above your half. Your shot is not a correct return (Rule 14), so your pair loses the point. Rule 13l doesn't apply here, because it covers unrelated elements on the ground on your own side, but the result is the same. 4. Right above the net The ball touches a beam that runs directly over the net. The rulebook doesn't address the exact position above the net. In practice it makes no difference: the ball has touched something unrelated to the court before bouncing in the opponents' court, so it is not a correct return (Rule 14), and the hitter loses the point whichever side of the net you place it. 5. Your opponents' shot hits the ceiling after bouncing on your side Your opponents hit a hard ball that bounces on your side, flies off the glass and into the lights before you can reach it. The same rule applies the other way round: it is a correct return under FIP Rule 14d, and in practice their point. Common misconceptions “Any ball that hits the ceiling is out.” Only if it hasn't yet bounced in the opponents' court. After a correct bounce, a ceiling hit is a correct return (Rule 14d). “If it hits the ceiling, replay the point.” The FIP rulebook gives no let for ceiling hits. A let point comes from things such as a ball splitting or a foreign object entering the court (Rule 10), not from a shot hitting the roof. “A ball that drops back into the court after hitting a light is still in play.” If it hit the light before bouncing in the opponents' court, the point was already lost (13g and 14). If it hit it after bouncing, Rule 14d makes it a correct return, so in practice the point goes to the hitter. “The ceiling counts like the glass.” It doesn't. The glass is part of the court (Rule 12); the ceiling and lights are not. Social games vs official matches The FIP rules are clear, but some clubs with unusually low ceilings or obstructions, such as heating pipes or netting between courts, set their own house rules. If you play somewhere new, ask at reception or agree with your opponents before the first point. Common local arrangements include treating a particular beam differently or replaying points where the ball touched netting. Those are club conventions, not FIP rules, so agree them in advance rather than in the middle of a tight game. Without an umpire, the hitting pair usually has the best view of whether a lob touched a light. The honest convention is that they call it on themselves. Lobbing indoors: practical tips Check the ceiling in the warm-up. Hit two or three lobs and see where the lights and beams are. The 3-minute courtesy rally (Rule 2) is the time to learn the building. Flatten your lob. Indoors, a lower, deeper lob with a little topspin is more reliable than a towering one. You still push the opponents back without bringing the roof into play. Our guide to the lob covers the technique. Aim the peak over the net, not over their service box. The highest point of a lob should come around the middle of the court, not above where it will land, so it isn't climbing into the lights over their half. Use the roof on the smash. A smash that bounces and kicks high is a correct return if it reaches the ceiling (14d), which in practice wins you the point. On low-ceilinged courts, a solid topspin smash can be more effective than trying to put the ball out of the court. See our smash guide . Frequently asked questions Is it out if the ball hits the ceiling in padel? If it hits the ceiling before bouncing in the opponents' court, the hitter loses the point (Rules 13g and 14). After the bounce, Rule 14d makes it a correct return, so in practice the point goes to the hitter. What if the ball hits the lights? The lights are treated like the ceiling: an element unrelated to the court. Before the bounce in the opponents' court, the hitter loses the point; after it, the hitter wins it (Rules 13g, 14 and 14d). Is a ceiling hit a let in padel? No. The FIP rulebook lists the reasons for a let point in Rule 10, and a ball hitting the ceiling is not one of them. What is the minimum ceiling height for a padel court? The FIP court specifications require at least 6 metres of free height across the whole court, with 8 metres recommended for new facilities. Can you play the ball after it hits the ceiling? No. Before the bounce in the opponents' court the point is already lost; after the bounce, Rule 14d makes it a correct return, so in practice the point goes to the hitter. For walls and fences, see what happens when the ball hits the fence , or browse the full rules hub . 
##### padel-ball-on-top-of-the-wall
If your shot bounces in your opponents' court and then gets stuck on the flat top of the wall, wedges in the metal fence or escapes through a hole in it, you win the point (FIP Rules 15a and 15b). A ball that lands in the corner where the wall meets the floor is also a good ball (FIP Rule 14i). The catch is the bounce: all of this only works in your favour once the ball has bounced on the other side first. The short answer After a correct bounce in the opponents' court, a ball that gets stuck on top of the wall, stuck in the mesh or goes through a hole in the mesh wins the point for the player who hit it. A ball landing in the floor-wall corner is good. If the ball reaches the wall or fence before it bounces, the hitter loses the point instead. What the rules say Everything in this article hangs off one principle from FIP Rule 12. Once your shot has bounced on the opponents' ground, it may touch any part of the court and stays in play, and the opponents must return it before it bounces a second time. The rulebook lists the parts of the court as the inside of the walls, the metal mesh fence including its frame, the ground, the net and the posts. So after the bounce, glass and mesh are both fair game. The question is what happens when the ball doesn't come back out again. That is where Rules 15 and 14i come in. Stuck on the flat top of the wall (Rule 15b) If the ball bounces in the opponents' court and then comes to rest on the flat top of the wall, the point goes to the player who hit it (FIP Rule 15b). The opponents can't reach it to play it, so the rulebook simply awards the point. Stuck in the mesh or through a hole (Rule 15a) If the ball bounces in the opponents' court and then gets stuck in the metal fence, or leaves the court through a hole in the mesh, that is also a point won for the hitter (FIP Rule 15a). Rule 15a covers a ball that goes through, or sticks in, a hole in the metal fence itself. A gap under the fence is not explicitly covered, so agree how to treat it before playing. The corner where wall meets floor (Rule 14i) A ball that bounces right in the corner where the wall meets the ground in the opponents' court is a correct return (FIP Rule 14i). These balls often skid out flat and are nearly impossible to play, which is exactly why the rule exists: it settles the argument in favour of the hitter. Why the bounce decides everything (Rule 13g) Now flip it round. If your shot strikes the opponents' walls, their metal fence or any element not part of their court without bouncing first, you lose the point (FIP Rule 13g). That covers a volley straight into their mesh where it sticks, and a smash that hits their glass full. The same ball that would have won you the point after a bounce loses it before one. What happens Before bouncing in the opponents' court After bouncing in the opponents' court Ball stuck on the flat top of the wall Point lost by the hitter (13g) Point won by the hitter (15b) Ball stuck in the mesh Point lost by the hitter (13g) Point won by the hitter (15a) Ball goes through a hole in the mesh Point lost by the hitter (13g) Point won by the hitter (15a) Ball hits the fence frame and comes back Point lost by the hitter (13g) Still in play (Rule 12) Ball lands in the floor-wall corner n/a – the corner contact is the bounce (14i) Good ball (14i) Point won Your shot bounces in the opponents' court, then sticks on top of the wall, sticks in the mesh, slips through a hole in the mesh, or lands in the floor-wall corner (Rules 15a, 15b, 14i). Point lost Your shot hits the opponents' wall or mesh before bouncing, wherever it ends up, including stuck in the fence or on top of the wall (Rule 13g). Real match situations The lob that parks on the back wall You play a high, deep lob. It bounces near the opponents' baseline, kicks up off the back glass and settles on the flat top of the wall. Verdict: your point (FIP Rule 15b). Nobody needs to climb anything to keep the rally going. The smash that wedges in the side fence Your smash bounces in mid-court, flies sideways into the side mesh and jams in the wire. Verdict: your point (FIP Rule 15a). It bounced first, so the fence was a legal part of the court, and the opponents can't return a ball that is stuck. The volley that goes straight into the mesh At the net, you punch a volley that never touches their floor and buries itself in their side fence. Verdict: you lose the point (FIP Rule 13g). The ball hit their fence before bouncing, and getting stuck doesn't rescue it. For more on how the fence behaves, see what happens when the ball hits the fence . The ball that dies in the corner Your drive lands exactly where the back glass meets the floor and skids out without a playable rebound. The opponents say it hit the glass first. If it landed in the corner, it is a good ball (FIP Rule 14i). If it clearly struck the glass before touching the ground, it falls under Rule 13g instead. That judgement is the whole dispute, which is why calling it honestly and quickly matters. The ball that clips the top edge and drops back in After bouncing in the opponents' court, the ball catches the top edge of the glass and falls back into their court. The FIP rulebook doesn't specifically address the top edge of the wall. What it does say is that after a correct bounce the ball stays in play when it touches the court, and must be returned before the second bounce (FIP Rule 12). The usual club convention is to treat this like any other wall contact: play on. If it stays on top, Rule 15b applies; if it leaves the court, the out-of-court rules (13d and 13e) decide it. The serve that sticks in the mesh Your serve lands in the right box and then jams in the side fence. Verdict: fault, not a winner (FIP Rule 7e). On the serve, touching the metal fence before the second bounce is a fault, even though the glass is fine. Rule 15a is about rallies, and the serve has its own rules. Common misconceptions "A stuck ball is always a let." No. After a bounce in the opponents' court it is a point for the hitter (Rules 15a and 15b). Before the bounce, it is a point for the opponents (13g). Nothing gets replayed. "The frame of the fence is out." The rulebook counts the metal mesh fence including its frame as part of the court (Rule 12), so after a bounce it is in play like the rest of the mesh. "A ball in the corner hit the wall first, so it's out." A ball that bounces in the floor-wall corner is good (Rule 14i). Only a ball that clearly hit the wall before the floor loses the point. "The opponents can knock the ball off the top of the wall and keep playing." Rule 15b awards the point when the ball is stuck there, so the rally is over. "If the serve sticks in the fence, the server wins." Serves follow Rule 7e: mesh before the second bounce is a fault. Social games vs official matches With an umpire, these calls are the umpire's to make. In social and club games, players call their own side, and the corner ball is the classic flashpoint. A good habit: the pair on the receiving side calls it, and if nobody saw it clearly, agree beforehand whether you replay the point. That is a convention, not an FIP rule, but it saves a lot of arguments. It is also worth agreeing before you start how you treat the top-edge clip, since the rulebook doesn't spell it out. And when a ball lodges high in the fence or on top of the wall, finish the point first, then fetch it safely. Climbing the fence mid-game is how people get hurt. Practical tip: don't stop playing early The most common lost point in this area isn't a rule dispute. It is a player who assumes a ball heading for the corner or the top of the glass is dead and stops moving, then watches it pop back out. Unless the ball is actually stuck, keep playing until it bounces twice. Defending deep balls off the glass is a skill in its own right; our guide to playing off the back glass covers the footwork. Frequently asked questions Who wins the point if the ball gets stuck in the fence in padel? If the ball bounced in the opponents' court before sticking in the mesh, the hitter wins the point (FIP Rule 15a). If it went into the mesh without bouncing, the hitter loses it (FIP Rule 13g). What happens if the ball lands on top of the wall? After a correct bounce in the opponents' court, a ball that stays on the flat top of the wall is a point won for the hitter (FIP Rule 15b). If it lands there straight from the racket, it struck the opponents' wall without bouncing, which loses the point (Rule 13g). Is a ball in the corner good in padel? Yes. A ball bouncing in the corner where the wall meets the ground in the opponents' court is a correct return (FIP Rule 14i). What if the ball goes through a hole in the fence? If it bounced in the opponents' court first, it is a point for the hitter (FIP Rule 15a). What if my serve gets stuck in the fence? It is a fault. A serve that touches the metal fence before its second bounce is a service fault (FIP Rule 7e). See every way a serve can be a fault . Does the ball have to come back out of the corner to be good? No. Rule 14i treats the corner bounce itself as valid. It is then up to the opponents to return it before the second bounce, which is often impossible. For the full picture, see our rules of padel guide. 
##### padel-out-of-court-play
You can only leave the court to play the ball if out-of-court play has been authorised, which depends on the court and its surrounding safety area meeting FIP specifications (FIP Rule 16). If it hasn't been authorised, hitting the ball with any part of your body outside the court loses the point (FIP Rule 13p). Many club courts are played without it, so check before you sprint for the door. The short answer Only on courts where out-of-court play is authorised. There, you may chase a ball that has gone out over the side wall or through a door and return it before it bounces again, but a ball over the end wall is always lost. Everywhere else, a ball that bounces in and then leaves the court is the opponents' point, and you can't play from outside. What the rules say The condition: Rule 16 Rule 16 ties out-of-court play to the court meeting the FIP safety-area specifications. The rulebook doesn't say who authorises it; in competitions the organiser normally announces whether it applies. Either way, it isn't something players decide mid-rally, and an opening in the side wall doesn't automatically mean you can run through it. The rulebook's safety-area specifications for out-of-court play are: Access points: 2 on each side of the court. Clear space outside: an area free of obstacles, no less than 3 m wide (4 m recommended), 4 m long on either side and at least 3 m high. Door openings with one access per side: at least 1.05 × 2.0 m and at most 2.20 × 2.20 m. Door openings with two accesses per side: at least 0.72 × 2.00 m and at most 1.10 × 2.20 m. If your club court doesn't have that clear space around it, out-of-court play isn't on, whatever the doors look like. When out-of-court play is not authorised Your body must be inside. If the player hitting the ball has one foot, both feet or any part of the body outside the court, the point is lost (FIP Rule 13p). A ball that leaves the court is finished. If the ball bounces correctly and then crosses the marked outer limit of the court, at the side or the end, or goes out through a gate, the pair that had to return it loses the point (FIP Rule 13d). So a ball that bounces in your court and flies out is the opponents' point. Serves through the gate. On a court without a safety zone, a serve that lands in the box and leaves through a gate is a fault (FIP Rule 7f). When out-of-court play is authorised Shots from outside are valid. A ball hit from outside the court is a correct return as long as the other conditions for a correct return are met (FIP Rule 14j). The end wall is still final. If the ball bounces correctly and then goes over the end wall, the point is lost for the side that had to return it, and the ball can't be played (FIP Rule 13e). Side wall and door balls can be chased. If the ball goes over a side wall or out through a door, the point is only lost if the ball bounces a second time or touches an element unrelated to the court (FIP Rule 13e). The neutral post. The vertical post dividing the door openings is a neutral zone above 0.92 m, and any player may touch or hold it (FIP Rule 13b). Normally, touching the net, posts or the opponents' court during a point loses it (Rule 13a); this is a specific exception for out-of-court play. Situation Out-of-court play not authorised Out-of-court play authorised Ball bounces, then goes over the end wall Point to the hitter (13d) Point to the hitter, can't be played (13e) Ball bounces, then goes over the side wall Point to the hitter (13d) Playable outside before the second bounce (13e) Ball bounces, then goes out through a door Point to the hitter (13d) Playable outside before the second bounce (13e) You hit the ball with a foot outside the court Point lost (13p) Valid if the return is otherwise correct (14j) Holding the dividing door post above 0.92 m Not covered by the 13b exception Allowed, neutral zone (13b) Allowed On an authorised court: running out through the door to return a ball that went over the side wall or through a door, before it bounces a second time, and holding the neutral post above 0.92 m (Rules 13b, 13e, 14j). Not allowed Playing from outside on a court where out-of-court play isn't authorised (13p), chasing a ball over the end wall on any court (13e), or letting the ball bounce a second time outside (13e). Real match situations The smash that flies out of a club court Your opponent smashes, the ball bounces in your court and sails over the side glass. You run out through the door and somehow hit it back in. On a court where out-of-court play isn't authorised, your shot doesn't count: the point ended when the ball left the court, and it goes to the smasher (FIP Rule 13d). The same smash on an authorised court Now the court and event allow out-of-court play. You sprint out, reach the ball before its second bounce and send it back so it lands in the opponents' court. That is a correct return (FIP Rules 13e and 14j), and the rally continues. Over the back wall The smash bounces and goes over your end wall instead. Even with out-of-court play authorised, the point is lost for your side and the ball can't be played (FIP Rule 13e). Save your legs. One foot through the doorway On a non-authorised court, you stretch for a ball by the open doorway and plant your foot outside the court as you hit. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13p). It doesn't matter that the ball was still inside; your body was not. The ball hits you outside You're outside on an authorised court and the opponents' shot hits your body instead of your racket. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13k). Rule 13k makes no distinction between inside and outside the court, so it applies wherever you are standing. Grabbing the post to get back in On an authorised court, you return the ball from outside and grab the vertical post between the door openings to swing yourself back in. Above 0.92 m that post is a neutral zone, so it is allowed (FIP Rule 13b). Common misconceptions "If I can reach it, I can play it." Not unless out-of-court play is authorised for that court (Rule 16). Otherwise, Rule 13d ends the point when the ball leaves. "A ball that goes out of the court is replayed." No. After a correct bounce, a ball leaving the court is a point for the hitter (13d), or playable on an authorised court (13e). Neither is a let. "An open door means out-of-court play is on." Doors exist on every court. What matters is the court and safety area meeting specifications and the play being authorised (Rule 16). "Outside, you get one bounce." The ball has already bounced once in your court. Outside, you must reach it before a second bounce (13e). "End-wall balls can be chased too." Never. Rule 13e makes those unplayable. Social games vs official matches The rulebook doesn't say who authorises out-of-court play; in competitions the organiser normally announces whether it applies, and the umpire then applies Rules 13d, 13e and 13p. In club and social games, the convention at most venues is to play without it, often because there isn't safe space around the court or the next court is right there. Agree before the first point. If anyone in your four is unsure, play it as not authorised: that way a ball leaving the court is simply the hitter's point, and nobody runs into a fence or a neighbouring game. Practical tips On a non-authorised court, stop chasing. Once the ball has left the court after a bounce, the point is over. Running after it only risks injury. On an authorised court, read the smash early. Out-of-court play usually starts with a hard smash that bounces and kicks high. Start moving towards the door as soon as you see the bounce, rather than waiting under the ball. Aim your return deep and safe. From outside you have little control. A high, deep ball into the opponents' court gives you and your partner time to get back into position . Frequently asked questions Is out-of-court play allowed in amateur padel? Only when the court and safety area meet specifications and play outside has been authorised (FIP Rule 16). Check the event's rules. In casual club play the common convention is that it isn't allowed. What happens if the ball bounces and goes out of the court? If out-of-court play isn't authorised, the pair that had to return it loses the point (FIP Rule 13d). If it is authorised, a ball over the side wall or through a door can still be played before its second bounce, but a ball over the end wall can't (FIP Rule 13e). Can I touch the door post when playing outside? Yes, when out-of-court play is authorised, the vertical post dividing the doors is a neutral zone above 0.92 m that any player may touch or hold (FIP Rule 13b). Which door do I have to use to go out? The FIP rulebook doesn't specifically say. What it does cover is that touching the net, posts or the opponents' court during a point loses it (Rule 13a), so make sure you never step into their side on the way out or back. What if the serve goes out of the door? On a court without a safety zone, a serve that bounces in the box and leaves through a gate is a fault (FIP Rule 7f). More in our guide to service faults . For every other rule, see our complete rules of padel . 
##### padel-ball-hits-opponents-wall-directly
No. If your shot hits the opponents' glass, metal fence or anything else on their side before it bounces on their ground, you lose the point (FIP Rule 13g). Your ball has to bounce in their court first; after that it can hit any wall or the fence and stay in play (FIP Rule 14c). It is one of the most common mistakes new players make, especially on smashes and volleys. The short answer Your shot must bounce on the opponents' ground before it touches their glass or mesh. Glass first, floor second is a lost point (Rule 13g). Floor first, then glass or mesh, is a perfectly good shot (Rule 14c). What the rules say Padel is a game of walls, but the walls belong to different people at different moments. The rules treat your own walls and the opponents' walls in completely different ways. Your shot must bounce first on their side A return is correct when it bounces directly in the opponents' court, or when it hits a wall of your own court first and then bounces in theirs (FIP Rule 14b). Once it has bounced there, it may hit their fence or walls (FIP Rule 14c) and the opponents have to deal with it. Their walls before the bounce: point lost If you return the ball, directly or off your own walls, and without bouncing it strikes the opponents' walls, their metal fence or any element not part of their court, you lose the point (FIP Rule 13g). 13g also covers "any element not related to" the court, which takes in the ceiling and lights. The net cord doesn't save it If your return touches the net or posts and then goes straight into the opponents' wall, fence or an unrelated element without bouncing, that is a point lost too (FIP Rule 13h). Compare that with a net cord that drops into their court: that one is fine (FIP Rule 14e). The corner is good A ball that bounces in the corner where the wall meets the floor in the opponents' court is a correct return (FIP Rule 14i). That is the only real grey zone, and the rule settles it for the hitter. Your shot Verdict Rule Bounces in their court, then hits their glass Good, rally continues 14c Bounces in their court, then hits their mesh Good, rally continues 14c Hits their glass or mesh without bouncing Point lost 13g Hits your own glass, then bounces in their court Good 14b Hits your own glass, then their glass without bouncing Point lost 13g Clips the net, then hits their wall directly Point lost 13h Clips the net, then bounces in their court Good 14e Bounces in the floor-wall corner Good 14i Good shot Ball bounces on the opponents' ground first, then hits their glass, mesh or both. Or it comes off your own glass and bounces in their court (Rules 14b, 14c, 14i). Point lost Ball hits the opponents' glass, mesh, ceiling or lights before bouncing on their side, including after a net cord (Rules 13g, 13h). Real match situations The flat smash into the back glass You get a short lob and hit it flat and hard. It flies over the opponents' heads and hits their back glass full, then bounces. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13g). This is why good players hit the smash down into the court so it bounces first and then rebounds off the glass. The volley into the side mesh At the net you angle a volley sharply. It doesn't touch their floor and clatters into their side fence. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13g). Angled volleys need to land in the court before the side wall, not after. Your own back glass, then theirs Under pressure, you play the ball against your own back glass to get it out. It flies the full length of the court and hits the opponents' back glass on the full. Verdict: point lost. Rule 13g explicitly covers balls returned off your own walls. Using your own glass is legal (14b), but the ball still has to bounce on their side before touching their walls. The net cord that kicks into their glass Your drive clips the tape, pops up and drops straight onto their side glass without touching the floor. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13h). If it had dropped onto their floor first, it would have been a correct return (14e). The opponent who volleys a ball that was going out Your lob is too long and is heading straight for their back glass. The opponent at the net instinctively reaches up and volleys it. Verdict: that counts as a correct return by you, and the rally continues (FIP Rule 14a). Rule 14a says a ball the opponents volley, or that touches their body, clothing or racket, is a correct return. The ball that hits the opponent first Your overhit drive is about to hit their back glass on the full, but it hits the opponent standing in front of it first. Verdict: your point (FIP Rule 13k). A ball that touches a player's body or equipment other than the racket loses them the point, and Rule 13k makes no exception for a ball that was going out. More in what happens if the ball hits you . Common misconceptions "Glass is glass, so it's always in play." Only your own glass can be used before the bounce (14b). The opponents' glass only comes into play after the ball bounces on their side (14c, 13g). "The serve works the same way." Not quite. A serve must bounce in the receiver's box, and after that the glass is fine but the metal fence is a fault (FIP Rule 7e). In a rally, both glass and mesh are fine after the bounce. "A net cord gives you a second chance." No. Net cord then their wall, without a bounce, is still a lost point (13h). "If the opponent touches it, it doesn't matter where it was going." Right, and that cuts both ways. Once they volley it, it is a correct return (14a), so they can't then claim it would have hit the glass. "The ceiling is neutral." Before the bounce, hitting the ceiling or lights is a lost point under 13g. After the bounce, the ball leaving the court via the ceiling or lights is a correct return (FIP Rule 14d). See ball hits the ceiling . Social games vs official matches With an umpire, glass-before-floor calls are made for you. In social games, the opponents usually have the best view of their own back glass, but it is often clearer to the hitter's partner at the net. The fair convention is simple: if you saw your own ball hit their glass on the full, say so. A ball that hits the glass and floor so close together that nobody can tell is where many groups agree to replay the point; that is a club convention, not an FIP rule. Tactical tip: let it go Because of Rule 13g, the best defensive skill against an overhit ball is doing nothing. If a lob or drive is clearly going to hit your glass before bouncing, step aside and let it. The moment you volley it or it touches you, you have made it a good shot for the opponents (14a, 13k). The more you trust your read, the more free points you collect. On the attacking side, aim to land the ball in the court with enough depth to reach the glass after the bounce. That is what makes deep balls so hard to defend: the opponents have to read the bounce and the rebound. Frequently asked questions Can the ball hit the side glass in padel? Yes, as long as it bounces in the opponents' court first (FIP Rule 14c). You can also play off your own side glass before the ball crosses the net (FIP Rule 14b). Can you hit your own wall in padel? Yes, your own glass walls can be used, provided the ball then bounces in the opponents' court (Rule 14b). Your own mesh is not allowed (Rule 13l). Details in playing the ball off your own wall . Is it a point if the smash hits the back glass before the floor? No. The smasher loses the point, because the ball struck the opponents' wall without bouncing (FIP Rule 13g). What if the ball hits the net post and then their wall? If it goes from the net or post straight into the opponents' wall or fence without bouncing, the hitter loses the point (FIP Rule 13h). What if the ball lands exactly where the glass meets the floor? A ball bouncing in that corner is a correct return (FIP Rule 14i). 
##### padel-double-hit-rule
A double hit in padel loses you the point (FIP Rule 13i), but not every double contact counts: if the ball touches your racket twice within the same movement and its natural exit isn't substantially changed, play continues (FIP Rule 14h). Both partners hitting the ball, at the same time or one after the other, also loses the point (FIP Rule 13o), with one specific exception for clashing rackets. The short answer Two separate hits by you, or one hit each by you and your partner, lose the point. A double contact within one swing is legal if it doesn't substantially change where the ball was naturally going. If you both swing and one racket hits the ball while the other hits your partner's racket, that is not a double hit. What the rules say Rule 13i: the double hit A double hit is listed among the ways to lose a point (FIP Rule 13i). Rule 12 also sets the rhythm of the game: the pairs hit the ball alternately. Each pair gets one hit per crossing of the net. Rule 14h: when it isn't a double hit The rulebook carves out the most common real-world case. A double contact is not a double hit if it happens in the same movement and the ball's natural exit is not substantially changed (FIP Rule 14h). Two conditions, both required: Same movement. One swing, not a swing and then a second push or flick. Natural exit not substantially changed. The ball goes roughly where that swing would have sent it anyway. Rule 13o: both partners If both partners hit the ball, simultaneously or one after the other, the pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13o). The exception: if both players try to hit it at the same time and one hits the ball while the other hits their partner's racket, that is not a double hit. Rule 13j: the ball comes back onto you Related but separate: if the ball touches you, your partner or anything either of you is wearing after you hit it, you lose the point (FIP Rule 13j). What about carrying the ball? The FIP rulebook doesn't have a separate rule called "carry". A ball that sits on the strings or is slung is judged by the same test in Rule 14h: was it one movement, and was the ball's natural exit substantially changed? If the contact stays within one swing and the ball goes where the stroke was sending it, it is fine. If the ball is guided somewhere it wouldn't otherwise have gone, the umpire can treat it as a double hit. Point continues A mishit where the ball touches the frame and the strings in the same swing and flies roughly where you aimed (14h). Both partners swing, one hits the ball, the other only hits the partner's racket (13o exception). Point lost Two separate hits by the same player (13i). Both partners touch the ball (13o). The ball rebounds off your racket onto your body, your partner or your clothing (13j). Real match situations Situation Verdict Rule Frame, then strings, in one volley swing Play on 14h Ball pops up off your racket and you hit it again Point lost 13i You and your partner both touch the ball Point lost 13o Rackets clash, only one touches the ball Play on 13o exception Ball hits your racket, then your arm Point lost 13j The frame-and-strings volley A fast ball comes at you at the net. You get the racket up late, the ball catches the frame, rolls onto the strings and floats over. You heard two noises, but it was one swing and the ball went roughly where the volley was going. Verdict: legal (FIP Rule 14h). The pop-up and second touch You mistime a block, the ball loops up just in front of you, and you tap it over with a second motion. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13i). The second touch was a separate movement. The ball down the middle A ball comes down the middle. You both go for it, your partner gets a touch, then it hits your racket and goes over. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13o). It doesn't matter how small the first touch was. The clash Same ball, same two players, but this time your partner's racket hits your racket while only yours touches the ball. Verdict: play on (13o exception). The rulebook specifically says this is not a double hit. The ball that comes back onto you You block a smash, the ball deflects off your racket and grazes your forearm before going over. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13j). The rule makes no distinction between your arm and any other part of your body. Your own glass, then a second hit You hit the ball, it comes back off your own back glass, and you or your partner hit it again before it crosses. Verdict: point lost. Your pair has hit the ball twice in one turn (13i, or 13o if it was your partner). Playing off your own glass is only legal as part of a single shot that then reaches the opponents' court (14b). Common misconceptions "Any double contact is a fault." No. One swing with a double contact is fine if the ball's natural exit isn't substantially changed (14h). "A tiny touch by my partner doesn't count." It does. Any touch by both partners loses the point (13o). "If our rackets hit each other, we lose the point." Not if only one of you touched the ball. That is the 13o exception. "A ball off the frame is a double hit." The rules don't distinguish frame from strings. A frame shot is one contact like any other. "The ball hitting my racket hand doesn't count." Rule 13j covers the ball touching the player after the hit, and the rulebook doesn't carve out the hand holding the racket. The safe reading is that it counts. Social games vs official matches With an umpire, "same movement" and "substantially changed" are the umpire's judgement. In social games, the player who made the contact is the only one who really knows, so the convention is that you call your own double hits and partner touches. Most players will accept a frame-then-strings mishit without a word. What they won't accept is a deliberate second push or flick, and neither does Rule 13i. Practical tip: talk to your partner Most double hits in doubles aren't technical. They are two players going for the same ball. Agree simple calls before the match, like "mine" and "yours", and decide who takes balls down the middle. A common agreement is that the player with the forehand in the middle takes it, but whatever you choose, say it before the point, not during it. Our guide to positioning in doubles covers how to split the court so these moments become rare. On the technical side, double contacts mostly come from late, stiff volleys. A compact volley with the racket out in front reduces mishits far more than any rule knowledge does. Frequently asked questions Is a double hit allowed in padel? A double hit is a point lost (FIP Rule 13i). The exception is a double contact within the same movement that doesn't substantially change the ball's natural exit (FIP Rule 14h). Can both partners hit the ball in padel? No. If both partners hit the ball, one after the other or at the same time, the pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13o). What if my racket hits my partner's racket? If you both tried to hit the ball at the same time, and one of you hit the ball while the other only hit the partner's racket, it isn't a double hit (FIP Rule 13o exception). Is carrying the ball illegal in padel? The rulebook has no separate carry rule. It is judged by Rule 14h: one movement, and the ball's natural exit not substantially changed. A ball that is guided somewhere new can be called a double hit. What if the ball hits the racket and then me? You lose the point. After you hit the ball, it must not touch you, your partner or anything you are wearing (FIP Rule 13j). See also what happens if the ball hits you . For every other rule in one place, see our rules of padel . 
##### padel-racket-strap-rule
Yes. The FIP racket specifications make a non-elastic safety cord, no longer than 35 cm and worn on the wrist, mandatory. If the cord breaks or you drop your racket during a point, your pair loses the point immediately (FIP Rule 13r), and a ball hit with a thrown racket is also a lost point (FIP Rule 13m). The short answer The wrist cord is compulsory equipment, not an optional extra. Drop your racket or break the cord in a rally and your pair loses the point straight away. Throwing your racket at the ball never wins a point. Two-handed shots and switching hands aren't prohibited by anything in the rulebook. What the rules say The safety cord is part of the racket specification The FIP equipment rules describe the padel racket in detail, and the safety cord is part of that description. It must be non-elastic, no longer than 35 cm and worn on the wrist, and it is mandatory. A racket without a cord, or played with the cord hanging loose and not on your wrist, doesn't meet the specification. Rule 13r: breaking the cord or dropping the racket If a player breaks the safety cord or drops the racket, the pair immediately loses the point (FIP Rule 13r). Notice two things. First, it is the pair that loses, so your partner's dropped racket costs you both. Second, it is immediate: the point doesn't play on to see whether the ball would have come back. Rule 13m: the thrown racket If the ball is hit with a thrown racket, the point is lost (FIP Rule 13m). However good the shot, a racket that has left your hand can't make a legal return. Rule 13a: the racket and the net Your racket counts as part of you. If it touches the net, the posts, the tension cable or any part of the opponents' court while the ball is in play, you lose the point (FIP Rule 13a). That includes a racket that slips out of your hand and lands over there. For the full rule, see can you touch the net in padel . The rest of the racket specification Racket element FIP limit Total length Max 45.5 cm Handle Max 20 cm long, 50 mm wide, 50 mm thick Head width Max 26 cm Head thickness Max 38 mm (2.5% tolerance) Holes 9–13 mm in the centre area; edge holes up to 20 mm allowed Safety cord Non-elastic, max 35 cm, worn on the wrist, mandatory Allowed Playing with the cord around your wrist, adjusting it between points, two-handed shots and switching hands (no prohibition in the rulebook). Not allowed Playing without the cord or with it off your wrist, dropping the racket or breaking the cord during a point (13r), hitting the ball with a thrown racket (13m), the racket touching the net or opponents' court during play (13a). Real match situations The racket that flies out on a smash You go for a big smash , sweaty grip, and the racket leaves your hand. The ball still goes over and the opponents can't reach it. Verdict: your pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13r). Dropping the racket loses the point immediately, whatever the ball does. The cord snaps mid-rally The cord on an old racket finally gives way during a rally. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13r). Rule 13r names breaking the cord alongside dropping the racket. Check the cord for fraying before you play, and replace it when it starts to wear. The desperate throw A lob is sailing over you, you can't get there, and you throw your racket at it. It connects and the ball drops over the net. Verdict: point lost (FIP Rule 13m). A thrown racket is also exactly the kind of thing the cord exists to prevent. The racket that slips but hangs on the cord Your grip slips, the racket leaves your fingers but dangles from the cord on your wrist. The FIP rulebook doesn't define exactly what counts as "dropping" the racket, so this is a judgement call. With an umpire, it is the umpire's decision. In a social game, the honest approach is to stop and give the point if you couldn't play on normally. If the dangling racket touched the net, Rule 13a decides it anyway. Switching hands for a wide ball A ball goes wide to your backhand side and you swap the racket into your other hand to play a forehand. The FIP rulebook doesn't specifically cover switching hands, and we found no prohibition in it. The practical problem is the cord: it is on one wrist, so swapping hands mid-rally can twist it or pull the racket out of position. Most players who try it find it isn't worth the risk of a drop under Rule 13r. The two-handed backhand Some players, especially juniors and players from tennis, hit backhands with two hands. The rulebook doesn't address two-handed grips and contains no prohibition on them. Keep the cord on your dominant wrist and play. Common misconceptions "The strap is optional in friendly games." In the FIP rules it is mandatory equipment. Clubs can't change the physics either: a racket without a cord can fly into someone's face. "Any cord will do." The specification calls for a non-elastic cord of no more than 35 cm. An elastic band or a long lanyard doesn't meet it. "Dropping the racket only matters if you then miss the ball." No. Rule 13r loses the point immediately, even if your shot was a winner. "A thrown racket is fine if it's a great shot." Rule 13m says the point is lost, every time. "Two-handed shots are illegal in padel." Nothing in the FIP rulebook prohibits them. Social games vs official matches In official FIP-rules matches, the equipment specification and Rules 13r and 13m apply as written. In club and social games, wearing the cord is both the rule and the expected etiquette, and most clubs and coaches insist on it for safety. The grey areas are the ones the rulebook doesn't spell out, like a racket that slips but stays on the cord. Agree before you start how you will treat those, and give the point away when it is your mistake. Practical tips: wear it properly Hand through first. Put your hand through the loop before you take your grip, not afterwards. Tighten it. Slide the stopper or twist the loop so it sits snugly on your wrist without cutting in. A loose cord lets the racket travel further if it slips. Check it regularly. Look for fraying where the cord meets the handle, especially on older rackets. Dry your hands. Most drops happen with a wet grip. Use the time between points (up to 20 seconds under FIP Rule 2) to wipe your hand, and replace a worn overgrip. If you're new to the game, our rules of padel guide covers everything else you need to know before your first match, and the rules hub collects every specific question in one place. Frequently asked questions Do you have to wear the wrist strap in padel? Yes. The FIP racket specification makes a non-elastic safety cord, worn on the wrist, mandatory. How long can a padel racket strap be? No longer than 35 cm, and it must be non-elastic, under the FIP equipment specification. What happens if you drop your racket in padel? If you drop your racket during a point, your pair loses the point immediately (FIP Rule 13r). Can you throw your racket at the ball in padel? No. Hitting the ball with a thrown racket loses the point (FIP Rule 13m). Can you switch hands or play two-handed in padel? The FIP rulebook doesn't specifically address either, and we found no prohibition. The cord stays on one wrist, so switching hands is awkward and raises the risk of a drop. What size can a padel racket be? Max 45.5 cm long with a handle of up to 20 cm, a head up to 26 cm wide and up to 38 mm thick (2.5% tolerance), and holes of 9 to 13 mm in the centre area (edge holes up to 20 mm), under the FIP specification. 
##### padel-change-of-ends
In padel you change ends after the first game, the third game and every odd game after that, and in a tie-break you change every six points (FIP Rule 5). The point of the rule is fairness: sun, wind, lighting and slightly different glass all even out when both pairs play from both ends. Below is exactly when it happens, how long you get, and what to do when everyone forgets. The short answer Change ends whenever the total number of games played in the set is odd: 1, 3, 5, 7 and so on (FIP Rule 5). In a tie-break, change after every six points. After the first game of a set you change without any rest; at the other changes of ends you have up to 90 seconds, and at the end of a set up to 120 seconds (FIP Rule 2). In a tie-break, play is meant to be continuous (Rule 2.5), though Rule 2.10 grants up to 20 seconds for the change. What the rules say The change of ends is covered by two short rules in the FIP rulebook, one for when and one for how long . Rule 5 (when). Pairs change ends after the first game, after the third game and after every subsequent odd game in the set. In a tie-break they change every six points. If a change is missed by mistake, it is corrected as soon as someone notices, and every point played before the correction stands. Rule 2 (how long). Play is meant to be continuous. You get a maximum of 20 seconds between points, and the rulebook does not allow eating or drinking between points. After the first game of each set, play is continuous and you change ends without a rest period (Rule 2.5). At the other changes of ends you have up to 90 seconds. At the end of a set you have up to 120 seconds. In a tie-break, play is meant to be continuous (Rule 2.5), though Rule 2.10 grants up to 20 seconds for the change. The rulebook is not fully consistent here, so in an official match follow the umpire. The easiest way to apply Rule 5 is to add both pairs' games in the current set together. The count restarts with every new set. If the total is odd, you swap. A score of 2-1 is three games, so you change. A score of 3-1 is four games, so you stay. It does not matter who is winning or who served. Score in the set Games played Change ends? 1-0 1 Yes 1-1 or 2-0 2 No 2-1 or 3-0 3 Yes 2-2, 3-1 or 4-0 4 No 3-2, 4-1 or 5-0 5 Yes 4-3, 5-2 or 6-1 7 Yes 5-4 or 6-3 9 Yes 6-5 11 Yes Tie-break Every 6 points Yes, at 6, 12, 18 points and so on Change ends After game 1, 3, 5, 7, 9 or 11 of a set. In a tie-break, when the points played add up to 6, 12, 18 and so on (FIP Rule 5). Stay where you are After an even number of games, such as 1-1, 3-1 or 4-4, and at every tie-break score that is not a multiple of six (FIP Rule 5). Real match situations You win the first game of the match The score is 1-0, one game has been played, so you change ends straight away (FIP Rule 5), but with no rest: Rule 2 says play is continuous after the first game of each set. Walk round the net, take up your positions and start the next game. Save the drink and the tactical chat for the change after the third game, where up to 90 seconds are allowed. It is 3-1 and someone heads for the bench Four games have been played, which is an even number, so there is no change of ends (FIP Rule 5). The next game starts within the normal 20 seconds between points (FIP Rule 2). This is the most common mix-up in social padel: players remember "every two games" and lose track of where the cycle started. Count the total, not the gap since the last change. You realise at 3-3 that you never changed at 3-2 Suppose you should have changed at 3-2 (five games) but carried on, and someone notices during the game at 3-3. Rule 5 says the mistake is corrected as soon as it is discovered, and all points already played stand. So you finish the current point, swap ends, and the score stays exactly as it is. Nobody replays points or loses them because of the missed change. One detail: if the mistake is discovered after a first-serve fault, the server has only one serve left (FIP Rule 5.3). The tie-break reaches 4-2 Six points have been played (4 + 2), so the pairs change ends (FIP Rule 5). In a tie-break, play is meant to be continuous (Rule 2.5), though Rule 2.10 grants up to 20 seconds for the change. The next change comes when the points total 12, which can only be at 6-6, because 7-5 ends a tie-break. Since any pause is so short, have your towel and water ready before the tie-break starts; this is not the moment for a long drink. A set ends 6-3 or 6-4 The count restarts each set (Rule 5). After 6-3 (an odd game) you change ends with up to 120 seconds' rest, then change again, without rest, after the first game of the new set (Rule 2). After 6-4 you stay, then change after the first game of the new set. Either way, the first game of every set is followed by a change of ends, so the new set always starts with the same rhythm. You play a super tie-break instead of a third set Rule 1 lists a 10-point super tie-break as an alternative scoring option. Rule 5 gives the change of ends for a tie-break as every six points, and most organisers apply that same pattern to the super tie-break, so you change at 6, 12 and 18 points played. If the event uses a different format, the organiser should announce it in advance. Common misconceptions "You change every two games." Not quite. You change after odd-numbered games, which works out as every two games only after the first change. The first change comes after a single game (FIP Rule 5). "If we forgot to change, the last game is void." No. The error is corrected when discovered and earlier points stand (FIP Rule 5). "You can drink whenever you like." Rule 2 does not allow food or drink between points. Your chances are the changes of ends and the break between sets. "The change of ends is a 2-minute rest." It is up to 90 seconds, and after the first game of a set there is no rest at all. Only the end of a set gives up to 120 seconds (FIP Rule 2). "The game count carries over from the last set." No. Rule 5 counts odd games in the set, so the count restarts with each new set. "In a tie-break you change after the first point, like the first game." No, the tie-break pattern is simply every six points (FIP Rule 5). Social games vs official matches In an official match the umpire tracks the score, calls the changes and times the breaks. In a social match nobody does, which is why missed changes are so common. A few habits help: Whoever serves announces the game score before the first point of each game. Hearing "3-2" out loud makes the odd total obvious. Agree before the first ball whether you are playing a tie-break or super tie-break, so nobody argues at 6-6. Keep the breaks honest. Ninety seconds is plenty for a drink and a quick word with your partner. Club courts are often booked by the hour, and long breaks eat into everyone's playing time. If you are new to scoring, it helps to read our guide to padel scoring alongside this page, because the change of ends only makes sense once the game count is second nature. Using the change of ends well The change of ends is more than a rest. It is the only regular time you get to talk tactics without the 20-second clock (FIP Rule 2). Use it for one concrete adjustment, not a speech: "lob their left-side player more", "I'll take the middle balls", "let's serve to the body". Think about the conditions at the new end too. An outdoor court can put the sun straight into your eyes on lobs from one end, and an indoor court can have a light that sits right where a high ball comes down, which changes how and when you play the lob . If there is a ball change in your match, it is announced in advance and happens after an established odd number of games (the warm-up counts as two games and a tie-break as one), never at the start of a tie-break, or at the start of a set (FIP Rule 17), so it often falls at a change of ends as well. For the full picture of how points, games and breaks fit together, see the padel match time rules and the main rules of padel . Frequently asked questions Do you change sides after the first game in padel? Yes. Rule 5 lists the first game as the first change of ends, followed by the third and every odd game in the set after that. There is no rest at that first change: play is continuous after the first game of each set (FIP Rule 2). How long is the break at a change of ends? Up to 90 seconds, except after the first game of a set, when you change without a rest. At the end of a set it is up to 120 seconds. In a tie-break, play is meant to be continuous (Rule 2.5), though Rule 2.10 grants up to 20 seconds for the change. When do you change ends in a padel tie-break? Every six points: when the points played add up to 6, 12, 18 and so on (FIP Rule 5). What happens if we forget to change ends? You change as soon as someone notices, and every point played until then stands. Nothing is replayed (FIP Rule 5). Can I have a drink between points? No. Rule 2 does not allow food or drink between points. Use the changes of ends and the breaks between sets. Does the server change at the change of ends? The change of ends does not alter the serving order. Who serves next follows the normal rotation, explained in our guide to serving and receiving order . 
##### padel-serving-and-receiving-order
In padel the serve alternates between the pairs game by game, and the two partners in each pair take turns at their service games, in an order chosen before each set and fixed for that set (FIP Rule 6.8). Each game starts from the right-hand side and the server alternates boxes after every point (FIP Rule 6.5). Receiving has its own fixed order too, and the rulebook says exactly how to fix mistakes. The short answer One player serves a whole game, starting from the right and switching sides after every point (FIP Rule 6.5). The next game is served by a player from the other pair, and partners alternate their service games in an order fixed for the set (FIP Rule 6.8). On the receiving side, the pair decides who receives first at the start of the set, and the two players then receive alternately in that fixed order (FIP Rules 8.2 and 8.3). What the rules say Rule 4: the toss. Before the match, a coin toss decides who chooses first. The winner can choose to serve or receive, choose the end of the court, or let the opponents choose. Rule 6.5: which box. The serve goes diagonally into the receiver's box and must land inside its lines. Every game starts from the right-hand side, and the server alternates between the right and left boxes after each point. Rule 6.8: the serving order. The order of service is chosen before each set and stays fixed for that set. In practice the standard rotation looks like this, with pair A serving first: Game 1: pair A, first server (A1). Game 2: pair B, first server (B1). Game 3: pair A, the other partner (A2). Game 4: pair B, the other partner (B2). Game 5: back to A1, and the cycle repeats until the set ends. Rules 8.2 to 8.4: the receiving order. In the first game of each set that a pair receives, that pair decides which partner receives first. That player receives the first serve of every game the pair receives in the set, and the two partners then receive alternately (8.2, 8.3). The order is fixed for the set or tie-break. If the order is altered by mistake, play continues that way until the end of the game or tie-break and then goes back to the correct order (8.4). Rule 6.7: the wrong side. If a player serves from the wrong side, it is corrected as soon as it is noticed. Points already played stand, and a fault on a first serve made from the wrong side still counts. How this works on court Because every game starts from the right, the first point of each game is served from the server's right box to the receiver's right box (diagonally opposite). The player who receives first in each game is therefore the one standing on the right, often called the drive side. Points 1, 3, 5 and so on go to the right-side receiver; points 2, 4, 6 go to the left-side receiver, often called the revés side. That is why most pairs simply let the right-side player receive first: it keeps everyone on their usual side. With golden point scoring (one of the options in FIP Rule 1), the deciding point at deuce raises a practical question: which box? On a golden point (or Star Point), the receiving pair chooses whether to receive on the right or the left (FIP Rule 1). The receivers can't swap positions, and in mixed matches the receiver must be the same sex as the server. Correct The server starts every game from the right and alternates boxes each point (Rule 6.5). Partners take turns at their service games in the order set at the start of the set (Rule 6.8). The receiving order is fixed for the set (Rule 8.3). Incorrect Starting a game from the left, serving two points in a row from the same box, or swapping who receives first halfway through a set. These are corrected under Rules 6.7 and 8.4. Real match situations You win the toss You can choose to serve, to receive, to pick an end, or to let your opponents choose (FIP Rule 4). Serving first is the popular choice, but some pairs prefer to receive and pick the end with the better light or the sun behind them. Whatever you choose, your opponents get the other decision. New set, new order You won the first set with your partner serving first in your pair. Before the second set you can change that and serve first yourself, because the order of service is chosen before each set (FIP Rule 6.8). The receiving order can also be decided afresh in the first game of each set (FIP Rule 8.2). Once chosen, both stay fixed for that set. You serve the second point from the right again At 15-0 you serve from the right box instead of the left, and the point is played. When someone notices, you move to the correct side. The point you won stands. If the mistake is spotted after a first-serve fault from the wrong side, that fault still counts and you continue with a second serve from the correct side (FIP Rule 6.7). The receivers swap by accident Your opponents' left-side player receives the first point of a game when it should have been their partner. Under Rule 8.4 the game continues with the altered order until it ends, then the pair goes back to the original receiving order. Points played are not replayed. The wrong partner serves a game Rule 6.9 covers this: the correct player serves as soon as the mistake is noticed, points already played stand, and a single earlier fault is disregarded. If the game had already finished, keep the altered order until the end of the set. In a social match, fix it calmly and keep the score as it is. The tie-break at 6-6 The receiving order is fixed for the tie-break as well (FIP Rule 8.3). FIP Rule 1 sets the serving order: the player due to serve serves the first point from the right; the opposing pair serves the next two, starting from the left; then each player serves two points in turn, following the set's order. The next set is started by the pair that did not serve first in the tie-break. Common misconceptions "The same player in a pair always serves." No. Partners alternate their service games in the fixed order for the set (FIP Rule 6.8). "The order is fixed for the whole match." It is fixed for the set. You can choose again before each new set (FIP Rules 6.8 and 8.2). "Points served from the wrong side are replayed." No. They stand, and the server simply moves to the correct side (FIP Rule 6.7). "A receiving mix-up must be fixed immediately." Under Rule 8.4 you finish the game in the altered order, then revert. "The receiver can choose which box to stand in." "Diagonal" in Rule 3 only defines which opponent receives. Who receives each point is fixed by the receiving order (FIP Rules 8.2 and 8.3) and the alternating boxes (FIP Rule 6.5), wherever the players happen to stand. Social games vs official matches An umpire keeps track of the rotation in official play. In social padel, the simplest system is to fix two habits: the server calls the score before every point, and before every game someone says who is serving.
