If you lose track, work it out from the game count: in the standard rotation, the first server of the set serves games 1, 5 and 9, their partner serves games 3, 7 and 11. Practical tips Choose your serving order with the conditions in mind. If one end has the sun in the server's eyes, the player who handles that better should take the games served from that end. Pairs with one stronger server often put that player first, so they serve games 1, 5 and 9 in a full set. For the technique of the serve itself, see our guides to the serve and return and the waist-height serve rule . For where everyone stands while all this happens, read player positions in padel . Frequently asked questions Which side do you serve from first in padel? Always from the right-hand side at the start of each game, then alternating boxes after every point (FIP Rule 6.5). Do partners take turns serving in padel? Yes. The pairs alternate games, and the two partners alternate their service games in the order chosen for the set (FIP Rule 6.8). Can you change who serves first in a new set? Yes. The order of service is chosen before each set (FIP Rule 6.8), and the receiving order is decided again in the first game of each set (FIP Rule 8.2). What if you serve from the wrong side? It is corrected when noticed. Points already played stand, and a first-serve fault made from the wrong side still counts (FIP Rule 6.7). Who receives the first point of a game? The player the receiving pair chose at the start of the set. That player receives the first serve of every game the pair receives in that set (FIP Rule 8.2). 
##### padel-player-positions-rules
In padel only the server's position is fixed; the receiver, the receiver's partner and the server's partner may stand anywhere on their own side of the net (FIP Rules 3 and 6.1). That freedom is what makes tactics such as the Australian formation legal. The limits come from other rules: where the server's feet go, staying off the opponents' court, and being inside the court when you hit. The short answer Only the server's position is fixed (Rule 6.1: at least one foot behind the service line, inside the service box). The receiver, the receiver's partner and the server's partner may stand anywhere on their own side (Rule 3). The receiver just has to let the serve bounce in the box (Rule 8.1). During the rally you can move anywhere on your side, but you must not touch the net or the opponents' court (FIP Rule 13a), and you must be inside the court when you hit unless out-of-court play is authorised (FIP Rule 13p). What the rules say Rule 3: positions. The rule describes the server and receiver as diagonally opposite, but "diagonal" only defines which opponent receives the serve. Who receives each point is fixed by the receiving order (Rules 8.2 and 8.3) and the alternating service boxes (Rule 6.5). The receiver, the receiver's partner and the server's partner may all stand anywhere on their own side of the court. Rules 6.1 to 6.4: the server's feet. The server stands with at least one foot behind the service line, inside the service box area (between the extension of the central line and the side wall), and stays there until the serve is struck (6.1). The server may not touch the service line or the imaginary extension of the central line with either foot (6.3), and at least one foot must be on the ground at contact (6.4). Rule 8.1: the receiver. The rulebook does not fix a spot for the receiver, but the receiver must let the serve bounce in the receiving box before hitting it. That is why receivers stand back, usually behind the service line, giving the ball room to bounce and come up. Rule 13: the limits during the point. You lose the point if you, your racket or anything you wear or carry touches the net, the posts, the tension cable or any part of the opponents' court while the ball is in play (13a). You lose it if you jump over the net (13n). And you lose it if you hit the ball with one foot, both feet or any part of your body outside the court, unless out-of-court play is authorised (13p, with Rule 16 on when that is possible). Allowed Server's partner at the net on the same half as the server. Receiver's partner at the net or at the back. Both players on the same half of the court during a rally. Crouching at the net in the middle of your own side (FIP Rule 3). Not allowed Server standing on or across the service line or central line (Rule 6.3). Touching the net or stepping into the opponents' court during the point (Rule 13a). Hitting with a foot outside the court when out-of-court play isn't authorised (Rule 13p). The standard positions Player What the rules require Where players usually stand Server Correct box, at least one foot behind the service line, not touching the lines (Rules 6.1, 6.3, 6.5) A metre or so from the side wall or nearer the centre, depending on the serve Server's partner Anywhere on own side (Rule 3) At the net, diagonally opposite the receiver, ready to volley Receiver Anywhere on own side (Rule 3); must let the serve bounce in the box (Rule 8.1) Behind the service line, near the back glass Receiver's partner Anywhere on own side (Rule 3) Around the service line or at the net, watching the server's partner For how these positions evolve once the rally starts, our guide to positioning in doubles goes into detail. The Australian formation The Australian formation is a tactic, not a rule. The server's partner stands at the net on the same half of the court as the server, instead of the usual diagonal position. After the serve, the server covers the other half. The aim is to block the receiver's favourite crosscourt return and force them to play down the line, often into a less comfortable shot. It is completely legal, because Rule 3 lets partners stand anywhere on their own side. The only extra risk is Rule 7d: if the serve hits the server's partner, it is a fault. With your partner standing between you and the net on your half, aim your serve accordingly. A close relative is the "I" formation, where the server's partner crouches near the middle of the net and moves left or right after the serve, sometimes using a pre-agreed signal. This is also legal under Rule 3, as long as the partner stays on their own side and doesn't touch the net (Rule 13a). Real match situations Your partner stands right in front of you at the serve Legal (FIP Rule 3). If your serve clips them before bouncing, though, it is a fault (FIP Rule 7d). Either serve wider of them or ask them to move half a metre. The server's foot is on the central line Fault. The server must not touch the service line or the imaginary extension of the central line with either foot (FIP Rules 6.3 and 7a). The rule applies to the server only; partners can stand where they like. The receiver stands right up at the service line to take the ball early Allowed, as the rulebook does not fix the receiver's spot. What matters is that the receiver lets the serve bounce in the box and hits it before the second bounce (FIP Rule 8.1). Stand too close and you will struggle to let a fast serve bounce and still play it well. Both players end up on the same half during a rally Allowed. Rule 3 places no restriction on partners' positions on their own side. It is usually poor tactics, since it leaves half the court open, but it is never a rule breach. You chase a ball and your foot lands in the opponents' court If this happens while the ball is in play, you lose the point (FIP Rule 13a). Reaching over the net without touching anything is a different question, covered in can you hit the ball on the other side of the net . You run out through the door to hit a ball Only legal if out-of-court play is authorised, which requires the court and safety area to meet the specifications (FIP Rules 16 and 13p). On most club courts it isn't, so hitting from outside loses the point. See out-of-court play for the full rules. Common misconceptions "The server's partner must stand on the opposite half." No rule says so. Partners may stand anywhere on their own side (FIP Rule 3). "The receiver's partner must stay back until the serve is returned." No. They can stand at the net if they like (FIP Rule 3). "The Australian formation is a trick that pros get away with." It is fully within the rules. The only catch is that hitting your own partner with the serve is a fault (FIP Rule 7d). "You can stand anywhere as long as you don't hit the ball." Not quite. Touching the opponents' court is a lost point whether you hit or not (FIP Rule 13a), and a ball from the opponents that touches your body is a lost point (FIP Rule 13k). Rule 13k applies wherever you are standing; it makes no inside/outside distinction. Social games vs official matches Position rules rarely cause arguments in social padel, with two exceptions. First, server foot faults on the central line are almost never called without an umpire. If you want to play properly, agree at the start that the receiving pair can call obvious foot faults. Second, out-of-court play: at most clubs it is not authorised, so agree before playing that the court boundary is the limit. Tactical tip Try the Australian formation for a few points when the receiver is beating you with the same crosscourt return. It changes their picture, and many club players have never practised the down-the-line return. Pair it with a serve to the middle so the angle to the open side is narrow. For more on the serve, read the serve and return guide . Frequently asked questions Is the Australian formation legal in padel? Yes. Rule 3 allows partners to stand anywhere on their own side, including on the same half as the server. Where must the receiver stand? Anywhere on their own side. Only the server's position is fixed (Rule 6.1: at least one foot behind the service line, inside the service box). The receiver, the receiver's partner and the server's partner may stand anywhere on their own side (Rule 3). The receiver just has to let the serve bounce in the box (Rule 8.1). Can the server's partner stand in the service box? The partner may stand anywhere on their own side (FIP Rule 3). Just remember that a serve hitting your partner is a fault (FIP Rule 7d). Can I stand outside the court during a rally? You must be inside the court when you hit unless out-of-court play is authorised (FIP Rule 13p). A ball hit by the opponents that touches your body counts against you (FIP Rule 13k); that rule applies wherever you are standing and makes no inside/outside distinction. Can the server step over the service line? No. The server keeps at least one foot behind the service line and must not touch it or the central line with either foot until the serve is struck (FIP Rules 6.1 and 6.3). 
##### padel-interference-and-hindrance
In padel, hindering an opponent while they are playing a shot costs you the point if it is deliberate, and leads to a let (point replayed) if it is involuntary (FIP Rule 11). A second involuntary hindrance by the same pair also gives the point to the opponents. Separate let rules cover outside interruptions such as a ball rolling in from the next court (FIP Rule 10). The short answer Deliberately hindering an opponent's shot loses you the point; an accidental hindrance means the point is replayed, but a repeat by the same pair loses the point (FIP Rule 11). Outside interruptions, such as a stray ball entering the court, give a let, provided you call it immediately (FIP Rules 10b and 10e). What the rules say Rule 11: interference. Hindrance means a player interfering with an opponent who is executing a shot. The rulebook splits it into two kinds: Deliberate: the point goes to the opponents. Involuntary: a let is called and the point is replayed. If the same pair causes an involuntary hindrance again, the point goes to the opponents. Rule 10: let points. A point is replayed when the ball splits (10a); a foreign object enters the court (10b); play is interrupted by unexpected circumstances unrelated to the players (10c); or the ball hits an object on the opponents' side, such as another ball, and that object moves so that it could interfere or endanger someone (10d). How to claim it. A player who wants a let must tell the umpire immediately. If play continues, the right to the let is lost (10e). And if a let request turns out to be invalid, the player who asked loses the point (10f). That last rule matters: stopping play for a let that isn't one is expensive. Let (point replayed) A ball from the next court rolls in (Rule 10b). The ball splits (10a). An accidental hindrance, the first time for that pair (Rule 11). A ball you hit strikes a stray ball on the opponents' side and sends it moving where it could interfere (10d). Point lost Deliberately hindering an opponent's shot (Rule 11). A second involuntary hindrance by the same pair (Rule 11). Stopping play for a let request that turns out to be invalid (Rule 10f). What counts as hindrance The FIP rulebook defines hindrance by its effect, interfering with an opponent executing a shot, rather than listing specific actions. So the question is always: did something a player did get in the way of the opponent playing the ball? Whether it was deliberate or involuntary is a judgement call for the umpire, or for the players in a social game. The rulebook doesn't specifically cover talking to your partner during a point. It doesn't ban communication, and calls such as "mine", "yours" or "leave it" are a normal part of doubles. The sensible reading is that ordinary communication with your partner is fine, while noise aimed at putting off an opponent as they hit is exactly the kind of thing Rule 11 is there for. Real match situations An opponent shouts as you hit an overhead If the shout is aimed at you and interferes with your shot, it is hindrance under Rule 11. If the umpire judges it deliberate, you win the point. If they judge it involuntary, for example a startled yell, the point is replayed, and a second such incident from the same pair gives you the point. Your partner calls "yours" as the ball crosses Not hindrance. You are talking to your own partner, not interfering with an opponent's shot (Rule 11). The FIP rulebook doesn't address partner communication at all, and it does not prohibit it. A ball from the next court rolls across the back of your court That is a foreign object entering the court, so it is a let (FIP Rule 10b). Call it straight away. If you play on and then complain after losing the rally, you have lost the right to the let (FIP Rule 10e). A spare ball is lying on the opponents' side and your shot hits it If your ball strikes an object on the opponents' ground that is not part of the court, your return is still valid (FIP Rule 14f). But if that object then moves in a way that could interfere with play or endanger someone, the point is a let (FIP Rule 10d). A loose ball sits on your own side during the rally The FIP rulebook doesn't specifically cover a stationary ball on your own side that you might trip over. The sensible club convention is to clear every spare ball off the court or into a pocket before the point starts, because once play is under way you cannot rely on getting a let for it. You stop play claiming the ball split, but it didn't A split ball is a let (FIP Rule 10a). But if the umpire checks and the ball is fine, your request was invalid and you lose the point (FIP Rule 10f). Only stop when you are sure. Your opponent at the net is crowding the middle and you hit them Standing in a position on their own side is not hindrance; Rule 3 lets players stand anywhere on their own side. If your shot hits them, it is a correct return and your point (FIP Rule 14a). For more on that, see what happens if the ball hits you . Common misconceptions "Any noise during a point is a let." No. Rule 11 is about hindering an opponent executing a shot. Outside noise is only a let if it is an unexpected interruption unrelated to the players (FIP Rule 10c), and even then it must be claimed immediately (10e). "You can't talk during a point." The FIP rulebook doesn't ban talking to your partner. Communication is a normal part of doubles. "You can claim a let after the rally finishes." No. If play continues, the right to a let is lost (FIP Rule 10e). "Asking for a let is free." Not if it's invalid. An invalid let request loses the point (FIP Rule 10f). "Accidental hindrance is always just a replay." Only the first time. A second involuntary hindrance by the same pair gives the point to the opponents (FIP Rule 11). Social games vs official matches With an umpire, the umpire decides whether a hindrance was deliberate or involuntary and whether a let request is valid. In social padel the players make those calls, so a few conventions keep things friendly: Call a let the moment it happens, with a raised hand and a clear "let". Waiting to see how the rally goes is exactly what Rule 10e is designed to stop. Give the benefit of the doubt on involuntary hindrance and replay the point, but remember the "second time" part of Rule 11 if one pair keeps doing it. Agree before playing how you treat balls from neighbouring courts. Most clubs replay the point, which matches Rule 10b. Return stray balls to neighbouring courts between their points, not in the middle of a rally, so you don't become their foreign object. Practical tip Keep your communication short and early. "Mine" called as the ball leaves the opponent's racket is clear and harmless. A loud call just as the opponent is hitting is where friendly matches turn sour. Good pairs talk most between points, and for that you have up to 20 seconds (FIP Rule 2). For more on how points are won and lost, read the main rules of padel and our page on lets and net serves . Frequently asked questions Is shouting allowed in padel? The rulebook doesn't ban noise as such. If a shout hinders an opponent executing a shot, Rule 11 applies: deliberate means you lose the point, involuntary means a let. Can you talk to your partner during a point? The FIP rulebook doesn't specifically address it and contains no prohibition. Normal calls between partners are part of the game. What happens if a ball from another court comes onto yours? It is a foreign object entering the court, so the point is replayed (FIP Rule 10b), as long as you claim the let immediately (FIP Rule 10e). What is the difference between a let and hindrance? Hindrance is interference by a player with an opponent's shot (Rule 11). A let is a replayed point, which can result from involuntary hindrance or from the outside events listed in Rule 10. Can you lose a point for asking for a let? Yes. If your let request is invalid, you lose the point (FIP Rule 10f). 
##### padel-match-times-and-warm-up
Under the FIP rules a padel match starts with a 3-minute warm-up, allows a maximum of 20 seconds between points, up to 90 seconds at a change of ends (with no rest after the first game of a set) and up to 120 seconds between sets (FIP Rule 2). The same rule sets the time for injuries, suspensions and a pair that turns up late. Here is every time limit in one place, with what it means on court. The short answer Warm-up: 3 minutes. Between points: 20 seconds. Change of ends: up to 90 seconds, except after the first game of each set, when you change without a rest. End of set: 120 seconds. Tie-break: play is meant to be continuous (Rule 2.5), though Rule 2.10 grants up to 20 seconds for the change. Injury treatment: 3 minutes. A pair not ready 10 minutes after the scheduled start loses by walkover, except where the umpire considers it force majeure (FIP Rule 2.1). What the rules say All the timings in padel live in FIP Rule 2. The principle behind them is simple: play should be continuous, and breaks are limited to specific moments. Situation Time allowed (FIP Rule 2) Pair not ready after the scheduled start Loses by walkover after 10 minutes, except where the umpire considers it force majeure (Rule 2.1) Warm-up (courtesy rally) 3 minutes Between points Maximum 20 seconds; no food or drink Change of ends after the first game of a set No rest; play is continuous (Rule 2.5) Other changes of ends Up to 90 seconds End of a set Up to 120 seconds Change of ends in a tie-break Play is meant to be continuous (Rule 2.5), though Rule 2.10 grants up to 20 seconds Clothing or equipment problem A reasonable time Warm-up after a suspension of up to 5 minutes None Warm-up after a suspension of 5 to 20 minutes 1 minute Warm-up after a suspension of more than 20 minutes 3 minutes Injury or treatable medical condition 3 minutes of treatment; may be repeated at the next two changes of ends; once per condition Accident or sudden illness not caused by play, affecting a player Up to 15 minutes, at the referee's discretion Unusual incidents Up to 5 minutes to recover A suspended match resumes exactly where it stopped: same score, same server, same positions (FIP Rule 2). Rule 17 adds the ball change: if balls are to be changed during the match, it is announced in advance and happens after an established odd number of games (the warm-up counts as two games and a tie-break as one), never at the start of a tie-break, or at the start of a set. Allowed A drink and towel at a change of ends other than after the first game of a set (up to 90 seconds) or between sets (up to 120 seconds). A reasonable pause to fix a broken shoelace or racket cord. A 3-minute treatment for an injury (FIP Rule 2). Not allowed Drinking or eating between points. Taking more than 20 seconds between points. A second 3-minute treatment period for the same condition beyond what Rule 2 allows (FIP Rule 2). Real match situations Your opponents arrive 12 minutes late A pair not ready to play 10 minutes after the start time loses by walkover, except where the umpire considers it force majeure (FIP Rule 2.1). That exception is for the umpire to judge, so a late pair cannot simply claim it. In a club league, check the league's own rules as well. You want to hit a few smashes in the warm-up The warm-up is a 3-minute courtesy rally (FIP Rule 2). The word courtesy is the clue: it is for both pairs to get a feel for the ball, so feed your opponents reasonable balls too. Three minutes goes quickly, so cover groundstrokes, volleys and a few overheads rather than spending it all on one shot. Rain stops play for 12 minutes The suspension is between 5 and 20 minutes, so you get 1 minute of warm-up before restarting (FIP Rule 2). Play resumes exactly where it stopped, so if the score was 30-15 with the second serve to come, that is how the match restarts. A suspension of 4 minutes would give no warm-up; one of 25 minutes would give 3 minutes. Fading light is handled slightly differently: when light fails, Rule 2.13 says the match should be stopped at an even number of games in the set being played. You roll your ankle at 3-2 An injury or treatable medical condition allows 3 minutes of treatment. That treatment may be repeated at the next two changes of ends, and it is allowed once per condition (FIP Rule 2). So you get the initial 3 minutes now and up to two more treatment periods at the following changes of ends, but you cannot claim fresh time for the same ankle later in the match. A spectator collapses next to the court Play stops and the point is replayed (Rule 10c). The 15-minute medical allowance in Rule 2 is only for a player who suffers an accident or sudden illness not caused by play. Your racket safety cord snaps Clothing and equipment problems get a reasonable time to fix (FIP Rule 2). But note the separate point rule: if the cord breaks during a point, your pair immediately loses that point (FIP Rule 13r). For more, see the racket strap rule . Common misconceptions "There is a 5-minute warm-up." The FIP warm-up is 3 minutes (FIP Rule 2). Clubs can be more relaxed in social play, but that is a local choice. "You can sip water between points if you're quick." No. Rule 2 does not allow food or drink between points. "Every change of ends is 2 minutes." A change of ends is up to 90 seconds, and after the first game of a set there is no rest at all. Only the end of a set allows up to 120 seconds (FIP Rule 2). "After a rain delay you start the game again." No. A suspended match resumes exactly where it stopped (FIP Rule 2). "An injury gives you unlimited time." It gives 3 minutes, repeatable at the next two changes of ends, once per condition (FIP Rule 2). How long does a padel match take? The FIP rulebook doesn't set an overall duration for a match. The length depends on the scoring format chosen. Rule 1 allows the standard best of three sets with advantage scoring, and alternatives such as Star Point, golden point (a single deciding point at deuce), 4-game mini sets or, at one set all, a 7-point match tie-break or a 10-point super tie-break in place of the final set (Rule 1). Shorter formats such as golden point or a deciding tie-break make the match length more predictable, which is useful when your court is booked for a fixed slot. For the scoring itself, see padel scoring . Social games vs official matches In official matches the umpire or referee times the breaks and decides on walkovers and medical time. In social padel, the clock that really matters is the court booking. A few habits keep things fair: Agree the warm-up length before you start. Three minutes, as in Rule 2, is a good default when the booking is short. Agree the scoring format, such as golden point or a super tie-break instead of a third set, so you finish within the booking. Keep the 20 seconds between points. Long pauses to chat or check phones are the main reason social matches don't finish. If someone is hurt, stop. Rule 2's injury times are for competition; in a friendly game, health comes before the score. Practical tip Plan your breaks. Changes of ends come after odd games in the set (FIP Rule 5), so you know in advance when you will next be able to drink. In hot conditions, drink at changes of ends other than after the first game of a set, and between sets, even if you don't feel thirsty, because you can't do it between points. The full pattern of when you swap is explained in when do you change sides in padel , and the rest of the rulebook is summarised in the rules of padel . Frequently asked questions How long is the warm-up in padel? 3 minutes, described in the rulebook as a courtesy rally (FIP Rule 2). How much time do you get between points? A maximum of 20 seconds, and no eating or drinking (FIP Rule 2). How long can you rest between sets? Up to 120 seconds at the end of a set. A normal change of ends is up to 90 seconds, but there is no rest at the change after the first game of a set (FIP Rule 2). How late can a team be before losing by walkover? A pair not ready 10 minutes after the start loses by walkover, except where the umpire considers it force majeure (FIP Rule 2.1). How much injury time do you get in padel? 3 minutes of treatment, which may be repeated at the next two changes of ends, once per condition. An accident or sudden illness not caused by play, affecting a player, can allow up to 15 minutes at the referee's discretion (FIP Rule 2). When are the balls changed in padel? Only if announced in advance, and then after an established odd number of games (the warm-up counts as two games and a tie-break as one), never at the start of a tie-break, or at the start of a set (FIP Rule 17). 
##### best-padel-rackets-for-kids
A child playing with an adult racket is like learning to write with a heavy marker: possible, but it slows everything down. The right junior racket is lighter and easier to swing, which means better technique, more fun and less strain on young arms. Here is how to choose one, and five good options for 2026. The short answer For most children under about 10, choose a light, round junior racket of around 300 g or less. From about 10, a junior racket of around 310–330 g works well. Strong teenagers can move to a light adult racket around 12–13. Strength and height matter more than age. How to choose a junior racket HEAD's guide for parents gives a simple starting point, which matches what most coaches recommend: Age (roughly) Racket Typical weight Under 10 Shorter junior racket with a thinner profile About 300 g Over 10 Junior racket a little shorter than an adult one, adult thickness About 330 g Around 12–13 Light adult racket, full length Around 340 g These ages are only a guide. A tall, strong 9-year-old may be ready for a heavier racket; a smaller 12-year-old may not. HEAD also points out that a racket that's too big or heavy increases the risk of injury, and that a coach is the best person to judge the fit. Check the actual weight before you buy. Some rackets labelled "junior" weigh 330–350 g, which is heavy for a younger child. Shape, balance and materials for kids Shape: round is the safest choice for most children. The sweet spot is large and in the centre, so mishits still go over the net. Balance: low or medium. A head-heavy racket is harder to control and tires the arm. Face: fibreglass. It's softer and more forgiving than carbon. Core: soft EVA or foam, for comfort and easy power on slow balls. The official rules don't set separate limits for junior rackets. Junior models simply stay within the adult maximums: no longer than 45.5 cm, no wider than 26 cm, no thicker than 38 mm, and always with the wrist strap, which is compulsory. See our racket strap rule guide . ##### best-padel-rackets-beginner-to-intermediate
Your first racket got you playing. Now you're playing every week, your technique is settling and you want a racket that gives you a little more: better feel, more pop on overheads, a sweet spot that rewards good contact. The trick is to upgrade without jumping to a racket that's too demanding. The short answer Stay with a round or soft teardrop shape and a low to medium balance, but look for better materials: a carbon frame, a quality fibreglass or glass-carbon face and a comfortable EVA core. Keep the weight close to what you're used to, roughly 340–375 g for most adults. Are you ready to upgrade? Upgrade when at least two of these are true: You play at least once a week and can keep a rally going from the back and at the net. You can hit a controlled bandeja and play off the back glass . Your current racket feels "dead" on volleys or lacks pop on overheads. Your racket is worn: cracked face, damaged frame or a core that feels soft and lifeless. If none of those apply, keep your racket and spend the money on lessons. A new racket won't fix technique. What to change, and what not to Two buying guides we checked differ on exact weights: Decathlon suggests under 340 g for beginners and 340–360 g for intermediates, while Central Sports suggests bands about 15–25 g heavier. In practice, most 2026 adult rackets weigh 345–375 g. The safest rule is not to jump more than about 10–15 g heavier than what you use now, especially if you've ever had arm trouble. Keep: a forgiving shape (round or soft teardrop) and a low to medium balance. Upgrade: the materials. A carbon frame adds stability; a better face and core improve feel. Avoid for now: diamond shapes, hard cores and very head-heavy rackets. How to choose between them If you want… Look at Maximum comfort, lighter weight StarVie Nyra, Adidas Cross It Team Light A little more power on overheads (no arm issues) HEAD Radical Team Light Control and stability at the net Bullpadel Ionic Control, Nox ML10 Pro Cup Silver Shape and balance, explained Balance describes where the weight sits along the racket. Low balance (weight towards the handle) makes the racket quicker at the net and easier on the arm. High balance (weight towards the head) adds power on overheads but needs more strength and timing. For players in this stage, low to medium balance is usually the sweet spot. Common upgrade mistakes Buying a pro's racket. Professional models are usually stiffer, heavier in the head and far less forgiving. Going much heavier. A big jump in weight often brings arm trouble. See our guide to padel elbow . Choosing by colour or price alone. Shape, weight and balance matter far more. Never trying before buying. Demo rackets at your club are the best test. Frequently asked questions How long should my first racket last before I upgrade? There's no fixed time. Central Sports suggests replacing a racket after roughly 12–24 months of regular play. Upgrade sooner if it's damaged, or if your game has clearly outgrown it. Should my second racket be carbon? A carbon frame is a sensible upgrade. A full carbon face is stiffer and less forgiving, so many improving players are happier with a fibreglass or mixed face for now. Round or teardrop for an improving player? Round if control and comfort matter most. A soft teardrop if you want a bit more power and your technique is consistent. Is a heavier racket better? Not necessarily. A heavier racket can give more stability and power, but it's slower to move and harder on the arm. Choose the heaviest racket you can swing comfortably for a whole session, not the heaviest you can lift. Not sure where you are yet? ##### best-padel-rackets-for-intermediate-players
Intermediate players have the widest choice of rackets, and the easiest time picking the wrong one. You're strong enough to use more power, but most of your points are still won with control, placement and patience. The right racket rewards that, rather than punishing every off-centre hit. The short answer Most intermediate players are best served by a teardrop or round racket with a medium balance, a medium or soft core, and a weight of roughly 355–375 g. Choose a fibreglass face for comfort, or a carbon face if your technique is solid and you want more power. What "intermediate" means here We mean club players who play regularly, can hold their own at the net, hit bandejas and play off the walls, and are working on consistency and tactics. On many club scales, that's roughly level 3 to 4. If you're still learning the walls, see our beginner-to-intermediate picks . What to look for Shape: teardrop is the classic intermediate choice, mixing control and power. Round still suits control players. Balance: medium. A medium-high balance can work if you play mostly at the net and like to attack. Face: fibreglass for comfort and touch; carbon for stiffness and power. Mixed faces sit in between. Core: soft to medium. Hard cores need clean, fast swings to work well. As a rule of thumb, softer cores and frames are kinder to the arm, while stiffer rackets reward clean technique but forgive less. Your position in the pair can guide the choice. Players on the right (the forehand side for right-handers) often defend more and build the point, so control rackets suit them. Players on the left tend to take more overheads and finishing shots, so a little more balance in the head can help. Left-handers usually play the right side. This is a tendency, not a rule. You'll gain a little power on smashes and lose control on everything else. Ignoring your arm. If you've had elbow or wrist pain, stay with softer cores and medium or low balance. Buying guides differ: Decathlon suggests 340–360 g, Central Sports roughly 365–375 g. Most players do well somewhere around 355–375 g. Choose the weight you can swing comfortably for a full session. Is a teardrop racket good for intermediate players? Yes. It's the most popular shape at this level because it mixes the control of a round racket with some of the power of a diamond. Carbon or fibreglass for an intermediate player? Both work. Fibreglass faces are softer and more forgiving; carbon faces are stiffer and give more power on fast swings. If in doubt, choose a mixed face or a carbon racket with a soft core. How do I know when I'm ready for an advanced racket? When your contact is consistent, you hit overheads with confidence and you want more power without losing control. Our intermediate-to-advanced guide covers that next step. 
##### best-padel-rackets-intermediate-to-advanced
This is the stage where rackets start to reward good technique and punish bad habits. You want more power and precision, usually from a carbon face and a firmer feel, but you still need a racket that forgives the odd mishit in a long rally. These picks are built for exactly that step. The short answer Look for a carbon face, a medium or medium-firm core and a shape that matches your game: round for control, teardrop for balance, diamond only if you attack a lot. Most rackets in this range weigh 355–375 g. Signs you're ready for this step You hit the sweet spot consistently, even on fast balls. You can play bandejas, víboras and smashes with control. You play several times a week or compete in leagues. Your current racket feels too soft: the ball "sinks" on hard hits and you want more response. If you're not there yet, our intermediate picks will serve you better. What changes at this level Carbon faces give a stiffer, more direct response and more power on fast swings, but less help on slow, off-centre hits. Firmer cores give more power on hard shots and less on soft ones. They suit players who swing fast. Balance can move towards medium-high if you finish a lot of points with overheads. The comfort trade-off is real. If you've had arm trouble, choose a round or teardrop racket with a medium core rather than a hard diamond. Getting used to a stiffer racket Moving from a soft, forgiving racket to a carbon one takes a few sessions. The ball comes off the face faster and you'll feel mishits more. Start with drills rather than matches: volley rallies, controlled bandejas and lobs from the back glass. Shorten your swing on volleys at first, and let the racket do more of the work on overheads. If your arm feels sore after the first week, go back to your old racket for some sessions and build up gradually. Test before you commit Play a full session with a demo racket, not just a few rallies. Test your weakest shots , such as low volleys and balls off the back glass, not just smashes. Check your arm the next day. Stiffer rackets can take some getting used to. Compare with your current racket in the same session. Frequently asked questions Do I need a diamond racket to improve? No. Diamonds add power on overheads, but many strong players use round or teardrop rackets because they're more consistent across all shots. Is a full carbon racket bad for the elbow? Not automatically, but stiffer faces and hard cores transmit more of the impact. If you're prone to arm pain, choose a softer core and avoid a very head-heavy balance. What weight is right at this level? Most rackets here weigh 355–375 g. Pick a weight you can swing at full speed for a whole match without your technique breaking down. When should I move to an advanced racket? When you play at a high level regularly and want maximum power or precision. See our advanced picks . 
##### best-padel-rackets-for-advanced-players
At an advanced level, the racket becomes a precision tool. You hit the sweet spot almost every time, so you can choose a stiffer, more powerful frame, and small differences in balance and feel really matter. Here are five top rackets for 2026 and how to choose between them. The short answer Advanced players usually choose a carbon racket with a medium-firm to hard core. Pick a diamond with a high balance if you finish points with overheads, or a teardrop if you want power with more control. Most top models weigh 360–375 g. Who these rackets are for These are demanding rackets for players who play often, compete, and have consistent technique on every shot: volleys, bandejas , víboras and smashes . If you're still building that consistency, an advanced racket will make padel harder, not easier. Our intermediate-to-advanced picks are a better fit. What matters at this level Shape and balance define the racket's character. Diamond and high balance mean more power on overheads; teardrop and medium balance mean more control. Carbon type: brands use 3K, 12K, 18K, 24K and other carbons. The number is how many thousand carbon filaments are in each strand (3K = 3,000). Higher numbers usually feel firmer, but the whole build decides the feel. Core hardness: hard cores give maximum power on fast swings but less comfort; medium cores are more versatile. Adjustable weights: some 2026 models let you fine-tune balance with small counterweights. Comparison Racket Character Best for Bullpadel Vertex 05 Power, medium-hard feel Left-side attackers Nox AT10 Genius 18K Alum Control and precision All-court players HEAD Coello Pro Maximum power, head-heavy Net dominators Babolat Technical Viper 3.0 Hard, direct, powerful Fast-swinging attackers StarVie Black Titan Hard, attacking, a little easier Power with control Shape for advanced players Even at the top level, plenty of players choose a teardrop or round racket. As a general tendency, players on the right side build points, so control often wins them more matches than raw power; players on the left finish more points with overheads, so a diamond can make more sense. Power or control? A quick self-test How do you win most of your points? If it's with overheads and smashes, lean towards power. If it's by forcing errors with placement, lean towards control. Which side do you play? Left-side players tend to want more power; right-side players tend to want control. It is a tendency, not a rule. How is your arm? Any history of elbow or wrist pain points towards a medium core and a less head-heavy balance. How often do you play? Hard, head-heavy rackets suit players who train often enough to keep their timing sharp. Looking after a high-end racket Use edge protector tape to protect the frame from scrapes on the glass and floor. Keep it out of hot cars , which can affect the core and face. Replace the overgrip often for a secure, consistent feel. Replace the racket when it loses its response. Central Sports suggests roughly every 12–24 months of regular play. Frequently asked questions What weight do advanced players use? Most advanced rackets weigh around 360–375 g. Guides like Decathlon's suggest over 360 g for advanced players, but the right weight depends on your strength and swing speed. Is a harder core always better for advanced players? No. Hard cores give more power on fast shots, but less on slow ones and less comfort. Many advanced players prefer a medium core for versatility. Do pros use the same rackets you can buy? Brands sell signature models named after their players, but professional players may use customised versions. Treat the shop model as the same design, not necessarily an identical racket. Should I buy the 2027 model? Only if the changes matter to you. The 2026 versions of many top rackets are now discounted and remain excellent. 
##### how-to-play-padel
How to play padel, in one sentence: you and a partner rally against another pair on an enclosed court, serving underarm and letting the ball bounce once on your side before you hit it back, off the walls if you like. That's the whole game in one sentence. This guide takes you from that sentence to your first proper match, using the official rules of the International Padel Federation (FIP). What is padel, in thirty seconds Padel is a racket sport that mixes tennis-style scoring with squash-style walls. It was invented in 1969 in Acapulco, Mexico, by Enrique Corcuera, who built a smaller, walled court at his home because he didn't have room for a tennis court, according to the FIP. It spread to Spain and Argentina in the 1970s and has been growing worldwide ever since. Our full explainer, what is padel , covers the history and how big the sport has become. You need four things to play: a court, four players, a padel racket each, and padel balls. You don't need to be fit or experienced. Most people manage short rallies in their first hour. The court and the equipment A padel court is 20 metres long and 10 metres wide, fully enclosed by glass and metal mesh. The net is 88 cm high in the middle and 92 cm at the posts. On each side, a service line runs across the court 6.95 metres from the net, and a centre line splits that area into two service boxes. Those lines only matter on the serve. See padel court dimensions for every measurement. Racket: solid, with no strings, a perforated face and a maximum length of 45.5 cm. It has a wrist cord, and the rules make wearing it compulsory. Our guide to choosing a padel racket helps if you're buying your first one. Ball: it looks like a tennis ball but is slightly smaller, with a little less pressure. Shoes: court shoes with a sole suited to the surface. See our guide to padel shoes. Padel how to play: the basic rules Here is how a point works, from first serve to last bounce. Serve underarm. Stand with at least one foot behind the service line, bounce the ball behind the line, and hit it at or below waist height, with at least one foot on the ground. Serve diagonally. The ball must land in the service box diagonally opposite. You start each game from the right and switch boxes after every point. Like tennis, you get two attempts. Let the serve bounce. The receiver must let the serve bounce before returning it. After that, anyone can volley. One bounce, then anything goes. Once the ball has bounced on your side, it can hit any wall or the mesh and you can still play it, as long as you hit it before it bounces a second time on the floor. Bounce first on their side. Your shot must land on the opponents' floor before it touches their walls or mesh. If it hits their glass directly, you lose the point. Your own glass is allowed. You may hit the ball into your own glass walls so it rebounds over the net, as long as it then bounces in the opponents' court. Your own mesh isn't allowed. Allowed The ball bounces on your side, comes off the back glass, and you return it before it bounces again. Point lost Your shot hits the opponents' wall or mesh before bouncing, you touch the net, or the ball bounces twice on your side. A few more rules worth knowing on day one. You lose the point if you, your racket or your clothing touches the net or posts while the ball is in play, or if the ball hits you or your partner. A serve that clips the net and lands in the right box is replayed, unless it then touches the mesh before its second bounce. And on the serve only, a ball that bounces in the box and then hits the mesh is a fault, while the glass is fine. Our padel rules guide covers everything else. Padel for beginners: rules that confuse everyone Three situations catch out almost every new player: Waiting for the glass. Tennis instinct says a deep ball is gone. In padel it's coming back. Let it bounce, come off the glass and drop, then play it. Practising off the glass is one of the LTA's three tips for first-timers for exactly this reason. The mesh behaves differently. The ball dies or flies at odd angles off the metal fence. It's legal in a rally after the bounce, just less predictable. Volleying the return. You can't volley the serve, but from the return onwards you can volley everything. Padel how to score Padel uses tennis scoring. Points go love, 15, 30, 40, game. A set is first to six games with a two-game lead, with a tie-break at 6–6, and a match is usually best of three sets. Pairs change ends after the first game and then after every odd-numbered game. The one twist is what happens at 40–40. The FIP rulebook offers three options: classic advantage, the golden point (one deciding point), and the Star Point, which the professional tour adopted in 2026. Agree which you're playing before the first serve. Our padel scoring guide explains all three, plus tie-breaks and super tie-breaks. Who serves when? One player serves a whole game, then the serve passes to the other pair, then to the first server's partner, then to the remaining player. At the start of each set, the receiving pair decides which of them receives the first serve of every game, and that order stays fixed for the whole set. Padel doubles or singles? Padel is a doubles sport. The FIP rules are written for pairs throughout, and nearly every court and club game is two against two. That's part of the appeal: four people share a small court, the ball stays in play, and the social side comes built in. Singles does exist. Some venues build narrow singles courts, the same 20 metres long but only 6 metres wide, though they're rare: in 2024 The Padel Paper counted only six publicly accessible ones in the UK. You can also play one against one on a half court as practice, which is a common drill. For the details, see our guide to padel singles . How to play padel well: the beginner's game plan Knowing the rules gets you through a match. These habits help you win one. Keep the ball in play Most beginner points end with an error, not a winner. The LTA's first-session advice is simply to get the ball over the net and into the court, with less power and more control. Hit through the middle, aim deep, and let the other pair make the mistake. Move as a pair You and your partner should be level: both at the net, or both at the back. When one of you is up and the other back, the diagonal gap between you is the easiest target on court. See where to stand in doubles . Want the net The pair at the net usually wins the point. From the back, a deep lob over the net players is your way forward. At the net, stand about two to three metres back, racket up, so you can volley and still cover the lob. Prepare early Racket up, body turned sideways as the ball comes. The Padel School lists late preparation among the most common beginner mistakes, and fixing it improves consistency quickly. Talk Call "mine" or "yours" on every ball down the middle, and agree before the match who takes lobs and middle balls. Your first session, step by step Warm up for a few minutes: light movement first, then arm and shoulder swings. Before a match the rules require a three-minute warm-up rally. Rally cross-court in pairs, just keeping the ball going. Feed each other balls off the back glass. Practise the serve and the return. Play games, scoring as you go. A lesson with a qualified coach shortens the learning curve a lot, especially on the walls. Group beginner sessions are a good way to meet people to play with, too. Frequently asked questions Is padel easy to learn? Yes. The underarm serve, small court and walls mean most beginners can rally within their first session. Learning to play off the glass takes longer, and that's where most of the improvement happens. Can you hit the ball off the walls in padel? Yes, after it has bounced on your side. You may also hit it into your own glass so it rebounds over the net, as long as it then bounces in the opponents' court before touching their walls. How many players do you need for padel? Four is standard: two pairs. You can practise with two, and some venues have narrow singles courts, but the rules and most courts are designed for doubles. Is the padel serve overarm? No. The serve is always underarm: bounce the ball behind the service line and hit it at or below waist height, diagonally into the opposite box. How long does a game of padel last? It depends on the format. A best-of-three-sets match with advantage scoring can run well over an hour, which is why many leagues use the golden point or a super tie-break to keep matches shorter. Do I need my own racket to start? Not usually. Many clubs rent rackets and sell balls. Buy your own once you know you'll keep playing. 
##### what-is-padel
What is padel? It's a racket sport, usually played as doubles, on a smaller, enclosed court where the glass walls are part of the game. You serve underarm, score like tennis, and let the ball come off the walls like squash. It's quick to learn, very social, and one of the fastest-growing sports in the world. What is padel sport? The short answer Padel is a racket sport for four players, two on each side of a net, played on a 20 × 10 metre court that is completely enclosed by glass walls and metal mesh. The ball can bounce off the walls and stay in play, which makes rallies long and gives the game its own tactics. The official rules are set by the International Padel Federation (FIP), founded in Madrid in 1991. If you've never seen it, picture doubles tennis played inside a glass box, with solid rackets and an underarm serve. What is padel tennis? How the game works You'll sometimes hear padel called "padel tennis", because the scoring and the basic shots come from tennis. The main rules: The serve is underarm. You bounce the ball behind the service line and hit it at or below waist height, diagonally into the opposite service box. You get two attempts. One bounce per side. After the ball bounces on your side, it can hit the walls or mesh and you can still return it, as long as you hit it before it bounces twice. Your shot must land first. The ball has to bounce on the opponents' floor before touching their walls. You can use your own glass. Hitting the ball into your own glass so it rebounds over the net is legal, provided it then bounces on the other side. Tennis scoring. 15, 30, 40, game; sets to six games; best of three sets. At 40–40 you play advantage, a golden point or the Star Point, depending on the event. For the full picture, read how to play padel or our plain-English rules guide . What is padel game equipment? Item What it is Court 20 m × 10 m, net 88 cm in the middle and 92 cm at the posts, enclosed by glass and mesh Racket Solid and stringless, with holes in the face; maximum length 45.5 cm; wrist cord compulsory Ball Like a tennis ball but slightly smaller, 6.35–6.77 cm in diameter Players Almost always four: two pairs Where padel comes from According to the FIP, padel was invented in 1969 at Las Brisas in Acapulco, Mexico, by Enrique Corcuera. He didn't have room for a tennis court at his home, so he built a smaller one enclosed by walls. That became the first padel court. In the early 1970s Prince Alfonso of Hohenlohe took the game to Spain, building courts at the Marbella Club, and an Argentine friend of his, Julio Menditeguy, introduced it in Argentina. Spain and Argentina became the sport's two great strongholds, and most padel vocabulary, from bandeja to víbora, is Spanish. The FIP itself was founded on 12 July 1991 by the Argentine, Spanish and Uruguayan associations. It now has more than 100 national federations. How big is padel? Estimates vary with who's counting and how they define a player, so treat these as ranges rather than exact numbers: Playtomic's Global Padel Report 2026 , produced with Strategy&, counts 19.4 million players, 58,300 courts and nearly 20,000 clubs worldwide. The FIP's own World Padel Report 2026 puts the figure at around 40 million amateur players in 191 countries and territories, with more than 91,000 courts, and says women make up 47% of participants. Whichever number you take, padel has grown very quickly. Playtomic's report says nearly 22 new courts opened every day around the world in 2025. Is padel popular in the US? It's growing fast from a small base. The Sports & Fitness Industry Association (SFIA) included padel in its participation report for the first time in 2026, and its data shows about 1,073,000 Americans played padel in 2025. Of those, 238,000 played eight or more times. The United States Padel Association (USPA) says just over 1,000 courts were open across 31 states as of April 2026. Playtomic's report says the US added 250 new clubs in 2025, with growth concentrated in Florida, Texas, California and some East Coast cities. The USPA expects the country could reach 10,000 courts and more than 10 million players by 2030. That's a projection, not a fact, but it shows how quickly the sport is expanding from a small base. Is padel the same as pickleball? No, although people mix them up. Both are social, beginner-friendly and usually played as doubles. The differences are big: Walls: padel is played inside glass walls that are part of the game. Pickleball courts are open. Ball and racket: padel uses a pressurised rubber ball and a thick, perforated racket. Pickleball uses a light plastic ball with holes and a flat paddle. Scoring: padel uses tennis scoring; pickleball games go to 11. The kitchen: pickleball has a non-volley zone next to the net. Padel doesn't; you can volley right at the net as long as you don't touch it. Our full comparison, padel vs pickleball , goes through every difference. Is padel an Olympic sport? Not yet. As of October 2026, padel is not on the Olympic programme. The FIP is campaigning for padel to be included at the Brisbane 2032 Olympic Games, and renewed that call when it declared 20 October World Padel Day in 2026. Padel has, however, been part of several major multi-sport events: the 2023 European Games and, in 2026, the South American, Mediterranean and Asian Games. The Olympic Council of Asia confirmed padel as an official sport at the Aichi-Nagoya 2026 Asian Games in November 2025. How to pronounce padel In English, Dictionary.com gives PAD-uhl , with the stress on the first syllable, so it sounds like "paddle". That's also how it's usually said in the UK. In Spanish the word is written pádel , and the accent on the a puts the stress on the first syllable too: PA-del , with a short, open a and a clear e. You'll also hear pa-DEL , with the stress on the second syllable, from many players in the US. Nobody will correct you on court, whichever you choose. Just don't spell it "paddle": paddle tennis is a different game. Why people love it It's easy to start. Underarm serve, small court, short rallies from the first session. It's social. Four players, a small court and plenty of talking. It rewards brains over power. The walls keep the ball in play, so positioning and patience win more points than hitting hard. Mixed levels work. A stronger and a weaker player can pair up and still have a good game. Frequently asked questions What is padel in simple terms? A doubles racket sport on a small, enclosed court, where the ball can bounce off the walls. It uses tennis scoring and an underarm serve. Is padel a type of tennis? It's a separate sport with its own federation and rules, though it borrows tennis scoring and many tennis strokes. The walls make it play very differently. See padel vs tennis . Is padel an Olympic sport? No. As of October 2026 it isn't on the Olympic programme. The FIP is campaigning for inclusion at Brisbane 2032. Where was padel invented? In Acapulco, Mexico, in 1969, by Enrique Corcuera, according to the FIP. Can you play padel with two people? Yes, for practice or on a narrow singles court, but the sport is designed for doubles and nearly all matches are two against two. 
##### padel-vs-tennis
Padel vs tennis comes down to one thing: the walls. Padel is played as doubles on a smaller, enclosed court where the ball stays in play off the glass, with an underarm serve and solid rackets; tennis is open, bigger and built around power. Tennis players pick padel up fast, but the habits that win tennis points often lose padel ones. Here's what's different and how to adapt. Padel vs tennis at a glance Padel Tennis Court 20 × 10 m, enclosed by glass and mesh 23.77 m long; 8.23 m wide for singles, 10.97 m for doubles; open Walls In play after the bounce None: out is out Net 88 cm in the middle, 92 cm at the posts About 91 cm in the middle Racket Solid, stringless, perforated; max 45.5 cm; wrist cord compulsory Strung, longer Serve Underarm, after a bounce, at or below waist height Usually overarm, a major weapon Format Almost always doubles Singles and doubles Scoring Tennis scoring, plus golden point or Star Point options at 40–40 Standard tennis scoring The walls change everything In tennis, a ball that gets past you is gone. In padel, once it bounces on your side it can come off the back or side glass and you can still play it, as long as you hit it before the second bounce. You can even hit the ball into your own glass to get it back over the net. The only limit is that your shot has to bounce on the opponents' floor before it touches their walls. The result is longer rallies, fewer outright winners and a game decided far more by position and patience than by power. Hit a tennis-style drive deep and hard, and a decent padel player simply lets it come off the glass and plays it back. Padel ball vs tennis ball They look nearly identical, but they're not the same ball. Padel ball (FIP) Tennis ball (ITF) Diameter 6.35–6.77 cm 6.54–6.86 cm Mass 56.0–59.4 g 56.0–59.4 g Rebound from 2.54 m 135–145 cm 135–147 cm Internal pressure Slightly lower Slightly higher The padel ball is a little smaller and has slightly less internal pressure, which makes it a touch slower and easier to control in a small space. Can you use tennis balls for padel? For a casual knock, yes. For proper play, use padel balls: tennis balls bounce and fly differently off the glass. Is padel easier than tennis? To start, yes. The underarm serve removes tennis's biggest barrier, the court is smaller, and the walls give you a second chance. Babolat's comparison notes that complete beginners can often exchange 10 to 20 shots in their first padel sessions, while in tennis it can take months to rally properly. To master, no. Padel's difficulty moves from technique to decisions: when to let the ball come off the glass, when to lob, when to attack and where to stand with your partner. The Padel School puts it well: early progress can come quickly, but tactical understanding takes longer. Padel for tennis players: what transfers Coaches at The Padel School list the strengths tennis players typically bring: Volleys and reactions at the net. Clean ball striking and comfort with difficult balls. Power , which becomes useful once it's controlled. The split step , which coaches call crucial for preparation and movement. And what typically goes wrong: avoiding the walls, rushing the net, struggling to control the smash, and going for drop shots and winners too early. Padel tips for tennis players Use one grip. Padel coaching generally teaches the continental grip for forehands, backhands, volleys and overheads, so you never have to change grip in a fast exchange. It also makes slice and back-glass play easier. See our guide to the padel grip . Shorten the swing. A full tennis take-back is too slow in a 10 × 10 m half and sends the ball into the back glass. Compact, controlled swings win. Swap big drives for control and slice. The Padel School points out that a flat ball comes up higher after it hits the back glass, giving the opponents an easier ball, while slice keeps it low. Let the ball come to you. Practise letting deep balls come off the back glass instead of rushing to take them early. Learn to love the lob. In tennis the lob is an emergency shot. In padel it's how you win back the net. See our lob guide. Don't smash everything. A big smash often bounces off the back glass back to the opponents. Learn the bandeja to keep the net instead. Get padel coaching early. The Padel School's main advice for tennis players is to stop treating padel as a smaller version of tennis, and to fix positioning and wall play before tennis habits set in. Padel rules for tennis players Most rules will feel familiar. These are the ones that differ: Serve: underarm, after a bounce behind the service line, struck at or below the waist with at least one foot on the ground. A serve that bounces in the box and then hits the mesh is a fault; the glass is fine. No lines in the rally: the lines only matter on the serve. During the rally, the walls are the boundary. Walls: legal after the ball bounces on your side, and your own glass is usable to get the ball back over. Touching the net: lose the point, same as tennis, and the same applies to the posts. Scoring at deuce: check whether you're playing advantage, golden point or Star Point. See padel scoring . Wrist cord: compulsory, not optional. Calories: padel vs tennis There's no single honest number for either sport, because energy use depends on your weight, your level and how hard the game is. Here's what the research supports. The 2024 Adult Compendium of Physical Activities, the standard reference researchers use, has no entry for padel. For tennis it lists 4.5 to 6.0 METs for doubles and 8.0 METs for singles. A MET is a multiple of your resting energy use; roughly, calories per hour equal METs multiplied by your weight in kilograms. For a 70 kg player that's about 315–420 kcal an hour for doubles tennis and about 560 kcal for singles. For padel, studies summarised in the Aspetar Sports Medicine Journal report heart rates of roughly 50–70% of maximum (approaching 70% in amateur matches) and oxygen uptake below 45% of maximum: mostly moderate intensity, though the same review notes it can be more intense than doubles tennis. A fast, competitive padel match will burn more; a relaxed social game, less. Our padel calories guide goes into the evidence in more detail. Which should you play? Choose padel if you want quick progress, long rallies, a social game and a sport where tactics beat power. Choose tennis if you love singles, open space and big serves. Plenty of people play both, and the skills help in each direction, as long as you treat them as separate sports. Frequently asked questions Is padel easier than tennis? Easier to start, thanks to the underarm serve, smaller court and walls. Harder to master tactically, because the walls create many more options. Can tennis players play padel straight away? Yes, and they usually progress fast at first. The common hurdles are avoiding the glass, over-hitting and rushing the net. Is a padel ball the same as a tennis ball? No. It's the same weight but slightly smaller, with slightly lower pressure, so it plays a little slower. Does padel burn more calories than tennis? Not according to the available evidence. Recreational padel is mostly moderate intensity, in the range of doubles tennis or slightly above; singles tennis is usually more demanding. Your weight and the pace of the game matter more than the sport. Is padel harder on the body than tennis? It's different. Padel involves lots of short bursts, lunges, rotations and overheads. Build your playing time gradually, especially if you're coming from little or no racket sport, and see a qualified professional for persistent pain. 
##### padel-levels-explained
Padel levels explained in one line: there's no single official scale. Most systems run from about 0 or 1 (complete beginner) up to 7 (elite), but booking apps, national federations and clubs each use their own version, and the same number can mean different things. Here's how the main scales work, how they compare, and how to work out your own level honestly. Padel levels explained: why they matter Padel is a doubles game played in a small space, so a mismatch ruins it for everyone. If three players are rallying comfortably and the fourth can't return a serve, nobody has fun. That's why booking apps, clubs and leagues ask for your level before you join an open match, a social night or a tournament. A level is simply a shorthand for "who should I be playing with?" The catch: different organisations use different scales. Always check which one a club or app is using before you pick a number. Padel level rating: the main systems Playtomic: 0 to 7 Playtomic, the booking app many players meet first, uses a scale from 0 to 7, with 7 the highest. You get a starting level by answering a short questionnaire about your experience when you create your profile, and you can only do that once. After that, the level changes automatically based on competitive match results: the result itself, your opponents' levels, your partner's level and your "reliability". Reliability is the key idea. New players have low reliability, so their level moves a lot after each match; as you play more competitive matches, it settles down. Beating higher-rated players lifts you more, and losing to lower-rated players drops you more. Friendly matches don't count. A levelling assessment at a certified Playtomic Leveling Club can set your reliability to 50% straight away. LTA (Great Britain): 1.0 to 7.0 The LTA uses a self-rating scale from 1.0 to 7.0 in half-point steps, except that 1.5 isn't used. Ratings are grouped into three bands: beginner (1.0, 2.0, 2.5, 3.0), intermediate (3.5 to 5.0) and advanced (5.5 to 7.0). You pick the statement that best describes your game. The advanced band is tied to competition: 5.5 is for nationally ranked players, and 6.0 to 7.0 depend on professional rankings. World Padel Rating (US): 0 to 21 In July 2024 the United States Padel Association (USPA) announced World Padel Rating (WPR) as its official rating system. WPR uses a wider scale, from 0 to 21, and tracks a confidence level for the accuracy of each player's rating. Padel levels ABCD: letter categories Many clubs and tournaments group players into letter categories rather than numbers. In this system D is the lowest and A, or "Open", the highest. One Australian rating guide maps ratings to categories like this: Rating band Description Typical category 1.0–2.5 Beginner D to C 3.0–4.0 Intermediate C to B 4.5–5.5 Advanced B to Open 6.0–7.0 Expert Open Spanish-speaking clubs often use numbered categories instead, and here the order is reversed: a lower number means a better player. In one Spanish club tournament guide, 4ª is for beginners and players who aren't yet consistent, 3ª for intermediate players who keep the ball in play at medium and high pace, and 2ª for advanced players who control direction, pace and depth on both sides. Category systems vary from club to club, so always read the local definitions. Padel level chart: what each stage looks like Playtomic describes seven stages of a padel player's development on its 0 to 7 scale, shown in its help pages as a chart from the bottom of the scale to the top. Here they are in plain English, alongside our rough guide to the matching LTA band. Treat that column as a guide, not an official conversion. Stage What it looks like Roughly LTA Beginner Little racket-sport experience; needs basic lessons first Beginner Learning Just started padel; struggles with the walls; no clear difference between attack and defence Beginner Developing Getting comfortable; stays near the back, makes unforced errors, starting to use the walls Beginner to intermediate Coordinated Moves fluidly, uses the walls as part of the game, comes to the net more, still makes execution and positioning errors Intermediate Solid Good fundamentals, uses the walls regularly, knows attack from defence, fewer unforced errors Intermediate Advanced Moves smoothly between attack and defence, reads opponents, adapts during matches Advanced Top tier Competes in federation tournaments Advanced Padel level 2 and level 3: what do they mean? Because scales differ, "level 2" and "level 3" mean slightly different things in different places. In practice, on the 0–7 and 1–7 scales most clubs use: Level 2 is a beginner who can rally a little and serve legally but still finds the walls confusing. Playing off the back glass is the skill that moves you on. Level 3 is a developing player who rallies at a moderate pace, starts to use the glass and knows the basic positions, but still makes plenty of unforced errors. On the LTA scale, 3.0 is the top of the beginner band and 3.5 the start of intermediate. The jump to level 4 usually comes from three things: regular volleys at the net, a reliable bandeja , and moving with your partner instead of on your own. What padel level am I? A quick self-check Answer honestly. It's common to rate yourself a little too high. Can you rally ten shots in a row cross-court at a gentle pace? If not, you're at the very start of the scale. Can you return a ball that has come off the back glass, most of the time? If yes, you're past the beginner stage. Do you volley at the net and lob to win the net back, rather than staying at the back all game? Can you hit a controlled bandeja when lobbed, and keep your net position? Do you and your partner move together, and can you change the pace and direction of the ball on purpose? Do you win matches in local leagues or tournaments against players rated at your level? Each yes moves you up roughly a stage. If you're unsure between two levels, pick the lower one: playing up feels better than spoiling a game. Better still, book a levelling assessment at a certified Playtomic Leveling Club. It gives you an outside view and a more stable starting rating. How to move up a level Play competitive matches on the app or in a league, not just friendlies, so your rating reflects your game. Play some matches with stronger players. On rating systems like Playtomic's, beating higher-rated opponents counts for more. Train one skill at a time. Our guide on how to get better at padel sets out a plan, and the beginner drills give you the practice. Cut the errors. At most club levels, the pair that misses less wins. See common padel mistakes . Frequently asked questions Is there an official padel level scale? No single one. Booking apps such as Playtomic, national federations such as the LTA and rating platforms such as WPR each use their own scale. Most run from about 0 or 1 to 7, but WPR goes to 21. What is a good padel level? On a 0–7 or 1–7 scale, the middle of the range is a solid club player who uses the walls, volleys and moves with a partner. The top of most scales is reserved for ranked tournament players: on the LTA scale, 5.5 and above. Is category A or D better in padel? A, or Open, is the highest; D is the lowest. In numbered Spanish categories it's reversed: a lower number means a better player. How does my Playtomic level go up? By winning competitive matches, especially against higher-rated players. Friendly matches don't change your level, and new players' levels move more because their reliability is low. Should I put myself in a lower level? If you're between two levels, yes. Starting a little low and moving up is better for everyone than starting too high. 
##### padel-beginner-mistakes
The most common padel mistakes all have the same root: playing padel like tennis. Beginners hit too hard, run away from the glass, stand in the wrong place and try to win points that the walls will simply hand back. The good news is that each one has a simple fix, and fixing even three of them will win you more games than any new shot. Why common padel mistakes happen Padel looks simple, and in many ways it is. But the walls change what a good shot is. A hard, deep drive that wins the point in tennis comes off the back glass in padel and sits up for the opponents. A ball you'd chase in tennis comes back to you in padel if you wait. Almost every mistake below comes from instincts that work in other sports and don't work here. Coaches agree on the list remarkably well. The mistakes below appear again and again in coaching advice from The Padel School and the LTA. Mistakes with the ball 1. Hitting too hard The number one padel beginner mistake. Power sends the ball long into the glass, where it rebounds nicely for the opponents, or straight out of the court. The Padel School lists hitting too hard first among the most common tactical errors, and one of the LTA's three beginner tips is less power and more control. Fix: play at about 70% pace and aim for placement: deep, low and through the middle. Save real power for high balls near the net. 2. Going for winners too early Most beginner points end in errors, not winners. If you go for the line from the back of the court, you'll lose more points than you win. Fix: make it your job to put one more ball back than the other pair. Wait for a short, high ball before you attack. 3. Never lobbing Tennis players treat the lob as a last resort. In padel it's the main way to get the opponents off the net and move forward yourself. Fix: when both opponents are at the net and you're at the back, lob deep over them, then move up with your partner. See our lob guide. 4. Smashing at the wrong time Smashing while moving backwards, or smashing every high ball, usually ends with the ball bouncing off the glass and coming back. The Padel School's rule of thumb: only smash when you know you can regain your net position. Fix: on lobs you can't attack comfortably, play a controlled bandeja and stay at the net, or let the ball come off the back glass. Keep the full smash for balls you can hit in front of you. Mistakes with the walls 5. Running away from the glass Many beginners hit the ball before it reaches the back wall because they're nervous of it. That means hitting low, rushed shots from awkward positions. Fix: let the ball bounce, come off the glass and drop, then play it. Getting comfortable near the glass is uncomfortable at first; it's still the skill that separates beginners from improvers. Our back glass guide breaks it down. 6. Preparing late off the glass The Padel School's back-glass advice: the racket should go back as the ball is about to hit the glass, not after it comes out. Wait for the rebound and you're always late. Fix: turn side-on and take the racket back at the height of the ball as it travels towards the wall. Low ball, low racket; high ball, high racket. 7. Hitting the ball behind your body Crowding the wall, or standing too close to the ball's path, means contact happens beside or behind you. Fix: give yourself more space than feels natural, then step in so you meet the ball in front of your body. Mistakes with position 8. Standing in no man's land The area around the service line is the hardest place on court to play from. A ball at your feet there is almost impossible to attack. Fix: be either at the net or at the back. At the back, start about a step behind the service line. At the net, stand about two to three metres from it. Pass through the middle, but don't stay there. 9. Standing too close to the net Hugging the net feels aggressive but leaves you open to every lob. Fix: stay about two to three metres back, racket up. You can still step forward into volleys, and you can cover the lob. See our volley guide. 10. Not moving with your partner One player at the net, one at the back: the diagonal gap between you is the easiest target in padel. Fix: imagine a rope between you. If your partner moves forward, so do you; if they're lobbed back, you go back too. If they shift left, you shift left. More in padel positioning for doubles . Mistakes with preparation and teamwork 11. Racket down, feet flat Beginners often wait with the racket by their knees and their weight on their heels. By the time the ball arrives, there's no time to prepare. Fix: racket up in front of you, knees soft, weight on the balls of your feet. Use short side steps and stay on your toes, as the LTA recommends. Turn sideways early as the ball comes. 12. Not talking Silent pairs leave balls down the middle or collide going for them. Fix: call every shared ball: "mine", "yours", "go". Agree before the match who takes lobs and balls down the middle. Two rule mistakes beginners make Legal Hitting the ball into your own glass so it rebounds over the net and bounces on the other side. Volleying any ball except the serve. Point lost Your shot hits the opponents' glass before bouncing. Touching the net with your racket or body. Letting the ball hit you. Volleying the serve. Also: the wrist cord is compulsory, and a serve that bounces in the box and then hits the mesh is a fault. Our rules guide covers the rest. Padel tips for beginners: fix them in practice You can't fix twelve things at once. Pick one per session, which is also the LTA's advice for practice. For hitting too hard: play a game where any ball that hits the back glass without bouncing loses you two points.
