For wall fear: have a partner throw balls at the floor just in front of the back glass, and return each one softly. For consistency: rally cross-court and count. Try for ten in a row, then fifteen, then twenty. For position: after every shot in a practice match, check: are we level? Our padel drills for beginners turn each of these into a structured drill. Frequently asked questions What is the most common padel mistake? Hitting too hard. Power sends the ball into the glass, where it comes back to the opponents. Control and placement win more points at every club level. Why do I keep losing points off the back glass? Usually because you prepare too late or stand too close. Take the racket back as the ball approaches the glass, give yourself space, and hit the ball in front of you. Where should I stand as a beginner? Either at the back, about a step behind the service line, or at the net, about two to three metres from it. Avoid staying in the middle of the court, and stay level with your partner. Should beginners smash? Only on easy, high balls you can hit in front of you near the net. On other lobs, a controlled bandeja or letting the ball come off the glass is safer. How long does it take to stop making beginner mistakes? It varies with how often you play and whether you have coaching. Fixing one habit at a time, with a lesson or two, speeds things up a lot. 
##### how-to-get-better-at-padel
How to get better at padel comes down to four habits: control before power, playing off the back glass, moving with your partner, and practising one skill at a time between matches. That's the short version, and it works at every level. Below is a practical plan for turning court time into real progress, including what to do when you can't get on court. The big idea: consistency first At club level, padel matches are won by the pair that makes fewer mistakes. The LTA calls padel "a game of consistency", and every coaching source we checked says some version of the same thing: accuracy and placement matter more than power. So the fastest way to get better is not a new shot. It's missing less. That shapes everything below. Learn the shots that keep you in the point, practise them until they're boring, and only then add the attacking shots. How to get better at padel as a beginner: the skills in order Build your game in roughly this order. Each step makes the next one easier. Controlled forehands and backhands. Short backswing, contact in front, ball deep and through the middle. Grip matters here; see our padel grip guide. The back glass. Let the ball come off the wall and play it as it drops. This is the skill that moves most beginners up a level. See how to play off the back glass . The volley. A short, firm punch from about two to three metres off the net. Our volley guide covers it. The lob. Your way from the back to the net. The bandeja. The controlled overhead that lets you keep the net when lobbed. The serve and return. Not glamorous, but they start every point. See serve and return. The víbora, the smash off the glass and the chiquita come later. You'll get far more out of a reliable lob and bandeja. How to practise so it actually works One skill per session The LTA's advice is to practise one skill per session. Trying to fix your volley, your lob and your back glass in the same hour usually fixes none of them. Set targets Count. Rally cross-court and set a target number of shots without an error. The LTA's "ladder rally" is a good one: rally two shots, then three, then four, and any mistake sends you back to the start. Practise the glass with a feeder One player throws the ball at the floor just in front of the back wall; the other lets it rebound and returns it softly. To make it harder, use the corner so the ball comes off two walls. Ten minutes of this every session builds the skill quickly. Play pressure games The LTA's "yellow card, red card" game is excellent: play normally, but your first error in a game is a yellow card and a second error in the same game is a red card, which loses you the game. It trains exactly what wins matches. Play smaller to miss less HEAD suggests practising on a smaller court area to build margin for error. If you can rally on a tight target, the full court feels easy. For ready-made sessions, see our padel drills for beginners . How to get good at padel fast There's no shortcut past practice, but these speed things up the most. Take lessons early. The LTA recommends a qualified coach for technique and game understanding, and The Padel School notes that many players turn to lessons when their progress stalls. A few early lessons stop bad habits before they set. Play often, and increase gradually. Regular short sessions beat one long one. If you're new to sport, build up your number of games a week gradually so your body adapts, as the sports-science advice in the Aspetar Sports Medicine Journal recommends. Play with slightly better players. They punish your mistakes and show you better positions. Play competitions or leagues. Match pressure exposes what practice hides, and the LTA recommends competitions and ladders for exactly that. Slow down. The Padel School's advice is to be patient, slow down mentally and physically, and think before you hit. Good players seem to have more time; mostly, they just don't rush. How to get better at padel at home You can't recreate the glass at home, but you can train the hands, eyes and legs. Racket control: tap a ball up in the air with your racket, alternating faces. The LTA recommends this for touch. Wall volleys: volley against an outside wall to build consistency. Keep the swing short and the racket up. Footwork: practise short side steps and a split step, staying on the balls of your feet. Shadow swings: slow-motion bandejas and volleys in front of a mirror, checking that you turn side-on. Watch padel with a purpose: pick one player and watch only where they stand and when they move forward. Strength and mobility: padel involves lunges, rotation and overheads. Our padel exercises guide covers safe options. Our solo padel drills give you full routines you can do on your own. Padel tips and tricks that work at every level Play through the middle. Balls down the middle make opponents hesitate about who takes them, and the net is lowest there. Aim at the feet of the net player, so they have to hit upwards. Use the lob when both opponents are at the net, then move up together. Racket up at the net, every time. Move as a pair. If your partner moves, you move. Only smash if you can get back to the net afterwards. Otherwise, play a bandeja. Don't stay in no man's land. Be at the net or at the back. Talk. Call every shared ball. Warm up properly. Light movement first, then padel-specific movements for arms, shoulders and legs, as the LTA recommends. See our padel warm-up . Track your progress Improvement in padel is easy to feel and hard to see. Keep it simple: Count unforced errors in one set a week. If the number falls, you're getting better, whatever the score. Note how many balls off the back glass you return in a session. Record a match on your phone every month and watch your positioning, not your shots. If you use a booking app with ratings, play competitive matches so your level reflects your game. Our guide to padel levels explains how ratings work. Frequently asked questions How long does it take to get good at padel? It depends on how often you play, whether you have coaching and your racket-sport background. Most beginners rally in their first session; becoming a solid club player usually takes months of regular play. What's the fastest way to improve at padel? Cut your errors. Play with control, learn the back glass, move with your partner, and get a few lessons early. Can I improve at padel without a partner? Yes. Wall volleys, racket control drills, footwork and strength work all help. Some clubs also have ball machines. Should I take padel lessons? If you can, yes, especially early. A coach fixes grip, positioning and wall play before habits set, which saves you months. Does playing more matches make you better? Matches help, especially competitive ones, but combine them with focused practice. Matches show what's wrong; practice fixes it. 
##### padel-grip
The standard padel grip is the continental grip, often called the hammer grip: you hold the racket as if you were about to knock a nail in with its edge, and you use that one grip for almost every shot. Get it right and volleys, bandejas and wall shots all become easier. Get it wrong and you will fight your racket all session. Here is how to hold a padel racket, how tightly, and how to set up the handle so it fits your hand. Why the continental padel grip is the default Padel is played close to the net, at speed, with very little time between shots. A grip change costs time you don't have. The continental grip solves that problem: it gives you a fairly neutral racket face that works on the forehand, on the backhand and overhead, so you can go from a forehand volley to a backhand volley without moving your fingers. That is why coaching guides from retailers and academies alike describe it as the base grip for nearly everything in padel: volleys, low volleys, lobs, balls off the glass, slices, the serve and the overheads. The face sits slightly open, which suits padel's controlled, sliced style of play far better than the closed face of a modern tennis forehand grip. How to hold a padel racket, step by step Here is a simple way to find the grip every time. Instructions are for a right-hander; left-handers mirror them. Hold the racket by the throat with your free hand , with the edge of the frame pointing at the floor and the face pointing sideways, as if it were a small axe. Shake hands with the handle. Put your hitting hand on the handle as if you were about to hammer a nail in with the edge of the frame. Check the V. The V shape between your thumb and index finger should sit on the top edge of the handle, or just to the left of it. Set the heel of your hand level with the end of the handle, so you're not choking up halfway up the grip for normal shots. Spread the index finger slightly , a little like a trigger finger. It gives you more feel for the face. Leave a small gap between your fingertips and your palm. If your fingers dig into your palm, the handle is too thin or you're squeezing. Now hold the racket out in front of you. The face should be roughly upright with a slight tilt backwards. That small tilt is what puts gentle slice on volleys and keeps the ball low. Padel grip types: continental, eastern and others You will hear about several grips. In padel they are not equal partners: the continental grip does most of the work, and the others are occasional tools. Grip How to find it Used for Continental (hammer) V on the top edge of the handle, as if holding a hammer Almost every shot: volleys, serve, bandeja, víbora, smash, lobs, wall shots Eastern forehand Turn the hand slightly towards the forehand side from continental, palm more behind the handle Some coaches allow it for a flatter forehand from the back when you have time Eastern backhand Turn the hand slightly the other way, knuckles more on top Occasionally for a firm one-handed backhand drive; many players never use it Semi-western (tennis forehand) Palm well under the handle Not recommended in padel: it closes the face and makes volleys and overheads awkward Sources don't completely agree on how much room the eastern grips deserve. One coaching guide suggests sticking with continental for your first six months before experimenting at all. Others mention small adjustments for specific shots once your technique is settled. The safe rule for most club players: learn everything with continental, and only adjust if a coach sees a clear reason. Padel grip technique: how tight should you hold it? Grip pressure matters as much as hand position. Squeezing the handle locks the wrist, kills your feel for the ball and tires the forearm, which is one of the habits linked to padel elbow . Relaxed while you wait. One coaching guide puts it at about 5 out of 10 during the rally: firm enough that the racket doesn't twist, loose enough to move the wrist. Firmer at contact. Around 7 out of 10 as you hit, then relax again straight away. Never white knuckles. If your knuckles go pale, you're gripping far too hard. A useful image from one guide: hold the handle like a small animal you don't want to crush and don't want to let escape. Padel grip size: is there one? Not really, at least not in the way tennis players expect. Tennis rackets are sold in numbered grip sizes. Most padel rackets come with one standard handle, and you adjust the thickness yourself with overgrips. The FIP rulebook sets the limits: the handle can be no more than 20 cm long and no more than 50 mm wide and 50 mm thick (FIP equipment rules). How to tell whether the thickness is right for you: Too thin: the racket turns in your hand on off-centre hits, and you end up squeezing to stop it. Too thick: the racket feels clumsy, you lose manoeuvrability at the net and the wrist can start to ache. About right: a small gap between fingertips and palm, and you can hold the racket securely with a relaxed hand. For more on choosing the racket itself, see how to choose a padel racket . Padel grip tape: overgrips and base grips Every padel racket has a base grip, the original covering on the handle. An overgrip is a thin tape you wrap over it. It's cheap, quick to change and does three jobs: it absorbs sweat, improves your hold and protects the base grip from wearing out. Thickness: standard overgrips are roughly 0.4 to 0.6 mm thick. Many players add two to build up a slim handle. Tacky or dry: tacky overgrips feel slightly sticky and suit dry hands. Dry, absorbent or perforated ones suit players who sweat a lot. How often to change: one retailer guide suggests every four to six sessions for club players, or sooner if it feels slippery or hard. Base grip: if the handle still feels wrong with overgrips, the base grip itself can be replaced. Don't forget the cord The wrist cord is part of the racket specification. It must be non-elastic, no longer than 35 cm and worn on the wrist, and it is mandatory. Drop the racket or break the cord during a point and your pair loses it (FIP Rule 13r). See the racket strap rule . Common padel grip mistakes Keeping a tennis forehand grip. The closed face makes volleys and overheads awkward and encourages topspin, which padel rewards far less than placement. Changing grip mid-rally. At the net there's no time. One grip for everything removes the fumble. Holding it in the palm like a frying pan. With the handle pressed flat into the palm, the wrist locks and the face points the wrong way. Squeezing all the time. Tension belongs at contact, not between shots. A grip that gives away the shot. The Padel School points out that a grip which changes for each shot tells your opponents what's coming. A neutral grip keeps the lob, the chiquita and the flat ball all possible from the same set-up. Practising the grip Grip habits form fast, so it's worth a few minutes of deliberate practice. Bounce the ball on the racket face, then on the other side of the face, without changing your hand. Then volley gently against a wall, alternating forehand and backhand. If you notice your hand creeping round, reset with the hammer image. Our volley guide covers the next step. Frequently asked questions What is the best grip for padel? The continental grip, also called the hammer grip. It works for forehands, backhands, volleys, the serve and overheads without changing hands, which is why coaches teach it first. How do you hold a padel racket for beginners? Shake hands with the handle as if holding a hammer, with the V between thumb and index finger on the top edge. Keep a relaxed hand and a small gap between fingers and palm. Do padel rackets come in grip sizes? Generally no. Most come with one standard handle, and you adjust the thickness with one or two overgrips. The FIP limits the handle to 20 cm long and 50 mm wide and thick. How often should I change my padel overgrip? When it starts to feel slippery or hard. One retailer guide suggests every four to six sessions for club players, more often if you play a lot or sweat heavily. Can I use a tennis grip in padel? You can, but it causes problems. A semi-western tennis grip closes the face, which makes volleys, the bandeja and wall shots harder. Most players improve faster once they switch to continental. 
##### padel-bajada
The padel bajada, or bajada de pared, is an attacking shot you play from the back of the court after a lob has bounced and come high off your back glass: instead of defending, you hit it firmly from around shoulder height or above. It turns a defensive moment into an attacking one. Here is what the bajada is, when it's on, how to hit it and how it differs from the bandeja . Bajada meaning: what the word tells you Bajada is Spanish for a descent or a drop, and pared means wall. So bajada de pared is, roughly, the ball coming down off the wall. Spanish padel glossaries define it as a high attacking shot from the back of the court, played against the glass. In English you'll also see it called the off-the-wall smash, which is the term NOX and the research literature use. The idea is simple. An opponent lobs you. The ball bounces in your court, hits the back glass and comes out high. Most club players let it drop and play a defensive lob. A player who can hit the bajada attacks it instead, while it's still high, and puts the opponents under pressure. Bajada vs bandeja: what's the difference? Both are answers to a lob, and both are usually hit side-on with slice. The difference is when and where you hit them. Bandeja Bajada When Before the bounce, in the air After the bounce and after the back glass Where you are Near the net or moving back from it At the back of the court, near the glass Purpose Keep the net position Turn defence into attack and win back time Typical contact Around head height, in front As high as the rebound allows, shoulder height or above, in front Put simply: the bandeja stops you losing the net, the bajada starts the fight to get it back. If you're at the net and can reach the lob, play a bandeja. If the lob has beaten you and is coming high off the glass, the bajada is the attacking option. When the bajada shot is on The ball comes out high. NOX suggests the ball should be at least around waist height after leaving the glass, and the higher the better. Shoulder height or above gives you the best angle down into the court. You've got behind the ball. You need time to get between the ball and the wall. If you're still chasing it, play safe. You're balanced. A bajada hit while falling backwards usually becomes an error. If the ball comes out below waist height, it's a normal back-glass ball. See how to play off the back glass for those, and consider a lob instead. Padel bajada technique, step by step Read the lob early. As soon as it goes over you, move back fast and judge where it will come out of the glass. Get behind the ball. Your body should end up between the ball and the wall, a little further back than you'd stand for a volley. The Padel School's main warning: most players wait for the ball to come to them off the glass and end up hitting it behind their body, which makes it almost impossible to attack. Turn fully side-on. Shoulders and feet face the side wall, with your weight on the back leg. Bring the racket up early. Arms up over the shoulders, much like a high volley or a bandeja. Contact high and in front. Let the ball leave the glass, then hit it as high as you comfortably can, with the arm close to full extension. Cut through the ball. Swing forward and slightly down with slice. Control matters more than raw pace; on the backhand side especially, NOX notes you can't generate much power, so rely on spin and direction. Adjust your pace to how comfortable your position is. Step forward. Move your weight onto the front leg through the shot and keep moving towards the net with your partner if the reply is weak. Forehand and backhand bajada Most bajadas are forehands. In the study of professional players below, around 90% were hit from the centre and right of the court and on the player's dominant side. The backhand bajada exists, and NOX has a guide to it: same set-up, contact around shoulder height with the arm nearly straight, and a cut under the ball. NOX calls it a medium-to-advanced shot and suggests mastering the lower backhand off the glass first. Where to aim the bajada Your partner can help by calling where the opponents are. Then choose from three options, safest first: Down the middle , between the opponents. Safest, and it can cause hesitation over who takes it. The Padel School also favours an aggressive bajada down the middle. At an opponent's body , if they're close to the net. A ball into the body is hard to volley well. Into a gap , for example towards the opponent who is further back. Most skilful, least margin. If both opponents are at the back, there's no need to take risks. If they're tight on the net, the body or a lob may be the better choice. What the research says about the bajada A 2024 study in Perceptual and Motor Skills (Martín-Miguel and colleagues) analysed 441 off-the-wall smashes from the 2022 World Padel Tour Final Master. Its findings give a realistic picture of what the shot does: Women hit twice as many bajadas as men. About 90% were played from the centre and right side of the court, on the dominant side. In about 73% of cases the rally simply carried on with the opponents volleying. About 21.5% won the point. The lesson for club players: even at the top level, the bajada is mostly a pressure shot rather than a winner. Aim to make the opponents uncomfortable, not to end the point every time. Common bajada mistakes Contact behind the body. Usually from waiting for the ball. Get back early and behind it. Trying it on low balls. If the rebound isn't high, play a normal glass shot. Too much power. A hard, flat bajada tends to fly long or hit the opponents' glass on the full, which loses the point (FIP Rule 13g). Standing still afterwards. A good bajada creates time. Use it to move forward. Crowding the glass. Leave space so the ball can come out to you. How to practise the bajada Have a partner stand at the net and feed lobs that bounce deep and come high off your back glass. Start with ten forehands aimed down the middle, with slice and moderate pace. Count how many land deep without hitting the opponents' glass first. Then add a target, such as the feet of a net player, and finally play the point out. Our intermediate drills include feeding patterns for this. Frequently asked questions What is a bajada in padel? An attacking shot played from the back of the court after the ball has bounced and come high off the back glass. You hit it from around shoulder height or above, usually with slice, to put the opponents under pressure. What does bajada de pared mean? Literally "descent from the wall": bajada means descent or drop, and pared means wall. In English it's often called the off-the-wall smash. Is the bajada the same as the bandeja? No. The bandeja is hit in the air before the bounce, to keep the net. The bajada is hit after the ball has bounced and come off the back glass, to turn defence into attack. Is the bajada a beginner shot? Not really. It needs good reading of the glass, and NOX rates the backhand version a medium-to-advanced shot. Learn to play comfortably off the back glass first, then add the bajada on high rebounds. Where should I aim my bajada? Down the middle is the safest choice. At an opponent's body works when they're close to the net, and into a gap is the most ambitious option. 
##### padel-backhand
A good padel backhand is short, side-on and controlled: you turn your shoulders early, keep the backswing compact with your free hand on the racket, and push through the ball in front of your body using the continental grip. It doesn't need tennis power. Here is how to hit it, how to volley on the backhand side and when two hands make sense. Why the padel backhand is different from tennis In tennis, the backhand is a big shot from the baseline. In padel, the court is smaller, the ball is slightly slower and the walls give you a second chance. LTA Padel stresses getting prepared and in position early, and using a short backswing because you don't need much power. The job of most backhands is to keep the ball low and deep, or to set up a lob or chiquita, not to hit winners. That is good news for beginners. A compact, reliable backhand is easier to learn than a tennis backhand, and it wins more points. Padel backhand grip Use the continental grip, the same grip you use for volleys and overheads (see our padel grip guide ). The face sits slightly open, which helps you keep the ball low and lets you play a flat ball, a lob or a chiquita from the same set-up. The Padel School warns against a tennis-style backhand grip. It produces topspin backhands that work, but aren't very effective in padel, and it tells your opponents what's coming. An open face keeps your options hidden. Padel backhand technique, step by step These steps are for a right-hander. Left-handers mirror them. Ready position. Feet about shoulder-width apart, knees slightly bent, weight forward, and keep the feet moving. NOX stresses that you shouldn't stay still while you wait for the ball. Turn early. As soon as you read the ball, turn your shoulders so your right shoulder points towards the ball and your feet face the side wall. The shoulder turn is your main source of power. Free hand on the throat. Take the racket back with your left hand on the throat. It guides the racket and keeps the backswing short. Keep the backswing compact. The racket shouldn't go higher than your shoulder. Coaches commonly suggest around waist height for a normal groundstroke. Adjust with small steps , then plant your feet before you hit. Contact in front. Hit the ball level with your front foot or slightly ahead of it, never beside or behind your hip. For low balls, bend your knees more rather than dropping the racket head. Release the free hand and push through. Your left hand lets go as you swing, and your weight moves from back foot to front foot. Finish towards the target , arm extended and racket face pointing where you want the ball to go, then recover. Padel backhand slice: chop or push? Many padel backhands carry a little slice, which keeps the ball low and makes it die off the glass. But coaches warn against overdoing it. The Padel School's view is clear: a steep downward chop gives you spin but very little power, and it often comes from holding the racket too high in the backswing. Their fix is to swing more horizontally through the ball and to keep the end of the racket level with the ball. Other guides describe glass and defensive backhands as usually being played with slice for control. Both views fit together: a gentle high-to-low path with an open face is fine; a big vertical chop is not. If your backhand keeps floating or dying in the net, flatten the swing. The padel backhand volley At the net, the backhand volley is a punch, not a swing. Our volley guide covers it in full, but the essentials are: Same continental grip , so there's no change between forehand and backhand volleys. Turn the shoulders a little more than on the forehand side and keep the elbow in front of the body. Tiny backswing. On fast balls the shoulder turn is enough. Contact in front , face slightly open, short follow-through high to low. Stand about two metres from the net , so you can step into the ball and still cover the lob. Many players find the backhand volley easier to control than the forehand volley once the grip is right, because the arm naturally stays in front of the body. Two-handed backhand in padel Two hands are allowed. The FIP rulebook contains no rule against two-handed shots or switching hands. Whether you should use one is a coaching question, and the sources weigh it up like this: Two-handed: pros More stability and leverage on body-height balls, natural for former tennis players, useful on returns and some side-wall balls. NOX also notes it may reduce strain on the elbow. Two-handed: cons Less reach on wide and deep balls, awkward off the back glass, harder on low balls, no real backspin, and padel handles are short for two hands. NOX suggests beginners without a racket-sport background start one-handed, and recommends the two-handed version mainly for balls that don't involve the wall. Padel Browser suggests a hybrid: two hands when the ball sits up near your body, one hand when you have to reach or play off the back glass. If you come from tennis, that hybrid is a sensible middle ground. Padel backhand vs forehand Most club players feel more confident on the forehand, but in padel the backhand is often just as reliable, for three reasons. The grip doesn't change. The free hand on the throat makes the backswing naturally compact. And the arm sits in front of the body, which helps control. The forehand usually gives you a little more power and reach. For the other side, see our padel forehand guide . Where to aim your backhand Cross-court and deep when the opponents are at the back: the longest distance and most margin. Low at the feet of net players, so they have to volley upwards. A lob when both opponents are at the net and you're under pressure. Common padel backhand mistakes Late preparation. Turn as soon as you read the ball, and shorten the backswing. Hitting beside or behind the hip. Contact belongs in front, near the front foot. Racket too high in the backswing , which turns the stroke into a chop. Reaching instead of moving. Use small steps to bring your body to the ball. Slowing the racket down at contact. Commit to a smooth follow-through. Taking steps during the swing. Plant, hit, then move. A simple backhand practice routine Ask a partner to feed balls to your backhand from the net. Start with ten balls hit cross-court past the service line, then ten low down the line, then ten lobs from the same set-up. Finish with balls off the back glass, where many backhands are played in matches. Frequently asked questions What grip should I use for a padel backhand? The continental grip. It keeps the face slightly open and is the same grip you use for volleys, so you never need to change at the net. Is a two-handed backhand allowed in padel? Yes. The FIP rules don't prohibit two-handed shots or switching hands. Whether it suits you depends on your background and the ball you're playing. Should a padel backhand be sliced? A little slice with a gentle high-to-low swing is common and helps keep the ball low. A steep downward chop is a mistake: it gives spin but little power. Where should I contact the ball on a backhand? In front of your body, roughly level with your front foot. Contact beside or behind your hip makes the shot late and weak. Is the backhand harder than the forehand in padel? Not necessarily. Many players find the padel backhand compact and reliable, especially on volleys. The forehand usually has slightly more power and reach. 
##### padel-forehand
The padel forehand is a compact, side-on stroke: turn your shoulders early, take the racket back only to about hip height, and swing through the ball in front of your body with a continental grip and a firm wrist. Power comes from the turn and the weight transfer, not the arm. Here is how to hit it, how to add slice and what changes on the forehand volley. What makes a good padel forehand If you've played tennis, your forehand is probably your biggest weapon. In padel it needs to calm down. A big tennis forehand from the back usually lands deep, hits the back glass and comes straight back to the opponents at a comfortable height. The padel forehand is about control: low over the net, deep or at the feet, and with enough variety that the opponents can't guess between a drive, a lob or a chiquita. LTA Padel sums up the difference: a short backswing and a push through the back of the ball, rather than a full tennis swing, with contact out in front of your body. Padel forehand grip Most coaching guides teach the forehand with the continental grip, the same hammer grip you use for volleys and overheads. It keeps the face slightly open, which suits slice, volleys and wall play, and you never need to change grip when the ball comes back to the net. Some coaches allow a slightly more eastern grip for forehands from the back when you have time, but beginners do best learning on continental. See the padel grip guide for how to find it. Padel forehand technique, step by step Instructions for a right-hander. Left-handers mirror them. Ready position. Racket up in front of you, free hand supporting the throat, knees soft, weight on the balls of your feet. Keep the feet moving. Turn side-on early. Turn your shoulders so your left shoulder points at the ball and your feet face the side wall. NOX calls the shoulder turn the most important thing in padel. The Padel School adds that you should take the racket back "way before" you play the shot. Short backswing. Take the racket back to about hip or waist height and no further, keeping it in your peripheral vision. Adjust with small steps so the ball will be at a comfortable distance. Bend your knees for low balls. Weight back to front. Step towards the ball and transfer your weight onto the front foot as you swing. Contact in front. Meet the ball in front of your body or roughly level with your front hip, at a comfortable distance from your body. Babolat suggests taking it after it has peaked off the bounce, on its way down. Swing through, not round. Finish towards your target rather than wrapping the racket round your shoulder. This keeps the ball low and deep. Recover to the ready position and move with your partner. Sources describe the contact point slightly differently, from "level with the front foot" to "at the side of your body at hip height". The common ground is that it should never be late or cramped, and that you should meet the ball with your weight moving forwards. Flat, slice or topspin forehand? NOX describes three versions of the forehand, and the difference is mostly the racket angle and swing path: Forehand Racket and swing Finish When Flat Face upright, swing through the middle of the ball Around shoulder height Balls around waist height, steady rallying Slice (cut) Face tilted slightly back, swing slightly downward Between hip and shoulder Balls above the waist; keeps the ball low so you can move to the net Topspin Face tilted slightly forward, swing upward Lower, below the hip in NOX's description Low balls, dipping passes at net players' feet Padel Browser recommends that most of your forehands be flat with a touch of slice: controlled, low and deep. Topspin is a tool, not a default: overuse it and your margin for error shrinks. Padel forehand slice: don't chop The Padel School's advice on sliced forehands is to hit through the ball rather than chop down on it. A steep chop is hard to time and produces a weak ball. On higher balls you can add a little more slice, but keep the swing travelling forwards. Padel forehand wrist: keep it quiet The wrist is where many forehands go wrong. Coaching guides agree that you shouldn't flick the wrist to generate pace. Power comes from the shoulder turn and the weight transfer, and the wrist stays fairly firm through contact. A flicky wrist makes the face angle unpredictable, sends balls long and puts extra load on the forearm, which is one reason it's linked to padel elbow . A firm wrist doesn't mean a tight grip. Keep the hand relaxed between shots and firm up only as you hit. The padel forehand volley At the net the forehand becomes a short punch. The essentials, which our volley guide covers in detail: Continental grip , unchanged from the groundstroke. Racket up and in front , around chest height, ready. Shoulder turn as the backswing ; the racket shouldn't go back further than your shoulder. Contact in front with the face slightly open and a short, high-to-low follow-through. No wrist flick. Power comes from the incoming ball and your body. The forehand off the back glass Many padel forehands are played after the ball has come off the back glass. The Padel School's advice is to move with the ball and turn early. Padel Browser adds: don't swing early, give yourself space rather than crowding the wall, and step into the shot as the ball comes out. Our guide to the back glass has the full method. Use the service line as a decision line LTA Padel suggests a simple rule: if the ball bounces in front of the service line, play it before the glass. If it bounces beyond it, let it come off the glass first. Common padel forehand mistakes Over-swinging , especially for tennis players. Swing harder than needed and you lose control. Late preparation , which forces a rushed swing. Open stance and arm only. The Padel School lists hitting from an open stance with only the arm as a key error. Turn side-on and use your body. Racket too far back , out of sight, which makes the timing harder. Hitting with the wrist. Moving the feet during the swing. Plant, hit, then reposition. Looking up before contact. A simple forehand practice routine Have a partner feed from the net. Hit ten flat forehands cross-court past the service line, then ten sliced forehands low over the net, then ten lobs from the same set-up so the opponent can't read your shot. Finish with ten balls fed off the back glass. Keep score of how many land in the target zone, not how hard they go. Frequently asked questions What grip should I use for a padel forehand? Most coaching guides teach the continental grip for the forehand, the same grip you use for volleys. Some allow a slightly more eastern grip on forehands from the back once your technique is settled. Should I use my wrist on a padel forehand? No flicking. Keep the wrist fairly firm through contact and get your power from the shoulder turn and weight transfer. Should padel forehands have topspin? Mostly no. Coaching guides recommend flat forehands with a touch of slice for most balls, and topspin occasionally, for example to dip the ball at a net player's feet. How big should the backswing be? Short. Take the racket back to about hip or waist height, no further, and keep it in your peripheral vision. Why do my forehands keep hitting the back glass? Usually too much swing and too little control, or contact too late. Shorten the backswing, swing through towards the target and add a touch of slice to bring the ball down. 
##### padel-warm-up
A good padel warm up takes about ten minutes before you step on court, then continues with a few minutes of easy hitting: raise your heart rate, move the joints you'll use, rehearse padel movements, and only then start hitting harder. Before an official match you get just a 3-minute courtesy rally (FIP Rule 2), so the real preparation has to happen before that. Here is a routine, the drills and the stretches worth doing. Why a padel warm up matters Padel looks gentle from the outside, but it's full of sudden starts, stops, lunges, twists and overheads. A cold body doesn't like any of those. Padel New Zealand notes that a poor warm-up is associated with muscle strains, ankle sprains and shoulder problems, and the physio advice from The Padel School is to build up gradually towards the changes of direction, overheads and faster movements the game demands. There's also a performance reason. The first few games are where cold players drop serve and miss easy volleys. A proper warm-up means you start the match at the level you finish it. What the rules allow: the 3-minute courtesy rally Under FIP Rule 2, players get an obligatory 3-minute courtesy rally before the match; the match itself starts with the first serve. The word courtesy matters: it's a chance for both pairs to get a feel for the ball and court, not a practice session of smashes at the opponents. Three minutes is nowhere near enough to warm up properly, so do your preparation before you walk on. For all the match timings, see padel match times and warm-up rules . The structure: a simple warm up routine Sports scientists often use the RAMP model, developed by Ian Jeffreys in 2007: Raise (heart rate and body temperature), Activate and Mobilise (key muscles and the joints and ranges of motion you'll use), and Potentiate (short, sharp, sport-specific efforts). Padel New Zealand's suggested padel warm-up follows the same shape. Here's a version that takes around 10 minutes off court and a few on it. Phase Time What to do Raise 3 min Easy jog or brisk walk, side shuffles, skipping, gentle changes of direction Mobilise 3–4 min Arm circles, shoulder rotations, trunk twists, walking lunges with rotation, high knees, heel flicks Activate 2 min Squats, a few calf raises, band or bodyweight shoulder rotations, wrist circles Potentiate 2–3 min Split steps, short accelerations and stops, lateral shuffles, shadow swings On court 5+ min Progressive hitting: mini rallies, volleys, lobs, overheads, serves Padel New Zealand suggests 15 to 20 minutes in total. If you have the time, use it. If you only have ten, the table above still covers the essentials. Padel warm up exercises, one by one Raise Jog and shuffle: one length of the court jogging, one length side-shuffling facing the net, one facing away. Skips: easy skipping with relaxed arm swings. Mobilise Arm circles: ten forwards, ten backwards, starting small and getting bigger. Walking lunge with rotation: lunge forward and turn your chest over the front knee. Five each side. Trunk twists: feet planted, rotate gently left and right. Leg swings: front to back and side to side, holding the fence or glass for balance. Activate Bodyweight squats: ten, controlled. Calf raises: ten, to wake up the ankles and Achilles. Shoulder rotations: elbow at your side, rotate the forearm out and in. With a light band if you have one. Wrist and forearm circles , plus gently opening and closing the hand. Potentiate Split steps: a small hop as an imaginary opponent hits, then a quick first step left, right or back. Accelerate and stop: three or four short sprints of a few metres with a controlled stop. Shadow swings: forehands, backhands, volleys, a few bandejas and serves without a ball. Benenden Hospital's physiotherapy advice suggests at least a minute of shadow strokes before playing. Padel warm up stretches: static or dynamic? This is where many players get it backwards. Holding long static stretches before you play isn't the best preparation for a fast sport. A 2016 review by Behm and colleagues found that static stretching tested straight afterwards caused a small-to-moderate drop in performance on average, with the bigger losses from holds of 60 seconds or more per muscle group. Dynamic stretching gave a small improvement. The same review also found that when stretching was followed by further dynamic activity, there was no clear effect on performance, and it recommends stretching within a warm-up that ends with dynamic work. So: Before you play Mostly dynamic movement. If you like to stretch a tight area, keep holds short, under 60 seconds, and follow them with the dynamic phase. Save for later Long static holds. Padel New Zealand puts them in the cool-down: 20 to 30 seconds per stretch, without bouncing or pain. One more piece of evidence worth knowing: a 2014 meta-analysis in the British Journal of Sports Medicine (Lauersen and colleagues) found no injury-prevention effect from stretching programmes, while strength training and balance training did reduce injuries. Stretch because it feels good and helps your range of motion, but get your injury protection from the strength work in our padel exercises guide. Padel warm up drills on court Once you're on court, build the ball speed gradually. A useful order: Mini padel at the net. All four players at the service line or closer, soft control rallies. Two minutes. Groundstrokes from the back. Cross-court rallies, including a few balls off the back glass. Volley and groundstroke. One player at the net, one at the back, then swap. Lobs and overheads. The back player lobs, the net player plays bandejas. Easy pace first. Serves and returns. A few from each side. Leave smashes until the end, and hit them at around three-quarters pace at first. The shoulder is the last joint you want to load cold. Padel warm up games If you're playing socially or coaching a group, a short game makes the warm-up go faster. Original ideas that work well: First to ten, net only. Rallies inside the service boxes, no smashes, first pair to ten points. Keep it alive. Both pairs cooperate to reach twenty shots in a row, all four players touching the ball. Glass only. A rally where every ball has to come off the back glass before you play it. Great for timing. More ideas are in our drills for four players . The cool-down After the match, take five minutes to walk around, then do your static stretches: calves, hamstrings, quads, hip flexors, glutes, lower back, chest, shoulders and forearms, 20 to 30 seconds each, as Padel New Zealand suggests. Not medical advice This routine is for healthy players. If you're returning from injury or have pain that persists, talk to a physiotherapist or doctor. Frequently asked questions How long should a padel warm up be? Around 10 minutes off court plus a few minutes of progressive hitting. Padel New Zealand suggests 15 to 20 minutes in total if you have time. How long is the warm-up in a padel match? Three minutes, as an obligatory courtesy rally before the match, under FIP Rule 2. The match itself starts with the first serve, so do your proper warm-up beforehand. Should I stretch before padel? Mostly dynamic movements. Long static holds, especially 60 seconds or more per muscle, can slightly reduce performance straight afterwards. Short holds followed by dynamic work are fine. What is the best warm up for padel? One that raises your heart rate, mobilises the shoulders, hips and ankles, rehearses padel movements like split steps and lunges, and then builds up the hitting gradually. Does warming up prevent padel injuries? Physio and federation advice links poor warm-ups to strains, sprains and shoulder problems. No warm-up guarantees you won't get injured, but it's a sensible habit alongside strength training and gradual increases in playing time. 
##### padel-exercises
The most useful padel exercises are simple strength and movement drills for the legs, core, shoulders and forearms, done two or three times a week: squats and lunges, calf raises, planks and rotation work, rotator cuff and shoulder-blade exercises, and short, sharp footwork. Research on sports injuries supports strength training far more than stretching. Here is what to do at home and in the gym, and why it matters for padel. What padel asks of your body Good padel exercises start from what the sport actually demands. A 2023 review of the research (Guijarro-Herencia and colleagues, Frontiers in Sports and Active Living ) found that points in professional men's padel last about 9 to 13 seconds, with longer points in women's padel, and that players work at around 70 to 80% of their maximum heart rate on average. Padel is intermittent: short bursts of movement and hitting, then brief rests. The injury picture points the same way. A 2023 systematic review in BMJ Open Sport & Exercise Medicine (Dahmen and colleagues) found around 3 injuries per 1,000 hours of training and 8 per 1,000 matches. The elbow was the most commonly reported injury site, followed by the knee, shoulder and lower back, and tendon and muscle injuries were the most common types. The authors are careful to say the evidence is limited, but it tells you where to focus: legs for the lunges and changes of direction, core and back for the rotation, and the shoulder, forearm and elbow for the hitting. What the evidence says about exercise and injuries Two pieces of research are worth knowing, even though neither is padel-specific. Strength beats stretching. A 2014 meta-analysis of randomised trials in the British Journal of Sports Medicine (Lauersen and colleagues) found that strength training reduced sports injuries to less than a third, and balance (proprioception) training also reduced them. Stretching programmes showed no beneficial effect. Shoulder programmes can work for overhead sports. A randomised trial in 660 elite handball players (Andersson and colleagues, BJSM , 2017) found that a shoulder programme done three times a week as part of the warm-up, targeting rotator cuff strength, shoulder-blade strength, thoracic mobility and the kinetic chain, lowered the risk of shoulder problems by 28%. Handball isn't padel, but the overhead load is a reasonable comparison for players who smash and play bandejas a lot. For general health, the NHS recommends strengthening activities that work all the major muscle groups on at least two days a week. Two short sessions a week of the exercises below, plus a pushing exercise such as press-ups for the chest and arms, meets that and supports your padel. Padel exercises at home (no equipment) All of these need nothing more than a bit of floor space, and a resistance band for the shoulder work if you have one. Padel New Zealand recommends this mix of leg, core and shoulder work for injury prevention. Exercise Sets × reps Why it helps on court Bodyweight squat 3 × 12 Getting low for volleys and glass shots Reverse or lateral lunge 3 × 8 each leg Lunging for wide balls and pushing back Single-leg calf raise 3 × 12 each leg Ankles and Achilles for split steps and stops Single-leg balance 3 × 30 s each leg Ankle and knee stability; add eyes closed when easy Glute bridge 3 × 12 Hips that support the knees and lower back Plank 3 × 30 s A stable trunk for overheads Press-up 3 × 8–12 Chest, shoulders and arms; do them on your knees or against a wall to start Side plank 3 × 20 s each side Rotation and side-to-side control Band external rotation 3 × 12 each arm Rotator cuff for the serve, bandeja and smash The sets and reps are sensible starting points, not prescriptions. Stop if something hurts, and build up gradually. Padel exercises for beginners If you're new to padel and to training, start smaller. Twice a week, pick five exercises from the table, do two sets of each and focus on clean technique. The Padel School's physio advice is that a programme should match your current ability and injury history, and that a routine you can follow consistently beats a complicated one. After three or four weeks, add a third set or a little load. Beginners also need to manage how fast they ramp up their playing time. Padel elbow is an overuse injury, so a sudden jump in playing time is a common risk; Padel New Zealand recommends increasing frequency and duration gradually and building in rest days. Padel exercises in the gym With weights available, you can load the same movement patterns: Goblet squat or trap-bar deadlift: leg and hip strength. Split squat or walking lunge with dumbbells: single-leg strength for lunges and changes of direction. Romanian deadlift: hamstrings and lower back. Single-arm row and lat pulldown: back and shoulder-blade strength to balance all the forward hitting. Cable or band rotations (pallof press, wood chop): controlled trunk rotation. Calf raises on a step: Achilles and calf capacity. Choose loads you can lift with clean technique, and progress slowly. If you're unsure about technique or loading, a session with a qualified coach or physiotherapist is money well spent. Padel shoulder exercises Every serve, bandeja, víbora and smash loads the shoulder. Benenden Hospital's physiotherapists list exercises such as external shoulder rotation, single-arm pull-downs, prone holds, lateral raises, wrist rotations and core strengthening for players with padel shoulder. For healthy players, a short routine based on the same ideas: Band external rotation , elbow at your side: 2–3 × 12. Band pull-apart , arms straight in front: 2–3 × 15, squeezing the shoulder blades together. Prone Y and T raises , lying face down, light or no weight: 2 × 10 each. Single-arm band pull-down : 2–3 × 12 each arm. Wrist extension and flexion with a light dumbbell, forearm supported: 2 × 15. Lower slowly. The handball trial above ran its programme three times a week as part of the warm-up. You can do the same: a few minutes of band work before you play doubles as shoulder preparation. See our padel warm up for the rest of the routine. Not medical advice If you have shoulder or elbow pain that lasts, see a physiotherapist or doctor before following any exercise programme. Persistent pain needs a proper assessment, not a generic routine. Padel training exercises for speed and footwork Because padel points are short bursts, your conditioning should be too. Long, slow runs have their place for general fitness, but on-court speed comes from short, sharp work with rest: Split step and react: a partner points left, right, forward or back; split step, take two quick steps, recover. 6 × 20 seconds. Lateral shuffle to touch: shuffle between two markers about three metres apart, touching each. 6 × 15 seconds with 30 seconds' rest. Net to back and back: start at the net, turn and move back to the service line as if for a lob, then return. 6 to 8 reps. Shadow points: play an imaginary point of five or six shots at full intensity, including footwork, then rest. Repeat 8 to 10 times. Keep the work bouts close to real point length, roughly 10 to 20 seconds, and rest between them. For on-court drills with a ball, see our solo padel drills . A sample weekly plan Day 1: lower body and core (home or gym), 30 minutes. Day 2: padel. Day 3: upper body and shoulders, plus footwork, 30 minutes. Day 4: padel. Day 5 to 7: one more padel session and at least one rest or easy day. Adjust to your schedule. The aim is two strength sessions, regular padel and enough recovery. The Padel School's physio advice is that if you're repeatedly sore or tired, reducing your load may help more than adding training. Frequently asked questions What exercises are good for padel? Squats, lunges, calf raises, balance work, planks and side planks, rotation exercises, and rotator cuff and shoulder-blade exercises, plus short footwork drills. They target the areas padel loads most. Can I do padel exercises at home? Yes. Bodyweight squats, lunges, calf raises, glute bridges, planks and balance work need no equipment, and a resistance band covers the shoulder exercises. How often should I train for padel? Two strength sessions a week is a good target, in line with the NHS recommendation for strengthening activities. Add footwork work and leave time for recovery. Does strength training prevent padel injuries? There's no padel-specific trial yet, but a large meta-analysis across sports found strength training reduced injuries to less than a third. Stretching programmes showed no beneficial effect. Which padel shoulder exercises should I do? Band external rotations, band pull-aparts, prone Y and T raises and single-arm pull-downs are common choices. If your shoulder already hurts, see a physiotherapist first. 
##### padel-tactics-for-beginners
The best padel tactics for beginners are simple: keep the ball in play, move with your partner, win the net with a deep lob, and play through the middle of the court. You don't need clever shots to beat most club pairs. You need fewer errors and better positions than they have, and this guide shows you how to get both. Why tactics matter more than technique at first LTA Padel puts it bluntly: at every level, most rallies are won and lost by mistakes. That's good news if you're new. You can't build a perfect bandeja in a week, but you can decide today to stop giving away free points. Padel also rewards position more than power. The walls bring hard shots back into play, so a big hit that isn't perfectly placed often just returns to your opponents at a comfortable height. A pair that stands in the right place and plays the percentage ball will beat a pair of hard hitters more often than you'd think. So the padel strategy for beginners below is mostly about two things: making fewer errors, and being in the right part of the court when you hit. Padel tactics for beginners: the eight rules 1. Get the ball back, one more time Your first tactic is consistency. HEAD's coaching advice is to keep the ball in play and let the opponents make the errors, and that's the right default for new players. Aim well inside the court, over the middle of the net where it's lowest, and give yourself margin. A rally you keep alive is a rally the other pair can still lose. 2. Move as a pair Think of you and your partner as tied together. When one goes forward, the other follows. When one is pushed back by a lob, you both go back. The Padel School's point is that if one of you stays back while the other goes up, a big diagonal gap opens through the middle, and good opponents aim straight at it. Side by side, the gaps are small. That means no "one up, one back" like many social tennis doubles. Both at the net, or both at the back. Our guide to padel positioning in doubles goes into the details. 3. The net is where points are won The pair at the net usually controls the point. From there you can see the whole court and choose between the feet, the middle, the body or an angle. From the back you have fewer options and you're mostly defending. LTA Padel compares it to a goalkeeper stepping off the line: you look bigger and the opponents have less to aim at. So your simple game plan is: from the back, look for a way to get forward; at the net, try to stay there. 4. Lob deep to get to the net When both opponents are at the net and you're at the back, hitting harder rarely works. They're close, quick and expecting it. A deep lob over their heads forces them to turn and run back, and gives you and your partner time to move forward together. That's why the lob is one of the most important shots in padel, not a sign of weakness. The key word is deep. A short lob sits up for a smash. Aim higher and longer than feels natural, and cross-court gives you the most room for error. 5. When in doubt, play down the middle The middle is the low-risk target. The net is lowest there, you're far from the side walls, and the opponents may hesitate over who takes it. LTA Padel also points out that hitting through the middle tends to bring the ball back through the middle, which makes it easier for your pair to cover. If you don't know where to hit, the middle is rarely wrong. 6. Let the glass help you Under the FIP rules, once the ball has bounced on your side it stays in play off the walls, as long as you return it before it bounces a second time (Rule 12). New players often rush to hit the ball before it reaches the back glass. Wait. Let it come off the wall, let it drop, and hit it in front of you. You get more time and an easier ball. Our back glass guide shows the footwork. When you're the one attacking, aim deep balls at the glass rather than the mesh. The glass gives a predictable rebound; the mesh can do anything. 7. Don't try to win the point at the back Babolat's coaching splits the court into zones. Near the back glass your priority is staying in the rally, not finishing it: lobs, high deep balls and slow cross-court shots. Big swings from deep in the court are where beginners lose most of their points. Save your attacking shots for when you're at the net and balanced. 8. At the net, set up before you finish The Padel School's advice on patience applies to everyone: don't go for the winner too early. When you feel the point is there, play one more good ball first, a deep volley or a ball at their feet, and finish on the next one. Even professionals rely on bandejas and lobs far more than smashes. Padel tactics for the serve and return The serve is underarm and has to be hit at or below waist height (FIP Rule 6.4), so it's not a weapon in the tennis sense. Its job is to get you to the net. When you serve: serve deep with a little slice, aim for the side glass or the middle, then move forward straight away. Your partner already starts at the net. When you return: don't go for winners. A deep lob over the server's partner or a low, soft ball at the incoming server's feet is enough. Second serve: safe and deep. A double fault is a free point (FIP Rule 13q). See our serve and return guide for the targets. Padel strategy tips: what to do when you're losing If a match is going badly, don't change everything. Change one thing: Lots of unforced errors? Slow down and aim bigger, safer targets. Getting smashed? Your lobs are short. Lob higher and deeper. Getting passed down the middle? You and your partner are too far apart, or nobody is calling the ball. Losing every net exchange? Lob more and play fewer drives at net players. HEAD also suggests finding the opponents' weaker side early. Backhands are often weaker than forehands, so test them and keep going there if it works. The beginner's game plan in one line Keep it in, move together, lob deep, take the net, play the middle, and let the glass give you time. Talk to your partner Call every ball that could be for both of you: "mine", "yours". LTA Padel adds a more advanced habit: tell your partner where the opponents are and what you're planning. And keep it positive. A partner who feels criticised plays tight. Encourage after mistakes, and save the analysis for the change of ends. Common tactical mistakes beginners make Do Move forward together after a good lob. Play the middle when unsure. Let the ball come off the back glass. Keep the second serve safe. Don't Stand one up, one back. Smash every lob. Go for winners from the back of the court. Stand still around the service line, where balls land at your feet. The area around the service line deserves a special mention. It's a place to pass through, not to stand in. A ball at your feet there is one of the hardest shots in the game. Where to go next Once these rules feel natural, you're ready for the next layer: transition, net control and patterns of play. Our guide to padel tactics for intermediate players picks up from here. Frequently asked questions What is the most important padel tactic for beginners? Consistency. Most rallies at every level are decided by errors, so getting one more ball back in play wins more points than any single shot. Should beginners play at the net in padel? Yes. The pair at the net usually controls the point, so learn to move forward with your partner after a good lob or serve, and stand around two to three metres from the net. Where should I aim in padel as a beginner? Down the middle when in doubt, deep with your lobs, and at the opponents' feet when they're at the net. Leave the sharp angles for later. Should I smash every lob? No. A hard smash that isn't perfect often comes back off the back glass. Smash only the easy, short balls and play a controlled overhead such as a bandeja on the rest. Is it a good tactic to hit every ball to the weaker player? It's a common and legitimate tactic, and many coaches recommend finding the opponents' weaker player or weaker side early. Just stay consistent while you do it. 
##### padel-tactics-intermediate
Padel tactics for intermediate players come down to one idea: win the transitions. You already know to move as a pair and lob to get forward. The next step is knowing when to go, when to wait, how to keep the net once you have it, and how to build a point instead of forcing it. Padel tactics for intermediate players: think in three zones A useful padel strategy for intermediate players is to read the court as three zones. Babolat's coaching model describes them like this: Zone Where Your job Typical shots Back Roughly the last two metres in front of the back glass Stay in the rally, buy time Deep lob, high ball to the centre, slow cross-court ball Middle Between the back zone and the service line area Decide: build or attack Ball at the feet, cross-court drive on a waist-high ball, chiquita Net From the net to about three metres back Attack and keep the position Volleys, bandeja, víbora, smash The zones move with the ball. A high, sitting ball in the middle zone is an attacking chance; a low ball at your feet near the net calls for caution. The question to ask before every shot is: what is this ball, from this position, allowed to do? Taking the net: go on the right ball Intermediate players rarely fail to go forward. More often they go on the wrong ball. Coaching notes from Ely Padel Club sum it up: move to the net after a good lob or a serve, not after a hopeful one. If your lob is short, the opponents are still at the net and you'll be running into a smash. Good triggers to move up together: Your lob gets past the net players and they turn to chase it. Your chiquita forces a soft, upward volley. The opponents are pinned against their back glass. You've just served and your partner is already at the net. When you go, go all the way. Stopping halfway leaves you in no-man's-land, which the Ely coaches call the space where players often get trapped. Keeping the net: the bandeja habit Once you're at the net, the opponents will lob you. Your answer decides the point. If the lob doesn't push you deep, play a controlled bandeja , recover forward and keep the position. The Padel School describes exactly this: one player steps back for the overhead and gets straight back to the net so the pair stays together. Two habits make this work: Expect to hit several. The Ely coaching notes that rallies may need three, four or even five bandejas before you can finish. That's normal, not failure. Don't admire the shot. Watching your bandeja instead of moving back to the net is one of the most common intermediate errors. If the lob is genuinely good, accept it. Drop back together, let it come off the glass, and lob back. Fighting a deep lob with a weak overhead usually costs the net anyway. Building a point instead of forcing it At intermediate level you have enough shots to try anything, and that's the danger. The Padel School's advice is to hold back on attacking shots and play a set-up ball first: when you sense the point is there, hit one more good ball, then finish on the next. Pros rely heavily on bandejas and lobs and pick the right ball to end the point. A simple pattern for the net: Deep volley to keep them back. Volley at the feet or down the middle to force them to lift the ball. Finish the high, weak ball that comes back, to an angle or the middle. Padel tips for intermediate players: where to aim The middle. Babolat's coaching calls it the "divorce" zone for a reason. Attacks through the middle cause hesitation and also reduce the angles the opponents can use against you. The feet. A ball that lands at the feet of a net player forces them to hit up. The weaker player. HEAD recommends focusing on one opponent. It puts pressure on the target, and the partner left out of the rally can lose rhythm. The side glass, not the mesh. Deep balls into the side glass give an awkward but predictable rebound; mesh rebounds are unpredictable for you as well as them. Change pace and height HEAD's tactical advice includes varying pace and placement so opponents can't settle. A sudden slow ball breaks rhythm; a quicker one rushes them. Intermediate players often have one speed. Learn three: a soft ball to the feet, a medium ball deep, and a fast ball only when you're balanced and the target is open. Covering the angles as a pair Movement sideways matters as much as forward and back. The Padel School gives two examples worth drilling: At the back: if you're pulled into the corner, your partner shifts towards the centre to cover the easy volley down the middle. At the net: if you volley down the line, your partner moves across to cover the middle. The opponents are left with a difficult cross-court ball into a small space, or a lob. Our padel doubles strategy guide covers communication and middle balls in more detail. A good intermediate checklist Did we go forward on a good ball? Did we go all the way? When lobbed, did we keep the net if we could? Did we set up before finishing? Were we level with each other? Padel tactics advanced: a short look ahead Once these basics are automatic, advanced tactics add layers on top rather than replacing them: Serve formations. In the Australian formation, the server's partner stands at the net on the same half as the server, to block the receiver's favourite cross-court return. It's legal because partners may stand anywhere on their own side (FIP Rule 3). See our guide to player positions . Attacking overheads. The víbora and the smash out of the court, "por tres", become real weapons instead of risks. Our smash guide explains when. Constant micro-adjustments. The Padel School notes that advanced and professional players adjust position several times per point to close gaps. Match plans. Babolat suggests keeping aggressive opponents pinned at the back with lobs and deep balls, and moving patiently forward against defensive pairs before accelerating. Frequently asked questions What separates intermediate from beginner padel tactics? Beginners learn to move together and lob to get forward. Intermediate players learn when to go forward, how to keep the net with bandejas, and how to build points instead of forcing winners. How do I stop getting stuck in no-man's-land? Commit. After a good lob or serve, move all the way to the net. After a good lob against you, drop all the way back. Don't stop around the service line. Should intermediate players smash or bandeja? Mostly bandeja. Smash the easy, short balls when you're balanced and close to the net, and play a controlled overhead on everything else so you keep the net. Is it fair to target one opponent? Yes. Playing to the weaker player or the weaker side is standard tactics at every level and is recommended by coaching sources such as HEAD. What is the Australian formation? A serving formation where the server's partner stands at the net on the same side as the server, so the receiver has to change their usual return. It's legal under FIP Rule 3. 
##### padel-left-side-vs-right-side
Padel left side vs right side is really a question of roles: for two right-handers, the right-side player traditionally builds the point and the left-side player finishes it. Neither side is better. The best pairs combine one patient player and one finisher, and many coaches now say the roles are less fixed than they used to be. How sides work in padel In doubles, each player usually keeps the same side for the whole set and receives serve from the same box every time. That isn't just habit. Under FIP Rule 8.2, the receiving pair decides in the first game of each set who receives first, and that player receives the first serve of every game in the set. Rule 8.3 keeps the receiving order fixed for the set. So you can swap sides at the start of a new set, but not halfway through. During a rally you'll cross over when you need to, but your home side stays the same. That's why players talk about being a "right side player" or a "left side player". Padel left side vs right side at a glance This is the traditional split for two right-handed players: Right side (drive / forehand side) Left side (revés / backhand side) Role Builds the point, controls the rally Finishes the point Typical profile Consistent, patient, good defender Aggressive, athletic, strong overhead The middle Backhand faces the middle Forehand and overhead face the middle Lobs down the middle Usually leaves them to the partner Usually covers them Key shots Lob, backhand off the glass, controlled volley Bandeja, víbora, smash The Padel School and All For Padel both call this the traditional view, and both add that players can now dictate play from either side. All For Padel's coach puts it simply: there's no longer such a marked difference between the sides. The right side player The Padel School calls the right-side player the strategist of the pair, and All For Padel calls them the "brain". Your job is to keep the ball in play, vary speed and angle, read the rally and set up your partner. Right side tactics Be the steady one. Fewer errors, deep lobs, low balls at the feet. The Padel School notes that from the right most lobs come over your left shoulder , so your choice of overheads is different and the bandeja becomes important. Work on your backhand off the glass. The backhand corner is where right-side players get into trouble. Use the víbora less. The Padel School says it's harder to hit from the right side. The left side player For a right-hander on the left, the forehand and the overhead face the middle of the court. That's why the left-side player usually takes the lobs and smashes through the centre and is expected to finish points. HEAD describes the left player as typically more aggressive and better at aerial shots. Left side player tips Own the middle. Your forehand covers it, so call and take the middle balls you can reach. Pick the right ball to attack. Being the finisher doesn't mean smashing everything. Wait for the ball your partner has set up. Develop the bandeja and víbora. The Padel School argues the bandeja is far more of an advantage from the left than a high backhand volley is from the right. Be ready to cover a little of your partner's side , as All For Padel notes left-side players sometimes do. Where left-handers play A left-hander's forehand points to the middle from the right side. The Padel School says left-handers almost always play on the right for that reason, and All For Padel notes that a right-hander and left-hander together have an advantage: both forehands cover the centre, where many shots are played. The flip side is lobs. With a lefty on the right and a righty on the left, both backhands face the side walls, so agree who takes the high balls down the middle before you start. A left-hander on the right also has a different role from a right-handed drive player: The Padel School says they should look to attack through the middle with smashes and bandejas. Padel racket for the left side Some retailers suggest power-oriented rackets for left-side players and control rackets for right-side players. Padel Market, for example, says the backhand-side player usually takes more smashes and finishing shots and often chooses a power or all-round racket, while the forehand-side player builds the point and tends to suit control. The same source adds the important caveat: it isn't a fixed rule, and if your technique isn't yet solid, control will win you more points even on the left. Choose your racket for your level and style first, then for your side. Our guide on how to choose a padel racket explains shapes and balance. Which side should I play? The Padel School's main advice is not to choose your side too early, calling it one of the biggest mistakes new players make. Play both, so you understand your partner's job too. Then use these questions: Which hand do you play with? Left-handers usually go right. What is your strongest shot? A reliable overhead points left; a reliable backhand and lob point right. What is your temperament? Patient builders often suit the right; natural finishers the left. Where do you feel comfortable? All For Padel's coach says personal feeling matters more than the traditional profiles. You'll usually find a side where you miss less and read the game better. Who is your partner? Professionals switch sides depending on their partner. Fit the pair, not just yourself. Short answer New to padel? Play both sides for a few months. After that, the more consistent player usually goes right and the stronger overhead goes left, unless one of you is left-handed. Making the partnership work Whichever side you play, The Padel School stresses that learning to move together as a pair matters more than the side choice itself. Agree who takes the middle, call every shared ball, and move up and back as one. Our guides to positioning in doubles and padel doubles strategy cover how. Frequently asked questions Is the left side harder in padel? It's different, not harder. The left side usually carries more overheads and finishing shots; the right side carries more defence, backhands and point-building. Both are demanding. Should the better player play on the left? Traditionally, the player with the stronger overhead and attacking game plays left. But if the better player is a left-hander or a superb defender, the right side may suit them more. Can you switch sides during a padel match? Not in the middle of a set. The receiving order is fixed for the set (FIP Rules 8.2 and 8.3), so pairs usually swap, if at all, at the start of a new set. Which side should a left-handed padel player play? Usually the right side, so the forehand covers the middle of the court. Do I need a different racket for the left side? Not necessarily. Some players on the left prefer more power, but level and technique matter more than side when choosing a racket. 
##### padel-doubles-strategy
The core of padel doubles strategy is that you win and lose as a pair: move together, agree who takes the middle, talk on every shared ball, and build the point until one of you gets the ball to finish. Padel is almost always played as doubles, so these team habits matter more than any single shot. Padel doubles strategy: one team, one shape The Padel School's rule for doubles is short: move forward and defend backwards, together. When one partner advances, the other follows; when one drops back, so does the other. If you separate, you open space for the opponents. The FIP rulebook itself is written around pairs, from the coin toss (Rule 4) to who hits the ball (Rule 12: pairs hit alternately). That team logic shapes everything below: where you stand, who hits which ball, and how you talk. Padel doubles positioning There are two home positions for a pair: Both at the net (attack): level with each other, about two to three metres from the net, racket up. Both at the back (defence): a metre or two in front of the back glass, with room to let the ball come off the wall. Around the service line is a transition area. Pass through it; don't stay there. Our full guide to padel doubles positioning explains both positions and when to switch. Sliding sideways Pairs also move left and right together. The Padel School's examples: if you're pulled into a back corner, your partner shifts towards the centre to cover the easy ball down the middle. At the net, if you volley down the line, your partner moves across to close the middle, leaving the opponents only a tight cross-court ball or a lob. Who takes the middle? Balls down the centre cause more confusion than any other shot. Agree a default before the match. The Padel School says it's usually the forehand player, though not always. Situation Common default Two right-handers, ball down the middle The left-side player, whose forehand faces the middle Lob down the middle at the net The player whose forehand or overhead side covers it Volley in the middle Often whoever is further forward, because they can take it earlier Right-hander and left-hander Both forehands face the middle, so agree in advance who takes it, and the overheads The rules give you a reason to sort this out. If both partners hit the ball, simultaneously or one after the other, the pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13o). And if your partner's shot touches you, the point is lost too (Rule 13j).
