The one exception: if you both swing at once and one hits the ball while the other only hits the partner's racket, it isn't a double hit. Padel doubles communication LTA Padel's advice is to call "yours" or "mine" during the point, and at a higher level to tell your partner where the opponents are and where you plan to hit. The Padel School adds: call early, call when you switch sides, and give information, not emotion. Calls worth agreeing "Mine" / "Yours": who takes the ball. Call early and once. "Switch": a lob has gone over you and your partner is taking it, so you swap sides. "Up" / "Back": move forward or drop back together. Where they are: when you're playing off the back glass, your partner can see the opponents and you can't. The Padel School recommends asking. Signals before the serve Many pairs use hand signals behind the back from the net player to plan the serve and whether they'll cross. Padel Browser describes one common set, such as one finger for a serve to the middle and two for wide. These are conventions, not an official code, so agree your own. Tone matters The Padel School suggests replacing blame with instructions: not "why did you hit that?" but "high ball next time, let's lob". LTA Padel stresses building your partner's confidence. A tight partner misses more. Agree simple patterns The Padel School recommends agreeing a few patterns so you don't have to think mid-rally. Useful ones include: Opponent hits short: attack the middle. Under pressure at the back: default to the lob, then move up together if it's good. One of you pulled wide: the other covers the middle automatically. Lobbed at the net: bandeja if you can, both back if you can't. Before the match, also discuss who makes tactical calls and who is more confident on lobs and smashes. After it, ask what worked, which patterns won points and where you got exposed. Playing with a stronger partner Expect the opponents to target you. HEAD's tactical advice to players is to focus on one opponent, and the weaker player is the obvious choice. Your job is not to play above your level but to be hard to beat: Cut your errors. Keep the ball deep and in play, and lob well. Let your partner finish. Feed your partner the attacking balls. Play towards the opponent who will give a high reply to your partner's side. Agree the middle in their favour if their forehand or overhead covers it. Don't apologise after every point. Reset and play the next one. Playing with a weaker partner Stay in your own space most of the time. The Padel School notes that each player generally covers their own side. Poaching everything leaves holes behind you. Take more of the middle and the overheads where you can reach them cleanly. Keep it simple. HEAD's advice to keep tactics simple matters most when one partner is under pressure. Encourage. The opponents want your partner to feel isolated; your calls and tone undo that. Common doubles mistakes Do Move as a pair, call every shared ball, cover the middle when your partner is pulled wide, and agree patterns before the match. Don't Play one up, one back, go silent on middle balls, chase your partner's balls, or criticise mid-match. Still deciding who plays where? Read padel left side vs right side , and see our beginner tactics guide for the basics. Frequently asked questions What is the best padel doubles strategy? Move together, take the net with deep lobs, keep it with controlled overheads, play through the middle, and communicate on every shared ball. Who should take the middle ball in padel? Agree before the match. For two right-handers, it's usually the left-side player, whose forehand faces the middle. At the net, whoever is further forward often takes the volley. What happens if both partners hit the ball? The pair loses the point (FIP Rule 13o). If you both swing and only one touches the ball while the other hits the partner's racket, play continues. Should we play one up, one back in padel? No. It leaves a diagonal gap through the middle. Padel pairs play side by side, both at the net or both at the back. How do I stop opponents targeting my weaker partner? You can't stop them, but you can help: cover more of the middle and the overheads you can reach cleanly, keep tactics simple, and keep your partner confident. 
##### padel-singles
Yes, you can play padel singles, one against one, but it's an informal format: the official FIP rules are written for pairs. You can play on a dedicated singles court, which is 20 metres long and 6 metres wide, or on a normal 20 × 10 m court with a few agreed house rules. Here's how both work, and how to play it well. Can you play padel 1v1? You can, and plenty of people do, especially for training. But padel is a doubles sport. The FIP Rules of Padel are written around pairs: Rule 3 begins with each pair of players on either side of the net, the coin toss is won by a pair (Rule 4), and the rulebook doesn't mention singles at all. Professional tours are played as doubles. So padel singles has no official rulebook. You play the normal rules wherever they still make sense, and agree the rest with your opponent before the first point. Padel singles court size Some clubs build dedicated singles courts. They keep the full length and the enclosure but are narrower: Doubles court Singles court Length 20 m 20 m Width 10 m 6 m Back wall glass panels Five panels of 2 m Three panels of 2 m Official FIP specification Yes No FIP singles specification The 20 × 6 m figure is reported consistently by Padel Market, Padel Addict and The Padel Paper. The FIP's court rules only define the 20 × 10 m doubles court (see our padel court dimensions guide). The Padel School notes that marked singles courts keep the same length and wall structure, with service boxes adjusted to the narrower width. Singles courts are still uncommon. The Padel Paper counted only a handful of publicly accessible ones in the UK when it surveyed them, and said most players without one simply adapt a standard court. Singles vs doubles court: how it changes the game The Padel Paper makes a neat point: on a 6 m singles court, one player covers 6 m of width, compared with about 5 m each in doubles. That small difference makes passing shots easier and the net less dominant than in doubles. On a full 10 m court, one player has the whole width to cover, which is far more running. The Padel School describes singles as far more physically intense than doubles, with less time to recover between shots, especially on the full doubles width. Padel singles rules Keep everything you can from the FIP rules: Scoring: 15, 30, 40, game; sets to six with a two-game margin; tie-break at 6-6 (FIP Rule 1). Our scoring guide has the details. Serve: underarm, bounced behind the service line, hit at or below waist height, diagonally into the box (FIP Rule 6). Walls: the ball must bounce on the floor before hitting the opponent's walls, and you can use your own glass but not your own mesh (Rules 13 and 14). Return: the receiver lets the serve bounce (Rule 8.1). Serving order: players alternate serving games, as in doubles. House rules for singles on a doubles court On a normal 20 × 10 m court, agree these before you start: Which area counts. Padel Market describes a common version where each player defends a diagonal half, and a ball landing outside it is out. Others play the full width, which is a serious workout. Which side walls are in. If you play a half-court, decide whether a ball that bounces in and then hits the "out-of-play" side glass is still good. Agree this in advance; many players count it as good. Service boxes. Usually unchanged: diagonal, alternating boxes each point. Match length. Padel Market suggests shorter formats, such as first to four games or a single set with a tie-break. The Padel School suggests no-ad scoring or a match tie-break instead of a third set. Three players? Padel Market's suggestion: play two against one, or rotate one player out each game. Padel singles tactics Singles is a different game from doubles, so some doubles habits need to change. Recover to the centre In doubles you cover your half. In singles, The Padel School says to hold a central base and recover to the middle after each shot, the opposite of the doubles instinct to stay on your side. Be patient and play cross-court Padel Market recommends cross-court as your default, because it's the longest line with the most margin, and hitting deep while you wait for a short ball. Rallies are longer, so building the point matters more than quick winners. Pick your moment at the net Rushing the net from a neutral ball leaves you exposed, because there's nobody beside you to cover the pass. Go forward after a good, high lob that pushes your opponent onto the back glass, or a short ball you can attack. Then cover the middle of the net. Use the lob The lob still works: high and deep pushes your opponent to the back glass and gives you time to move forward. Consistency first With no partner to cover your mistakes, unforced errors cost more. The Padel School puts consistency ahead of power. A simple singles session plan If you've booked a court for two and want a mix of play and practice, this structure works well: Warm up together. Start with gentle cross-court rallies from the back, then volleys at the net. The FIP rules require a three-minute courtesy rally before a match (Rule 2), but for a practice session give yourselves longer. Play a cross-court game. Each of you defends one diagonal half. It's the format closest to doubles and a good way to groove your lob and your play off the glass. Play a full-width set. One set with a tie-break on the whole court, if you have the legs for it. Finish with a rule-based game. Padel Market suggests games such as cross-court only, or every point must include a wall. Swap ends regularly so you both play from each side of the court, and agree before every game which house rules apply. Why play padel singles? Fitness: more running, more shots per player, less recovery. More touches on the ball: every shot is yours, so you practise more in the same hour. Glass play and defence: you'll play off the walls constantly. Decision-making: The Padel School notes players often return to doubles sharper and more composed. The trade-off, as Padel Market points out, is that singles doesn't teach doubles positioning or teamwork. Use it alongside doubles, not instead of it. Our padel drills for 2 players are another way to use a court with just two of you. Frequently asked questions Can you play padel with 2 players? Yes. Play singles on a dedicated 20 × 6 m court, or on a normal court using agreed house rules such as a diagonal half each. Two players can also run partner drills. Is padel singles official? No. The FIP rules are written for pairs and don't mention singles, and professional padel is played as doubles. How big is a padel singles court? Usually 20 m long and 6 m wide, compared with 20 × 10 m for doubles. There's no FIP specification for singles courts. How do you serve in padel singles? The same way as in doubles: underarm, after a bounce, at or below waist height, diagonally into the service box, alternating boxes each point. Is padel singles harder than doubles? Physically, yes. You cover more court with less recovery time, especially on a full-width doubles court. 
##### padel-calories
There's no official figure for padel calories burned, but a reasonable estimate for an hour of padel is roughly 300 to 600 kcal for most adults, depending on your body weight and how hard you play. Here's where that range comes from, why it varies so much, and what the research says about padel as a workout. Padel calories burned: the short answer For most adults, an hour of padel probably burns somewhere around 300 to 600 kcal. A lighter player in a relaxed social game sits at the bottom of that range; a heavier player in a hard, competitive match can go above it. Treat any single number you see online with caution. Calorie burn depends on your weight, the intensity of the game, how long the points are, and how much time you spend picking up balls and changing ends. That's why we give a range and show the working. Where the numbers come from The standard reference for the energy cost of activities is the Compendium of Physical Activities, last updated in 2024 and published in the Journal of Sport and Health Science. It gives each activity a MET value: how many times more energy it uses than sitting at rest. The 2024 Compendium has no entry for padel. It does list tennis, which is the closest well-studied racket sport: Compendium activity (code) MET Tennis, doubles (15685) 4.5 Tennis, doubles (15680) 6.0 Tennis, general, moderate effort (15675) 6.8 Tennis, singles (15690) 8.0 Tennis, general, competitive (15676) 8.0 Because padel is played as doubles, the doubles tennis values (4.5 to 6.0) are a sensible starting point for a social game, with the moderate and competitive values (6.8 to 8.0) as a guide for harder matches. This is an approximation: padel and tennis aren't the same sport, and no padel-specific MET value has been published in the Compendium. Padel calories per hour: estimates by weight The Compendium's conversion is: kcal per minute = MET × 3.5 × body weight in kg ÷ 200. Multiply by 60 for an hour. Using the tennis values above: Body weight Easy social game (4.5 MET) Steady game (6.0 MET) Hard, competitive (8.0 MET) 60 kg about 285 kcal about 380 kcal about 505 kcal 70 kg about 330 kcal about 440 kcal about 590 kcal 85 kg about 400 kcal about 535 kcal about 715 kcal These are estimates per hour of continuous play at that intensity. Real sessions include warm-up, breaks at change of ends and time between points, so your actual figure for "an hour at the club" will usually be lower than the table suggests. MET values also include the energy you'd burn anyway at rest. Why your number varies Body weight. A heavier person uses more energy for the same movement. That's built into the formula. Intensity. Long rallies and lots of running burn more than short, slow points. Level. Research shows points and match profiles differ between levels. Mellado-Arbelo and Baiget's 2022 systematic review found professional players had the longest game durations, and that distance covered and speed actually decreased as standard increased, likely because better players position and anticipate better. Rest. Padel is intermittent. In studies of professional play, average point duration ranged from about 9 to 17 seconds, with similar or longer gaps between points (Guijarro-Herencia et al., 2023). Fitness and age. Individual metabolism varies, which no table can capture. What about fitness trackers? Watches estimate calories from heart rate and movement. They're useful for comparing your own sessions over time, but their absolute numbers are estimates too. Don't treat them as more precise than the ranges above. Is padel a good workout? For most people, yes. The research describes padel as a moderate-intensity, intermittent sport: Heart rate. A 2023 review in Frontiers in Sports and Active Living found heart rate during play was consistently around 70 to 80% of maximum across studies (Guijarro-Herencia et al.). Oxygen use. A study of elite young male players found oxygen consumption during simulated competition stayed below 50% of their maximum, with average heart rate around 74% of maximum (Carrasco et al., 2011). Overall load. Mellado-Arbelo and Baiget's review concluded that physiological responses were mostly moderate, that perceived effort ranged from moderate to relatively high, and that work-to-rest ratios confirm padel's stop-start nature. The NHS recommends adults do at least 150 minutes of moderate-intensity activity a week, or 75 minutes of vigorous activity. The NHS doesn't list padel, but it does list doubles tennis as a moderate-intensity example. Given the research above, regular padel can be a good way to work towards that target, especially because it's social and people tend to keep playing. To get more out of it physically, warm up properly, play longer rallies and add some strength work. Our guide to padel exercises covers that, and our padel warm-up helps you start safely. Padel vs tennis calories We couldn't find a peer-reviewed study that measured calorie burn in padel and tennis side by side, so be wary of articles that claim one burns a precise amount more than the other. What we can say: the Compendium values for doubles tennis (4.5 to 6.0 MET) are lower than for singles tennis (8.0 MET). Padel is a doubles game on a smaller court, so a typical padel session is probably closer to doubles tennis than to singles. On the other hand, the walls keep the ball in play, which can mean more rallying and less time collecting balls. Overall, expect a broadly similar calorie burn to doubles tennis, with intensity mattering far more than the sport. Our padel vs tennis guide compares the two in more detail. How to burn more calories playing padel Play longer. Ninety minutes at a steady level burns more than an intense half-hour. Raise the intensity. Play opponents who push you, so rallies last longer. Try singles or drills. More shots per player and more running. See padel singles . Keep breaks short. The FIP rules allow up to 20 seconds between points (Rule 2). Casual games often take much longer. Frequently asked questions How many calories does 1 hour of padel burn? Roughly 300 to 600 kcal for most adults, based on tennis MET values from the Compendium of Physical Activities. Lighter players and relaxed games are at the lower end; heavier players and hard matches can go higher. Is there an official MET value for padel? No. The 2024 Compendium of Physical Activities doesn't list padel, so estimates use related sports such as doubles tennis. Does padel burn more calories than tennis? There's no reliable head-to-head study. Padel is probably closer to doubles tennis than singles tennis, and intensity matters more than which sport you play. Is padel good for losing weight? It can help as part of regular activity, because it's moderate exercise people enjoy and stick with. Weight loss also depends on diet and total activity, not one sport. Is padel cardio? Yes. Studies show heart rate around 70 to 80% of maximum during play, with an intermittent, mostly aerobic profile. 
##### padel-drills-for-beginners
The best padel drills for beginners are simple, repeatable exercises that build control first: short rallies, soft volleys, a reliable serve and comfort with the back glass. Below are eight drills you can run in a normal one-hour court booking, with the set-up, the scoring and how to make each one harder once it feels easy. How to use these padel drills for beginners Beginners improve fastest when every drill has a number attached. A number turns "having a hit" into practice: you know whether you are getting better, and you know when a drill is too easy. Pick three or four drills per session, play each for ten to fifteen minutes, and finish with points so the new habits meet a real game. Control before power. Every drill below rewards keeping the ball in play. Speed comes later. Use a continental grip for volleys and the serve. It's the grip coaches teach for net play because you don't have time to change it there (see our volley guide). Warm up first. Five minutes of easy mini-rallies and some movement is enough. Our padel warm-up has a full routine. Restart quickly. Keep a few balls in your pocket. Less time collecting balls means more touches. Control drills 1. Ladder rally Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Use one half of the court, split lengthways. Both players stand around the service line on opposite sides of the net. How it works: Rally co-operatively. Your first target is two shots in a row, then three, then four, and so on. Any mistake sends you back to two. This is a format the LTA recommends for building consistency. Goal: Reach a rally of ten. Write down your best ladder at the end of each session. Progression: Put one player at the net volleying and the other at the back letting the ball bounce. Swap after every completed ladder. 2. Soft volley chain Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Both players at the net, about two metres back, racket up in front of the body. How it works: Volley gently to each other without letting the ball bounce. Think of each volley as a firm block, not a swing, with the contact just in front of your body. Keep the ball below head height so nobody has to reach. Goal: Twenty volleys in a row as a pair. Count out loud. Progression: Alternate forehand and backhand volleys on purpose, or aim every volley at your partner's feet so they have to bend their knees. Shot drills 3. Serve to the box Players: 1 or 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: A bucket of balls. Place a target such as a racket cover or towel deep in the diagonal service box. How it works: Serve the way the rules require: bounce the ball behind the service line in your own box, then strike it at or below waist height with at least one foot on the ground (FIP Rule 6). Hit ten serves from the right, then ten from the left. A serve that bounces in the box and then hits the mesh is a fault; one that comes off the glass is fine. Our guide to the serve rules covers the details. Goal: Eight out of ten in the box, then three out of ten landing near the target. Progression: Add a receiver who returns cross-court. The server scores if the serve lands in the back third of the box, or the return lands short of the server's service line. 4. Back-glass catch Players: 2 · Time: 10–15 minutes Setup: Both players on the same side of the net. The feeder stands near the service line; the hitter starts a couple of metres in front of the back glass. How it works: The feeder throws or gently hits the ball so it bounces just in front of the back glass. The hitter lets it rebound, turns side-on, and returns it softly so the feeder can catch it. Coaches teach the same pattern: turn side-on, take the racket back early, use small steps, and swing low to high. Goal: Ten clean returns into the feeder's hands, alternating forehand and backhand. Progression: Feed into a back corner so the ball comes off two walls, then return over the net instead of to the feeder. Our back glass guide explains the footwork. 5. Lob ladder Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: One player at the back, one at the net. Mark the zone between the net player's service line and the back glass. How it works: The net player feeds a gentle ball. The back player lobs over them into the zone. The net player does not try to intercept; they just watch where it lands. Goal: Count lobs that clear the net player's reach and land beyond the service line without hitting the back glass on the full. Seven out of ten is a strong score for a beginner. Progression: Let the net player reach with a raised racket. Any lob they can touch doesn't count. Our lob guide covers the technique. Movement and teamwork drills 6. Split-step shadow Players: 2 · Time: 5 minutes Setup: One player at the net with a ball, the other at the net on the opposite side, no racket needed at first. How it works: The leader pretends to hit, pointing left, right or up. The mover does a small hop, landing just as the leader "makes contact", then moves two quick steps in the direction shown. The Padel School describes the timing: land the split step as your opponent hits. Goal: Ten reps where the landing matches the contact exactly. Progression: Add rackets and a real feed, so every split step is followed by a volley. 7. Call the middle Players: 3 (two hitters and a feeder) · Time: 10 minutes Setup: The pair stands at the net side by side. The feeder stands on the other side with a basket. How it works: The feeder sends balls down the centre. Before every shot, one player calls "mine" or "yours". Agree the rule first: usually the player with the forehand in the middle takes it. Goal: Any ball that isn't called, or that both players go for, is a point to the feeder. Play to ten. Progression: Mix in lobs down the middle so the pair also has to decide who takes the overhead. Our positioning guide covers the usual agreements. 8. Yellow card, red card Players: 2 or 4 · Time: 15 minutes Setup: Half-court singles for two players, the full court for four. How it works: Play normal points. Your team's first mistake in a game is a yellow card (in doubles, either partner's mistake counts). A second mistake in the same game is a red card, and you lose that game. This is an LTA game, built around the idea that padel is a game of consistency. Goal: Win the most games in fifteen minutes. Progression: Our own variation: make it one card only, so a single mistake loses the game. A simple one-hour beginner session Minutes Drill Focus 0–8 Warm-up and mini-rallies Feel for the ball 8–18 Ladder rally Consistency 18–28 Soft volley chain Net control 28–40 Back-glass catch Walls 40–48 Serve to the box Starting the point 48–60 Yellow card, red card Applying it under pressure Change the middle drills from week to week, but keep the warm-up and the game at the end. Over a month you'll cover every drill on this page at least twice. Track three numbers Your best ladder rally, your volley chain record and serves in out of ten. If all three are going up, your padel is too. When they stop moving, add the progression. Common mistakes in beginner drills Hitting too hard. A drill that keeps breaking down teaches nothing. Slow the ball until you can reach the target. Chasing the ball into the glass. In the back-glass drills, step back early and wait for the ball to come out to you. Standing on the service line. Mini-rallies near the service line are fine; in game drills, don't park there. Pass through mid-court on your way to the net or the back. Too many drills. Three drills done properly beat eight done in a rush. Once these feel easy, move on to our intermediate drills , and read up on beginner tactics so the skills fit into a game plan. Frequently asked questions What is the best padel drill for beginners? If you only do one, make it the ladder rally. It builds control, rewards patience and works with any partner. Add back-glass practice as soon as you can, because the walls are what make padel different from tennis. How long should a beginner padel drill session be? An hour is plenty. Spend about five to ten minutes warming up, forty minutes on three or four drills, and the rest playing points. Can I do padel drills without a coach? Yes. Every drill on this page can be run by players themselves. A coach helps with technique and feeding, but drills with clear targets are useful on their own. Should beginners practise the bandeja? Not first. Build reliable volleys, lobs and back-glass returns, then learn the bandeja once you can hold a rally at the net. How many balls do I need for drills? At least six, and ideally a basket of twenty or more for feeding drills. More balls mean less time collecting and more repetitions. 
##### padel-drills-for-2-players
Padel drills for 2 players are the most useful drills you can do, because one partner is all you need to work on every shot in the game. Here are eight partner drills, from cross-court rallies to back-glass feeding and half-court games, each with a clear goal and a way to make it harder. Why padel drills for 2 players work so well A match gives you a handful of chances to practise any one shot. A partner drill gives you dozens in ten minutes. With two players you can rally, feed and play competitive games, and you can isolate one thing at a time: a volley, a lob, a ball off the glass. Two habits make partner drills far more effective: Take turns as the feeder. A good feed is a skill. Feed the ball you'd want to receive, at a pace your partner can control. Agree a score before you start. A target or a game keeps both players focused and makes it obvious when to move on. Warm up first with a few minutes of easy rallying; our padel warm-up guide has a routine you can run together. Rally drills with a partner 1. Cross-court counter Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Both players at the back, diagonally opposite, each in their own half of the court. How it works: Rally cross-court only, letting the ball bounce and using the glass whenever it gets past you. Cross-court is the percentage shot because it passes over the lower centre of the net and gives you the longest court to land in. Goal: A team target of 30 shots, then 50. Start again from zero after any error. Progression: Every fifth shot must be a lob. The other player lets it bounce and plays it after the glass. 2. Ladder rally, net against back Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Half the court. One player at the net, one at the back. How it works: Rally to a target of two shots, then three, then four. A mistake sends you back to two. The LTA suggests exactly this sort of ladder, with the net-and-back version as the harder step. Goal: Reach twelve. Swap positions each time you complete a ladder or after five minutes. Progression: The net player must volley every ball deep, past the service line, so the back player gets practice off the glass. Net drills with a partner 3. Volley to volley Players: 2 · Time: 8 minutes Setup: Both at the net, about two metres back, on the same half of the court. How it works: Keep a volley rally going with short, compact swings and no winners. Split-step each time your partner hits, so you are ready to move either way. Goal: Count consecutive volleys as a team. Twenty is good; fifty is excellent. Progression: One player plays every volley to the other's feet. The receiver must keep it low and back over the net. 4. Feet and fence Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: One player at the net, the other at the back with a basket of balls. How it works: The back player feeds a low ball. The net player volleys it deep into the back corners, then volleys the reply to the feet. The idea is to practise the two volleys you need most: deep to push them back, low to stop them coming forward. Goal: Ten feeds per set. Score one point for a deep volley that reaches the back glass after bouncing, and one for a reply volley that lands before the service line. Progression: The back player stops feeding and plays the point out after the second volley. Back-glass and overhead drills 5. Glass and back Players: 2 · Time: 12 minutes Setup: The feeder stands at the service line on the same side as the hitter, facing the back glass. The hitter starts a couple of metres in front of the glass. How it works: The feeder sends slow, deep balls that bounce and hit the back glass. The hitter turns, lets the ball rebound, and returns it to the feeder. The LTA's version of this drill starts by allowing an extra bounce after the glass, then moves to hitting the ball straight after the rebound. Mix forehand and backhand feeds so the hitter can't guess. Goal: Fifteen controlled returns out of twenty. Progression: Feed into the corner so the ball comes off the back glass and side glass. Read our back glass guide for the movement: a small step back as the ball passes, then a step forward to hit. 6. Lob and bandeja Players: 2 · Time: 15 minutes Setup: One player at the back with a basket, the other at the net. How it works: The back player lobs. The net player turns side-on, plays a bandeja deep into the court, then moves straight back towards the net. Rotate after fifteen lobs. Goal: Count bandejas that land beyond the service line and leave the hitter back in position before the next feed. Progression: Let the net player choose: bandeja on a deep lob, smash on a short one. Add the víbora once the bandeja is reliable. Competitive games for two 7. Half-court singles Players: 2 · Time: 15 minutes Setup: Play only in one half of the court, split lengthways down the centre line. How it works: Normal padel points, except that you serve straight ahead into the service box opposite you, since the diagonal box is in the other half. Walls count as normal. Add the LTA's yellow card, red card rule if you like: the first mistake in a game is a yellow card, and a second mistake in the same game is a red card that loses it. Goal: First to six games. Progression: The server must follow the serve to the net. The receiver wins a bonus point for any lob that forces the server back. 8. Net challenge Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Half-court. One player starts at the net, the other at the back. How it works: The back player starts every point with a feed. The net player tries to keep the net; the back player tries to win it back with a good lob or a low ball at the feet. When the back player's lob gets over the net player, the back player must move forward. The Padel School's advice applies: don't come in behind a weak lob. Goal: Points are normal. Swap roles after ten points and compare scores. Progression: The net player may only hit bandejas on lobs, no smashes, which forces patience. How to build a two-player session A simple structure that works for most pairs: Warm-up (10 minutes): mini-rallies, then volley to volley. Technical block (25 minutes): two drills on one theme, such as glass and back plus cross-court counter. Overhead block (10 minutes): lob and bandeja. Game (15 minutes): half-court singles or the net challenge. Fair feeding Swap feeder and hitter often, every ten balls or every five minutes. Both players get the same number of reps, and feeding accurately is good practice in itself. When you have a full four, move on to our padel drills for 4 players . On days when your partner cancels, try the solo padel drills instead. Frequently asked questions Can you play padel with only 2 players? Yes, for practice. The FIP rules are written for pairs (doubles), but two players can drill, rally and play half-court games, which are some of the best ways to improve. What is the best padel drill with a partner? The glass and back drill, because the back glass is the skill most new players lack. Pair it with a cross-court rally so you also practise consistency. How long should each partner drill last? Around ten to fifteen minutes. Long enough to get into a rhythm, short enough to stay focused. Do we need a coach to feed? No. Take turns as the feeder and use a basket of balls. Feed at a steady, controllable pace. What is half-court padel? A practice game for two players using one half of the court, split lengthways. Serve straight ahead into the service box opposite you and play normal points. 
##### padel-drills-for-4-players
The best padel drills for 4 players turn a normal doubles booking into focused practice: two pairs, one theme and a score. Below are eight four-player drills that build net play, lobs, transitions and teamwork, plus a section of padel drills for 3 players for the days when someone drops out. Getting the most from padel drills for 4 players With four players the risk is that the session drifts into a casual match. Drills keep it focused. A few rules help: One theme per session. For example net play, defence, or the transition from back to front. Rotate partners. Swap pairs every drill so the stronger players don't always play together. Use conditioned points. Normal points with one added rule teach far more than plain matchplay, because the rule forces the habit you are training. Finish with a match. Ten minutes of normal points tests whether the drill has stuck. Net drills for four 1. Volley war Players: 4 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: All four at the net, about two metres back. Start with straight pairs: one pair facing each other in each lengthways half of the court. How it works: Each pair volleys with each other at the same time, staying in its own half. Keep the ball low, just over the net, aiming at your opponent's feet. Goal: First pair to complete a 15-volley rally wins the round. Play five rounds. Progression: Switch to diagonal pairs, at a controlled pace, so the two balls cross in the middle. Then use a single ball for all four players and play points from the net. Lobs are banned, so the rally is all volleys. 2. Defend the net Players: 4 · Time: 12 minutes Setup: One pair at the net, one pair at the back. How it works: The back pair starts every point with a feed and may only lob. The net pair must answer every lob with a bandeja or víbora and stay forward. When a lob is too good and the net pair has to run back, the back pair moves up and the roles reverse. Goal: The net pair scores a point for every rally they win without losing the net. Swap roles after ten points. Progression: Allow the back pair to play chiquitas as well as lobs. The net pair now has to cover low balls and high ones. Transition drills 3. Lob and take the net Players: 4 · Time: 12 minutes Setup: Pairs start in opposite positions: one at the net, one at the back. How it works: Play normal points, but the back pair earns a bonus point every time it lobs over the net pair and both back players reach the net before the next ball. Move together, as a pair. The Padel School's warning is the key to this drill: when your lob is not good, don't come to the net. Goal: First pair to fifteen (normal points plus bonuses). Progression: The bonus only counts if both players get past the service line and into proper net position, not stuck in mid-court. 4. Serve and stay Players: 4 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: A normal doubles start: server and partner on one side, receiving pair on the other. How it works: The server must follow the serve to the net. The receiving pair aims to win the net back with the return, often a lob or a low ball at the server's feet. Goal: Play service games with normal scoring. The serving pair counts how many points it wins while still at the net. Progression: Second serves only. With less margin on the serve, the first volley matters more. Teamwork drills 5. Middle madness Players: 4 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Pairs on each side of the net, both at the back or both at the net. How it works: Every shot must go through the middle third of the court, between imaginary lines about 3.3 metres in from each side wall. Players must call "mine", "yours" or "go" on every ball. Goal: Normal points. An uncalled ball, or both players going for it, loses the point immediately. Progression: Open the court back up but award two points for any winner hit through the middle. See our doubles positioning guide for who should take it. 6. Rope pairs Players: 4 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Normal doubles points, full court. How it works: Play points while consciously staying level with your partner, as though tied together with a short rope. When one moves forward, both move forward; when one is lobbed, both drop back. Goal: A pair loses a point if, at the moment an opponent hits, one partner is at the net and the other is at the back. Progression: Add a second condition: the pair also loses the point if both players end up on the same side of the centre line when an opponent hits. Match-pressure games 7. Yellow card, red card Players: 4 · Time: 15 minutes Setup: The full court, normal doubles. How it works: Normal games. A pair's first mistake in a game is a yellow card, whichever partner makes it; the second is a red card and loses the game. The LTA uses this game to build consistency. Goal: Most games in fifteen minutes. Progression: Our own variation: a mistake made from the net position is an instant red card. It sharpens volleys and overheads. 8. King of the court Players: 4 (works with 5 or 6) · Time: 15 minutes Setup: One side of the net is the "king" side. The challengers start on the other side. How it works: The challengers start each point with a feed. If the kings win, they stay and score a point. If the challengers win, they move to the king side. With six players, the losing challengers swap out for the waiting pair. With five, the losing challenger who has played longest sits out and the waiting player steps in. Goal: First pair to seven points on the king side. Progression: The kings must win two points in a row to score. Padel drills for 3 players Three players is a great number for drilling: one feeder works two hitters, or two players gang up on one. Rotate every five to ten minutes so everyone gets every role. 9. Two against one at the net Players: 3 · Time: 12 minutes Setup: A pair at the net on one side; the single player at the back on the other, covering only one half of the court (cross-court). How it works: The single player feeds and then defends in their half, using lobs, low balls and the glass. The pair can only hit into that half. Goal: The single player scores one point for every five balls kept in play, and three for winning a rally. Rotate after ten feeds. Progression: The single player may only lob, which turns it into overhead practice for the pair. 10. Feeder and pair Players: 3 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: A feeder with a basket on one side; a pair on the other. How it works: The feeder plays three-ball sequences: a low ball to the feet, a ball down the middle, then a lob. The pair volleys, calls the middle and plays a bandeja, then resets. Goal: Count sequences where all three balls go back in. Eight out of ten is a strong score. Progression: After the third ball, the feeder plays the point out against the pair using one half of the court. Balancing mixed levels In a four with uneven levels, mix the pairs; if one pair is still clearly weaker, give it a head start (for example 15–0 every game). Everyone stays competitive. A 90-minute four-player plan Minutes Activity 0–15 Warm-up: 5 minutes of mini-rallies, then volley war (10) 15–40 Defend the net, then lob and take the net (12 + 12, plus changeover) 40–60 Middle madness, then rope pairs (10 + 10) 60–75 Yellow card, red card 75–90 Normal match, rotating partners For fewer players, see our padel drills for 2 players . For tactical ideas to bring into these drills, read the padel doubles strategy guide. Frequently asked questions What are good padel drills for 4 players? Drills that use conditioned points: defend the net, lob and take the net, and yellow card, red card. They feel like a match but train one habit at a time. What padel drills work for 3 players? Two-against-one drills and feeder drills. One player defends a half-court against a pair at the net, or a feeder plays set sequences to two hitters. Rotate roles regularly. How do you keep four players busy in drills? Choose drills where all four hit the ball, like volley war or conditioned points. Avoid single-feeder drills with a queue when you have four. Should we drill or play matches? Both. A good split is two-thirds drills, one-third match, with the match at the end so you can test what you practised. 
##### solo-padel-drills
Yes, there are plenty of padel drills to do alone: ball-control taps at home, wall rallies against any flat wall, back-glass rallies on an empty court and serve practice with a basket of balls. Here are eight solo drills, from the living room to the court, with targets to beat and progressions. Padel drills to do alone: what solo training can and can't do Training alone won't teach you to read an opponent or work with a partner. It is, though, the best way to get thousands of touches on the ball, groove your contact point and build fitness and footwork. Two short solo sessions a week add up to a lot of extra repetitions over a season. All you need is a racket, a few balls and, for most drills, a wall. A garage door, a sports-hall wall or the glass of an empty padel court all work. Check with the owner or club before you start banging balls against a wall. Padel drills at home 1. Face taps Players: 1 · Time: 5 minutes Setup: A racket, a ball and a bit of space indoors or outside. How it works: Tap the ball up into the air with one face of the racket, keeping it at about head height. Then switch to the other face, then alternate faces every touch. NOX suggests this as a home drill for getting comfortable with the whole hitting surface. Goal: 30 taps on one face, then 30 alternating, without the ball dropping. Progression: Add touches on the frame between face taps, or walk slowly around a room while tapping. 2. Split-step and shadow Players: 1 · Time: 6 minutes Setup: A space of about three metres square. A phone with an interval timer helps. How it works: Stand in a ready position with your racket up. Do a small split-step hop, then move two steps to a forehand volley shadow and recover. Repeat to the backhand, then backwards for an overhead shadow. Turn side-on for every shot. The Padel School puts the split step at the heart of good footwork; this drill grooves it without a ball. Goal: Six rounds of 30 seconds on, 30 seconds off, staying light on the balls of your feet. Progression: Use a footwork ladder or a chalk grid, with quick in-and-out steps before each shadow shot. Wall drills 3. Volley wall Players: 1 · Time: 8 minutes Setup: Stand about two and a half to three metres from a solid wall, the distance NOX recommends for this exercise. How it works: Volley the ball against the wall without letting it bounce, alternating forehand and backhand. Keep the swing short and the contact in front of your body. Goal: 20 volleys in a row, then 40. Progression: Move sideways along the wall as you volley, or aim at a taped square at net height. 4. Bounce rally Players: 1 · Time: 8 minutes Setup: Step further back from the wall, enough to let the ball bounce once before each shot. How it works: Hit forehands and backhands against the wall, letting the ball bounce once each time. Turn side-on, take the racket back early and finish towards the wall. Goal: Your longest unbroken rally. Note it and try to beat it next time. Progression: Alternate a firm drive with a softer shot so you practise changing pace. Solo drills on an empty court 5. Back-glass rally Players: 1 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Stand three to four metres in front of the back glass, facing it. How it works: Hit the ball into the back glass so it bounces on the way back to you, then hit it again. Alternate forehand and backhand. This builds racket control and rhythm; the progression below practises the real back-glass shape. Goal: 20 in a row, then 50, then 100. Progression: Add a side step between shots, or turn around and play the rebound properly: drop the ball in front of the glass, let it come out, and send it over the net. The Padel School's key point applies: step back, then step forward into the ball rather than hitting it behind you. 6. Corner rebound Players: 1 · Time: 8 minutes Setup: A basket of balls in a back corner. How it works: Throw a ball so it bounces and then hits the side glass and back glass. Read the rebound, move to it with small steps, and play it over the net cross-court. Goal: Seven out of ten balls returned over the net past the service line. Progression: Throw harder and lower so the ball comes out faster, or vary which wall it hits first. 7. Serve targets Players: 1 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Put two targets in each service box: one near the centre line and one near the side glass, both deep. How it works: Serve ten balls at each target, using a legal serve: ball bounced behind the service line in your own box, struck at or below waist height with at least one foot on the ground (FIP Rule 6). A serve that bounces in the box and then hits the mesh is a fault (Rule 7), so aim for the glass. Goal: Serves in out of 40, plus target hits. Record both. Progression: Call your target aloud before each serve and only count it if it goes where you said. 8. Self-fed overheads Players: 1 · Time: 8 minutes Setup: Stand around the service line with a basket. How it works: Toss the ball high in front of you, turn side-on, and play a controlled bandeja deep into the opposite court. Recover two steps towards the net after each one, as if your partner were beside you. Goal: Ten bandejas that land past the opponent's service line without hitting the back glass on the full. Progression: Toss the ball slightly behind you so you have to move back with side steps first. A 20-minute solo session Minutes Drill 0–3 Warm-up and split-step shadow 3–8 Back-glass rally (or bounce rally at home) 8–12 Serve targets 12–16 Volley wall 16–20 Self-fed overheads or face taps Keep a log Write down one number per drill: consecutive back-glass hits, serves in out of 40, longest volley chain. Solo training lives or dies by whether you can see progress. Solo padel training mistakes Standing too close to the wall. It forces a flick of the wrist. Give yourself room for a proper short swing. Hitting everything hard. The wall gives power back. Control is the point. Forgetting your feet. Small adjustment steps before every ball, even when alone. Same drill every time. Rotate drills so you cover volleys, the glass and the serve each week. When you have company again, use our padel drills for 2 players . If you're just starting, the beginner drills are the place to begin. Frequently asked questions Can you practise padel alone? Yes. Wall drills, back-glass rallies, serve practice and footwork work well alone. You can't practise tactics or teamwork solo, but you can build technique and fitness. What padel drills can I do at home? Face taps, split-step and shadow footwork, and wall volleys if you have a suitable outside wall such as a garage. NOX also suggests a bounce rally if you have a patio or garage with more space. Can I hit against the glass on an empty padel court? Usually, if you've booked the court. It's the best place for solo training, because the glass behaves exactly as it does in a match. Check the club's rules first. How often should I train alone? Two short sessions of 15–20 minutes a week on top of your normal games is a realistic, useful amount for most players. Do I need special equipment for solo padel drills? No. A racket, a handful of balls and a wall are enough. A ball basket and a few cones or towels for targets make court sessions easier. 
##### padel-drills-for-intermediate-players
The most useful padel drills for intermediate players train decisions as well as shots: when to come to the net, how to keep it with the bandeja, and how to defend off two walls. These eight drills add movement, targets and pressure to the basics, so your technique holds up in real matches. What padel drills for intermediate players should train At intermediate level you can rally, volley and use the back glass. What usually holds you back is what happens between the shots: drifting into no man's land, coming in behind a weak lob, or hitting a bandeja and then standing still to watch it. The Padel School's intermediate match analysis picks out exactly these errors. So these drills share three features: Movement after every shot. You don't score unless you recover. Targets. Depth and direction count, not just getting the ball back. Decisions. Many drills give you two options and score you on picking the right one. Net and transition drills 1. Lob, read, go Players: 2–4 · Time: 12 minutes Setup: The drilling pair at the back (with two players, the single back player makes the decision). A feeder (or the other pair) at the net. How it works: The net side feeds a low ball. The back player lobs. If the lob clears the net players and pushes them back, the back pair moves forward together, past the service line and into net position. If the lob is short, they stay back and defend. The Padel School puts it simply: when your lob is not good, don't come to the net. Goal: Score one point for a good lob followed by moving up, one for a weak lob followed by staying back. Lose a point for the wrong decision. Progression: Play the point out after the decision. 2. Bandeja chain Players: 2 · Time: 15 minutes Setup: The hitter at the net, the feeder at the back with a basket. Place a towel about two metres in front of the opposite back glass. How it works: The feeder lobs five balls in a row. After each bandeja , the hitter must move forward again into net position before the next lob arrives. Intermediate players often need several bandejas in a row before they can finish a point, so this trains patience. Goal: Count bandejas that land between the towel and the glass. Ten out of twenty is a good score. Progression: Mix in short lobs. The hitter must smash those and play a bandeja on the deep ones. Smash the easy balls only. 3. Feet, deep, finish Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: The volleyer at the net, the feeder at the back. How it works: The feeder plays three balls in sequence: a low ball to the feet, a medium ball at body height and a high, easy ball. The volleyer plays the first back deep and low, the second into a corner, and puts the third away. Goal: A full sequence counts only if all three volleys hit their zone. Aim for six out of ten. Progression: The feeder randomises the order so the volleyer has to read each ball. Defence drills 4. Double-glass defence Players: 2 · Time: 12 minutes Setup: The defender in a back corner. The feeder at the net on the other side. How it works: The feeder sends balls into the corner so they come off the back and side glass, starting at a gentle pace and building up. The defender reads the rebound, takes small steps, and replies with a lob or a low cross-court ball. The LTA's back-glass drill also moves into the corner so the ball comes off two walls. Goal: Seven out of ten replies that are either a deep lob or a ball landing before the feeder's service line. Progression: Alternate corners so the defender also practises the backhand double-glass exit. Our back glass guide covers the basics. 5. Smash defence Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: The defender at the back, the feeder at the net hitting controlled overheads. How it works: The feeder hits medium-paced overheads deep into the court. The defender lets the ball come off the back glass and replies with a lob. Coaches stress that the ball is already travelling towards the net after the rebound, so you need little extra power: focus on control. Goal: Count lobs that land beyond the feeder's service line. Swap after ten balls. Progression: Increase the pace of the overheads gradually. Precision drills 6. Low and deep Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Mark two target zones with towels or cones in the opposite court: one deep in each back corner, about two metres from the glass. HEAD's precision drills use markers on the court floor in the same way. The feeder stands at the back of the opposite court, as opponents who have been pushed back would. How it works: This is a drive control drill for when both opponents are back. The feeder sends balls to the hitter's forehand side; the hitter plays flat forehands that skim the net and land in a target zone, pinning the opponents to the glass so your pair can move forward. A drive that floats high sits up for them, so the ball must cross the net low. Goal: Six out of ten that cross low and land in a target zone. Progression: Do the same with slice backhands, then mix forehand and backhand. 7. Half-court error game Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: A narrowed court: half the court, with the side lines moved in a metre using cones. How it works: A smaller target makes errors more costly, which is the point. HEAD recommends a smaller court for cutting out unforced errors. The first bounce must land inside the cones; after that the walls play as normal. Goal: Our own scoring: an unforced error costs two points. First to eleven. Progression: Add the yellow card, red card rule from the LTA: the first mistake in a game is a yellow card, and a second mistake in the same game is a red card that loses it. Pressure game 8. Three-ball net challenge Players: 4 · Time: 15 minutes Setup: One pair at the net, one pair at the back. How it works: The back pair gets three feeds each point: a low ball, a lob and then a live point. The net pair must hold the net through all three. If the back pair wins the net at any stage, they swap ends. Goal: The net pair scores a point for each three-ball sequence they survive at the net. First to seven. Progression: The net pair may not smash. Only bandejas and víboras , to train control over power. A weekly intermediate plan Session Theme Drills 1 Keeping the net Bandeja chain, feet deep finish, three-ball net challenge 2 Defence and transition Double-glass defence, smash defence, lob read go 3 Consistency Low and deep, half-court error game, then a match Footwork underpins everything Split-step every time your opponent hits, and set your feet before you swing. The Padel School's line sums it up: arrive early, set your feet, then hit. For more on decisions, read our padel tactics for intermediate players . If some of these drills feel like a stretch, start with the beginner drills . Frequently asked questions What should intermediate padel players practise most? The bandeja, the transition to the net, and defence off the glass. These decide most intermediate points, much more than power. How do I stop getting stuck in no man's land? Commit. After a good lob, move all the way to the net; after a weak one, stay back. The lob, read, go drill trains exactly this decision. How often should intermediate players drill? Once a week alongside your matches is a good start. Even 30 minutes of focused drills makes your matches more productive. Should intermediate players learn the víbora? Yes, once the bandeja is reliable. Use the bandeja chain first, then add víboras on medium lobs. 
##### padel-drills-for-kids
The best padel drills for kids are short, active games where children hit lots of balls and keep score in simple ways. Below are eight fun padel games and exercises for young players, plus the safety basics every parent and coach should know before the first session. Safety first The US Padel Association's guide to junior programmes sets the order of priorities as be safe, have fun, learn. That order is a good rule for any session with children. Right-sized racket. HEAD's guidance is roughly 300 g for under-10s and about 330 g for over-10s, with most children moving to an adult racket around 12 or 13. A racket that is too big or heavy makes injury more likely. See our best padel rackets for kids . Wrist cord on. The FIP rules make the racket's safety cord mandatory, worn on the wrist. It stops a racket flying out of a small hand. Eye protection. In a short case report of three padel eye injuries that needed surgery, ophthalmologists at the University of Gothenburg called padel a potentially high-risk sport for eye injuries and recommended goggles. Sports goggles are worth considering for children too, especially in net games. No hitting at people. The FIP etiquette rules ban hitting the ball at opponents when it is not in play. In kids' drills, ban smashing at anyone, full stop. Space and supervision. One group per half-court, balls cleared from the floor, no running on a wet court, and an adult on court. How to run padel drills for kids Keep everyone hitting. The USPA guide warns against line drills where children queue for one turn. Pair players up instead. Use easier balls for young beginners. The USPA suggests foam or low-compression balls for school clinics; slower balls keep rallies going. Allow two bounces for younger beginners. More contact means more fun and faster learning. Short sessions. Around 60 minutes suits most juniors; 90 minutes is usually too long for beginners and for under-12s. Simple scoring. Count rallies or play first to five before introducing proper padel scoring. Warm-up games 1. Racket balance relay Players: 4–8 · Time: 5 minutes Setup: Two teams at the back glass, a cone at the net for each team. How it works: Each child walks (or jogs) to the cone and back balancing a ball on their racket, then hands the ball to the next teammate. Goal: First team to finish wins. A dropped ball means stopping and starting again from that spot. Progression: Bounce the ball on the racket face while walking, then alternate faces. 2. Bounce count Players: Any number · Time: 5 minutes Setup: Each child with a racket and ball, spread out safely. How it works: Bounce the ball up off the racket as many times as possible. Then down onto the floor. Then alternate up and down. Goal: A personal best. Everyone counts out loud. Progression: Use the other face of the racket, or do it while moving sideways. Rally games 3. Rally ladder Players: 2 per half-court · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Pairs on half-courts, standing near the service line on opposite sides of the net. How it works: Rally gently. First target two shots, then three, then four. Two bounces allowed for younger children. The USPA recommends three to five-shot mini-rallies as the basic unit for juniors. Goal: Reach a rally of five, then ten. Change partners after each ladder. Progression: One bounce only, then one player volleys while the other lets the ball bounce. 4. Target zones Players: 1–3 per feeder · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Hoops, towels or cones in the opposite court. Small targets score more than large ones. With more than three children, add a second feeder on the other half-court. How it works: A coach or parent feeds an easy ball; the child plays it towards a target. Then it's the next player's turn. No more than two children should ever be waiting. Goal: One point for landing in the court, three for hitting a target. Progression: Call the target before the feed, or let children feed each other. 5. Wall bounce challenge Players: 2 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Both children on the same side. The thrower stands near the service line, the hitter a couple of metres in front of the back glass. How it works: One child gently throws the ball so it bounces and then hits the back glass. The other lets it come off the glass and taps it back to the thrower. Keep it exploratory: the USPA suggests that wall play for beginners should be playful, not technical. Goal: Five good returns, then swap. Progression: Return the ball over the net instead of to the thrower. Older children can read our back glass guide . Net and serve games 6. Volley catch Players: 2 · Time: 8 minutes Setup: One child at the net with a racket, a partner on the other side throwing underarm. How it works: The thrower sends soft balls at chest height. The volleyer blocks the ball back so the thrower can catch it. Short racket movement, no big swing. Goal: Ten catches in a row, then swap. Progression: Throw to alternate sides so the volleyer uses forehand and backhand. 7. Serve skittles Players: 2–4 · Time: 10 minutes Setup: Plastic cones or empty plastic bottles (never glass) standing in the diagonal service box. How it works: Children serve underarm, bouncing the ball first and hitting it at or below waist height, as the rules require. Each knocked-over cone is a point. Goal: The most cones knocked over in ten serves. Progression: Serve from the other side of the court too, or move the cones deeper. Team game 8. Crocodile and fish Players: 3–6 fish plus a crocodile · Time: 10 minutes Setup: The "fish" line up at the back on one side. The "crocodile" (an older child or adult) stands at the net on the other side. How it works: Each fish plays a rally against the crocodile, who hits only soft balls. A fish who wins three shots swims past and joins the crocodile's side; a fish who misses goes back in the queue. With more than three fish, run two crocodiles on two half-courts, so no more than two fish are ever waiting. Goal: Get every fish across. Progression: The crocodile may play gentle lobs too. For ages 10 to 14 Older children can move towards adult-style drills: ladder rallies with one bounce, cross-court rallies and simple doubles points. Start them on the beginner drills and keep sessions playful. Frequently asked questions What age can children start padel? HEAD says children can start once they are strong enough to hold a racket, typically from about age three. The USPA's junior guide suggests starting with an under-12 group (ages 7–12), adding an under-8 group (ages 5–8) as a programme grows. Are padel drills safe for kids? Yes, with the basics in place: a racket the right size, the wrist cord on, supervision, no hitting at people and, ideally, eye protection. What balls should kids use? Softer, slower balls such as foam or low-compression balls help young beginners keep rallies going. Older children can use normal padel balls. How long should a kids' padel session be? About an hour. Shorter for very young children, and keep every activity to ten minutes or less. What are fun padel games for kids? Rally ladders, target zones, serve skittles and team games like crocodile and fish. Anything with a score and lots of hitting works.
